Backtested on real BTC/ETH history (BTC +51.6%, ETH +55.3%, Sharpe 8-9, no liquidation events at conservative leverage). **Both BTC and ETH now paper trading live** (ETH added 2026-08-02) via the same `launchd` job (`com.quantplatform.fundingarbpapertrade`, checks in every 8 hours) against real Bitnomial (Kraken's regulated US venue) funding data.

## Storage footprint (checked 2026-07-30)

- `market.duckdb` size trend from the daily off-computer backups (`~/Library/Mobile Documents/com~apple~CloudDocs/quant-platform-backups/`): 2.34GB (07-24) → 2.62GB (07-25) → 2.46GB (07-26) → 2.46GB (07-27) → 2.77GB (07-28) → 2.79GB (07-29) → 3.71GB (07-30). Steady-state growth is modest (~50-200MB/day); the 07-29→07-30 jump (+0.98GB) lines up with the ticker-universe expansion (stocks/crypto/futures), a one-time step change rather than the ongoing rate.
- Mac's disk was found at 98% full (359Mi free) on 2026-07-30, but the actual cause was a Chrome disk-cache bug (a 111GB `sqldb2` cache file, logically near-empty per SQLite's own page count — a known issue with Chrome's newer SQL-backed cache backend), not platform data growth. Deleting it recovered 106Gi. Real platform data (`Trading/` dir) is only ~4.7G total.
- **Conclusion: no SSD upgrade needed right now.** At the current DB growth rate, an upgrade isn't warranted for a long time — revisit only if the universe keeps expanding aggressively (more tickers/asset classes) or market data storage strategy changes (e.g. tick-level or higher-frequency data).

## Quick commands

```bash
cd quant-platform && source .venv/bin/activate

python scripts/pull_data.py SPY QQQ --start 2015-01-01     # pull OHLCV
python scripts/run_backtest.py SPY --strategy sma           # backtest + walk-forward, logs to DuckDB
python scripts/run_backtest.py SPY --strategy bollinger
python scripts/show_history.py                              # terminal view of logged runs
python scripts/build_report.py && open reports/latest_report.html   # HTML report w/ equity curves
python scripts/paper_trade.py QQQ --strategy sma            # daily paper-trading check (manual, once/day)
python scripts/paper_trade.py QQQ --strategy breakout       # ditto
python scripts/paper_trade.py XLP --strategy bollinger      # ditto — all 3 current PROMOTEd combos
python scripts/research_cycle.py                            # full research cycle, widened grids, ~15s
python scripts/blend_backtest.py                             # phase-1 blend simulator: equal-weight the 3 current combos + SPY comparison, prints only
python scripts/blend_paper_trading.py                        # shared blended paper account view (unlevered), prints only — see item 26

python scripts/funding_arb_backfill.py                       # pull BTC/ETH funding-rate + hourly-kline history (Binance archive + Bitnomial)
python scripts/funding_arb_backtest.py                       # full backtest, both assets, logs to DuckDB — see item 93
python scripts/paper_trade_funding_arb.py                    # manual paper-trade check/update (also runs automatically via launchd, see item 93)
```

## What's built (in build order)

1. **Data layer** — `data/fetch.py`: yfinance → DuckDB, one table (`ohlcv_daily`), daily bars only, equities/ETFs only. Free source, no auth needed.
2. **Strategy interface** — `strategies/base.py`: plug-in `Strategy` ABC (`generate_signals(df) -> (entries, exits)`). Two concrete strategies, deliberately structural opposites to pressure-test the interface: `sma_crossover.py` (trend-following) and `mean_reversion.py` (Bollinger, contrarian). Both held up without needing interface changes.
3. **Backtest engine** — `backtest/run.py`: vectorbt `Portfolio.from_signals`, fees + slippage applied inside the backtest (not bolted on after).
4. **Walk-forward validation** — `validation/walk_forward.py`: splits data into sequential train/test windows, picks best param-grid entry on train, scores only on held-out test. Never trust a single full-history backtest.
5. **Deflated Sharpe Ratio** — `validation/deflated_sharpe.py`: corrects the train-side param search for multiple-testing bias (Bailey & López de Prado). Stdlib-only (`statistics.NormalDist`, no scipy dependency). Currently the grid is only 3 params/strategy, so the correction is mild — matters more once the grid (or a real Evolution Agent) grows.
6. **Results store** — `store/results.py`: DuckDB tables `backtest_runs`, `walk_forward_windows`, `backtest_equity_curves`. Append-only log, never overwritten. Schema grows via migration-safe `ALTER TABLE` (`_ensure_columns` helper) so old rows survive schema changes — this will keep happening over the project's life.
7. **Risk Manager** — `risk/`: `position_sizing.py` (volatility-target sizing, replaces vectorbt's implicit "bet 100% of equity" default — this alone measurably reduced realized drawdowns) and `limits.py` (hard max-drawdown gate, checked against both full-history and every walk-forward window, printed as PASS/FAIL and logged). Confirmed it actually fires: SPY/bollinger fails the gate (-25.2% dd vs -25.0% limit).
8. **HTML report** — `scripts/build_report.py`: self-contained (Plotly inlined, works offline) report built purely from what's in DuckDB — never re-runs a backtest, so it's a view of the actual record. Equity curves + full-history table + walk-forward rollup.
9. **Promotion decision** — `promotion.py`: combines the risk gate and DSR into one explicit PROMOTE/HOLD verdict per ticker/strategy combo (full-history risk pass + no OOS risk failures + every OOS window profitable + mean DSR ≥ 0.5), with a reasons list for any HOLD. Headline section of the HTML report. As of 2026-07-21, QQQ/sma is the only combo that clears all four gates.

Building this caught a real bug worth remembering: `backtest_runs.strategy_name` and `walk_forward_windows.strategy_name` used two different naming conventions (full parameterized instance name vs. short family name), so the tables could never be joined. Fixed by standardizing on the short family name in both; the specific params still live in the `params` JSON column. If cross-table joins on `strategy_name` ever look empty/wrong again, check this didn't regress.

10. **Paper trading** — `paper_trading.py` + `store/paper.py` + `scripts/paper_trade.py`: the first step into the vision's Paper Trade pipeline stage. No real money, no live orders. State is an append-only `paper_trade_events` log (entry/exit only, never UPDATEd) — "is a position open" and "current paper equity" are both derived by replaying the log (`paper_equity_summary`), not tracked as mutable state. Meant to be run once per trading day, after market close. Warns (doesn't block) if you paper-trade a combo that isn't currently `PROMOTE`d.

11. **Expanded ticker universe (2026-07-21)** — pulled 12 more diverse, liquid ETFs (IWM, DIA, XLF, XLE, XLK, XLV, TLT, IEF, GLD, USO, EFA, EEM — small-cap, sectors, bonds, commodities, international) and ran both strategies against all of them (28 combos total across 14 tickers). No new code, purely exercising the existing pipeline more broadly. **QQQ/sma is still the only combo that clears promotion.** Closest near-miss: **XLK/sma** fails only the full-history risk gate (a drawdown breach somewhere in 2015–2026) despite every walk-forward OOS window being profitable and DSR well above threshold.

12. **XLK/sma drawdown investigated, root-caused, not a bug** — the breach is a single event: -25.94% drawdown, peak 2020-02-19 to valley 2020-05-14 (the COVID crash), fully recovered by 2021-11-04; every other historical drawdown for this combo tops out around -17%. Confirmed the strategy was already holding a position through the crash (no new signal fired) and that volatility-target sizing *did* de-risk progressively (1.0x → 0.14x from mid-Jan to mid-March) but necessarily lagged the shock's onset, since a trailing-vol estimate can't front-run a sudden regime change. The risk gate correctly declined to promote a strategy with a genuine ~26% historical drawdown — this is the gate working as designed, not a false negative.

13. **Stop-loss and shorter vol-lookback tested as potential fixes, both rejected** — added `sl_stop` as an opt-in parameter to `run_backtest()`/`walk_forward()` (default `None`, no behavior change to anything already stored) and tested both ideas properly through the *actual* walk-forward + DSR + risk-gate + promotion pipeline across all 28 combos, not just a quick full-history metric sweep. Shorter lookback (5/10/15-day) made drawdowns worse on average across the universe and specifically worse for XLK (-29.5% vs -25.9%) — noisier vol estimates, rejected. A 10% stop-loss looked broadly positive in a naive full-history-only sweep, but under the real pipeline it **flips QQQ/sma from PROMOTE to HOLD** (its drawdown gets worse, -24.6% → -27.1%, a classic whipsaw: forced exit during a dip, signal doesn't change, re-entry lands worse) while unlocking zero new promotions anywhere. Rejected as a default — pure regression, no upside. The `sl_stop` capability stays in the engine (harmless when unused) for future use. This is the clearest demonstration so far of why the full validation pipeline exists instead of trusting an aggregate backtest metric: the quick sweep and the properly-validated result reached opposite conclusions.

14. **Third strategy: Donchian channel breakout** (`strategies/breakout.py`) — enters on an N-day high breakout, exits on an M-day low breakdown (default 20/10). First strategy to actually use the `high`/`low` columns instead of just `close`; the interface held up without changes. Ran across all 14 tickers immediately. Result: **promoted combos went from 1 to 3** — `QQQ/breakout` and `XLK/breakout` are new, `QQQ/sma` unchanged. Notably **XLK/breakout clears all four gates where XLK/sma failed** (see item 12) — different signal mechanics avoided that strategy's COVID-era drawdown breach on the same ticker.

    Checked return correlation among the 3 promoted combos before calling this an unambiguous win: `QQQ/breakout` vs `XLK/breakout` = **0.82** (nearly the same bet — both are tech-momentum exposure, unsurprising given QQQ/XLK composition overlap); `QQQ/sma` vs the other two ≈ 0.52-0.53 (more genuinely diversifying, different signal mechanism). So "3 promoted" is really closer to **2 distinct bets** — a tech-momentum cluster plus one moderately-different trend signal — not 3 independent edges. This is precisely the "correlation as a promotion gate" idea already logged in [[Enhancement Ideas]]; it hasn't been built yet, but this is the first time there's actually more than one promoted strategy to apply it to.

15. **Correlation-aware promotion gate built** (`promotion.py`) — the four-gate check alone now only determines `individual_verdict`. A second pass greedily walks individually-promotable combos in descending mean-DSR order and downgrades any candidate whose full-history return correlation with an already-selected (higher-DSR) combo is ≥0.7 (`DEFAULT_CORRELATION_THRESHOLD`) to `HOLD`, with an explicit reason (e.g. `"correlated 0.82 with QQQ/breakout (already selected, higher DSR)"`). `verdict` is now always the final, correlation-adjusted answer — `report.py` and `paper_trade.py` needed zero changes since they already just read `verdict`. Verified against real data: `XLK/breakout` (the correlated, lower-DSR duplicate) correctly drops to HOLD; `QQQ/breakout` (higher DSR) and `QQQ/sma` (genuinely diversifying) both remain PROMOTE. **Current final promotion set: QQQ/sma, QQQ/breakout.**

16. **QQQ/breakout added to paper trading (2026-07-21)** — both current final-verdict PROMOTE combos (QQQ/sma, QQQ/breakout) are now tracked in `paper_trade_events`. No warning printed (correctly, since it's a final PROMOTE, not just individually promotable). Flat/HOLD on first check — no breakout signal fired that day.

17. **Automatic daily scheduling via launchd (2026-07-21)** — user explicitly asked for this after seeing the concrete failure mode: manual-only paper trading silently misses single-day-pulse signals (e.g. SMA crossover) if nobody remembers to run it that exact day. `scripts/daily_paper_trade.sh` runs both tracked combos and logs to `logs/paper_trade_<date>.log`; `scheduling/com.quantplatform.papertrade.plist` is the launchd job (source of truth in-repo, installed copy in `~/Library/LaunchAgents/`), scheduled for 17:00 local (after NYSE close). Installed, loaded, and verified via an actual `launchctl start` trigger (not just running the script directly) — exit code 0, both combos logged correctly.

    **Important caveat, told to the user directly**: this machine is the MacBook (dev workstation), not the Mac mini the vision designates as the always-on server (see note at top of this doc). If the lid is closed or the Mac is asleep at 17:00, launchd silently skips that day — no error, no retry, no catch-up. Combined with item 10's single-day-pulse risk, this makes the schedule a real improvement over pure-manual but still not fully reliable while running on laptop hardware that sleeps. Revisit once/if this moves to always-on hardware.

18. **Research/Evolution loop v1 (2026-07-21)** — user asked for "an agent that is constantly researching, backtesting, coming up with, and dumping old strategies." Scoped deliberately: this searches a *wider parameter space within the 3 existing strategy families*, not novel strategy invention (that's LLM-driven strategy code generation — flagged as separate, much bigger, riskier future work). Entered plan mode, verified the actual current code before designing (not memory), got the plan approved before writing anything.
    - Widened each family's `grid` in `registry.py` from 3 to 8 combos. `default` (the full-history risk-gate's param set) stays static and human-edited only — **deliberately not** derived from what the wider search prefers, since `check_risk_limits()` has zero multiple-testing correction (unlike DSR's Sharpe correction), and letting a search pick "the" params would reintroduce the exact selection bias DSR exists to guard against, applied to the one criterion with no defense against it. This was the single biggest design decision in this pass — see `registry.py`'s module docstring.
    - New `promotion_snapshots` table (`store/results.py`, append-only like everything else) — the first time promotion *history* is tracked anywhere in this codebase; previously `evaluate_promotion()` only ever saw the latest run per combo. `cycle_id` (nullable) added to `backtest_runs`/`walk_forward_windows` via the existing migration pattern to link runs to a research cycle.
    - `promotion.py::evaluate_promotion()` gained `keep_run_id: bool = False` (both existing callers unaffected) and now aggregates `walk_forward_run_id` too.
    - New `scripts/research_cycle.py`: re-runs backtest + walk-forward across the universe (or a `--tickers`/`--strategies` subset), snapshots verdicts, diffs against the prior snapshot, flags loudly if a demoted combo is currently in `daily_paper_trade.sh`. Structurally cannot touch paper-trading state (doesn't import `paper_trading.py`/`store/paper.py` at all). No automatic scheduling yet — same manual-first rollout as `paper_trade.py`.
    - **Verified thoroughly before trusting it**: QQQ-only smoke test, inspected `promotion_snapshots` directly, ran a second identical cycle and confirmed it correctly reported "same" for all 42 combos (no false positives), confirmed `paper_trade.py`/`build_report.py` behavior unchanged, then ran the full 42-combo cycle (13.8s total — much faster than expected).
    - **Caught a real, instructive quirk in the process**: partial-scope runs (`--tickers QQQ`) compare freshly-recomputed combos against stale ones for everything else, which briefly flipped `QQQ/breakout`'s correlation-gate outcome against `XLK/breakout` in a misleading way (apples vs. oranges — one side had a wider-grid DSR correction applied, the other didn't yet). Resolved itself correctly once the full universe was re-run consistently. Documented in the README as an explicit caveat, not silently papered over.
    - **Real result from the widened search**: promotion set was **QQQ/sma, QQQ/breakout, XLF/sma** (XLF/sma new). Checked correlation before trusting it — XLF/sma was only 0.19–0.50 correlated with the other two, genuinely diversifying. But before adding it to paper trading, the user (correctly, from memory of the earlier narrow-grid run) questioned whether XLF/sma was really profitable — see item 19, which found a real bug this surfaced and reverted the verdict.

19. **Promotion-criteria bug found and fixed: `sufficient_oos_evidence` gate added (2026-07-21)** — the user recalled XLF/sma testing "extremely unprofitable" earlier and asked why the widened-grid search suddenly promoted it. Pulled the actual DuckDB history to check instead of trusting either memory:
    - **Original narrow (3-param) grid**: walk-forward window 2 genuinely lost -12.9% (Sharpe -2.04, drawdown -14.0%) — a real bad OOS result, correctly blocking promotion (`all_oos_windows_positive` failed).
    - **Widened (8-param) grid**: the train-side search picked a much slower `fast=75/slow=250` pair for windows 0 and 1 — a pair whose crossover simply never fires in either test window (0 trades, 0% return, undefined/`inf` Sharpe). Only window 2 (the genuinely bad one) got a real fix (a faster `5/20` pair, +2.8% instead of -12.9%). So half the "evidence" behind the PROMOTE flip was actually *no evidence at all*, not a real improvement.
    - **Root cause**: `evaluate_promotion()`'s `worst_oos_sharpe` calculation already excluded non-finite (0-trade) windows from the "worst" check — correct — but `mean_dsr` did not, silently averaging in a 0-trade window's *train-side* DSR (which reflects nothing about what happened out-of-sample), and there was no check at all for *how many* windows had actually traded. A wide enough grid could otherwise "promote" a strategy by handing it params that just sit out the windows it would have lost.
    - **Fix** (`promotion.py`): new fifth criterion, `sufficient_oos_evidence` — requires strictly more than half of a combo's OOS windows to have a finite test Sharpe (i.e. actually traded). Added a `_summarize_oos_windows()` helper so `worst_oos_sharpe` and `mean_dsr` are both computed only over evaluable (traded) windows, consistently — previously only one of the two was filtered. `evaluate_criteria()` and its docstring updated from "four criteria" to "five."
    - **Verified**: re-ran `evaluate_promotion()` directly — XLF/sma now correctly reverts to HOLD (`sufficient_oos_evidence` fails: 2 of 4 windows evaluable) with QQQ/sma and QQQ/breakout unaffected. Confirmed `build_report.py` and `paper_trade.py` still run cleanly against the new output columns. Re-ran the full 42-combo `research_cycle.py` — diff correctly reports exactly one change (`XLF/sma: PROMOTE -> HOLD`), all 41 other combos unchanged, confirming the fix is scoped and doesn't have unintended side effects elsewhere in the universe.
    - **Why this matters beyond XLF/sma**: this was a real, previously-undetected gap in the platform's core validation logic — not a strategy-specific edge case. It was only caught because the user cross-checked a promotion flip against their own memory of an earlier result instead of taking a widened search's output at face value. Reinforces the same lesson as the stop-loss investigation (item 13): a pipeline result is only as trustworthy as its weakest unverified assumption, and questioning a surprising flip is exactly the right instinct.

20. **Ticker universe expanded to search for genuinely uncorrelated bets, not just more equity beta (2026-07-21)** — with promotion criteria trustworthy again (item 19), the next lever pulled was diversification itself: with only 2 promoted combos at ~0.53 correlation, portfolio Sharpe has little room to grow (Grinold's Law, [[Enhancement Ideas]]). Pulled 9 new tickers deliberately chosen for exposure the existing 14-ticker universe didn't have, not just "more ETFs": defensive/rate-sensitive sectors (`XLU`, `XLP`, `XLRE`), previously-uncovered cyclicals (`XLI`, `XLB`, `XLC`), factor ETFs (`MTUM` momentum, `USMV` min-vol), and a genuinely different asset class (`UUP`, US dollar currency exposure). Universe is now 23 tickers × 3 strategies = 69 combos.
    - **Result: one new promotion, `XLP/bollinger`** — the first-ever Bollinger (mean-reversion) combo to clear the bar, on Consumer Staples (a defensive sector, not previously represented). Full-history: +85.2% return, Sharpe 0.95, -11.0% max drawdown. 3 of 4 OOS windows evaluable (correctly passes the new `sufficient_oos_evidence` gate on real evidence, not a technicality).
    - **Correlation checked before trusting it**: 0.24 with `QQQ/sma`, 0.04 with `QQQ/breakout` — essentially uncorrelated, and it makes structural sense (different strategy mechanism *and* different, defensive sector). This is a materially better diversification result than `XLF/sma` was.
    - **Honest caveat surfaced and not glossed over**: the worst evaluable OOS window has Sharpe **0.014** — barely positive, thin margin, low trade counts per window (1, 5, 2 trades). Clears every gate, but not a comfortable margin the way `QQQ/sma`/`QQQ/breakout` are.
    - **Decision**: user chose to hold off adding `XLP/bollinger` to paper trading until it survives another research cycle, rather than trusting a single cycle's result on a thin-margin combo — same "propose, don't auto-act" instinct as `XLF/sma` before it, now backed by good reason given how close this one is to the line.

21. **XLP/bollinger re-checked a day later (2026-07-22)** — pulled one more day of price history and re-ran `research_cycle.py` to see if `XLP/bollinger` survived a second look. It did: identical numbers (worst OOS Sharpe 0.014, mean DSR 85.8%, correlation 0.04–0.24 with the other two), all 69 combos reported "same," nothing regressed. Worth being precise about what this does and doesn't prove: the walk-forward windows are multi-year chunks of history, so one extra day of price data doesn't shift where any of the 4 windows fall — this confirms the result isn't a computational fluke, but it isn't a genuinely new out-of-sample test either. A re-run next week would likely show the same thing for the same reason.

22. **XLP/bollinger added to paper trading (2026-07-22)** — the "wait for another cycle" reasoning was reconsidered: paper trading is free, fake-money, and reversible, so the caution that applies to trusting something with real capital doesn't really apply here. More importantly, waiting on `research_cycle.py` re-runs isn't an efficient way to gather fresh evidence anyway (per item 21, they don't produce new out-of-sample data on a daily cadence) — starting paper trading immediately is a faster way to accumulate genuine forward-looking confirmation, one real day at a time. Ran `paper_trade.py XLP --strategy bollinger` once (HOLD, flat, no signal), added it to `daily_paper_trade.sh` (now covers all 3 final-verdict PROMOTE combos) and to `research_cycle.py`'s `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` constant (kept in sync by hand, per its own comment). Verified the full script runs clean end to end (exit code 0 for all 3 combos). Its thin OOS margin (item 20) means its paper-trading results are worth watching more skeptically than `QQQ/sma`/`QQQ/breakout` — not a reason to exclude it, a reason to read its results with more scrutiny.

23. **Published a results dashboard artifact (2026-07-21/22, outside the codebase)** — built a standalone, shareable HTML dashboard summarizing a research cycle: pass/fail for all 69 combos in plain English (translated criteria names like `sufficient_oos_evidence` into everyday sentences), the 3 winners as cards, a breakdown of why the other 66 were rejected, trade-frequency-by-strategy-type stats, and a simulated-$10,000 equity-curve chart for the 3 winners with an S&P 500 (SPY) buy-and-hold line added for comparison. Lives outside `quant-platform/` entirely (a Claude Artifact, not a repo file) — **URL: `https://claude.ai/code/artifact/20d39a44-28d7-4c0a-a77b-e323d3c6ae6e`**. Kept updated in place (same URL) as new findings came in rather than creating new links each time; that's the pattern to follow if asked to refresh it again. Not automatically regenerated — a manual "update the artifact" request each time, same propose/human-decides pattern as everything else.

    **Honest number surfaced by the SPY comparison**: over 2015–2026, simple SPY buy-and-hold returned **+264.3%** (worst drop -34.1%) versus the three paper-traded strategies' +95.9%/+85.2%/+70.3% (worst drops -24.6%/-11.0%/-12.3%). The strategies all have shallower worst-case drawdowns than the index, but none of them beat its raw return. Documented plainly rather than glossed over — this became the seed of item 24's discussion.

24. **"Can this beat the S&P 500?" — discussed, not decided (2026-07-22)** — user asked directly whether the platform could become more profitable than SPY. Honest answer given: unlikely with more of the same strategy shapes (single-asset, once-daily, trend/mean-reversion/breakout strategies are structurally built to trade *fewer* of the market's up days in exchange for smaller drawdowns, not to out-earn a fully-invested index). Four real levers discussed, ordered roughly by how much new risk/complexity each adds:
    - **Leverage on already-validated signals** (e.g. trade a 2x ETF version, or use margin) — fastest to try, needs no new code (position sizing already exists), but amplifies drawdowns too and leveraged ETFs specifically carry volatility-decay risk in choppy markets. More risk on the same edge, not a smarter edge.
    - **Stack more genuinely uncorrelated strategies, then leverage the combined portfolio to a similar risk level as SPY** — the textbook answer (Grinold's Law again, [[Enhancement Ideas]]), and the more legitimate long-term path, but needs a portfolio-level backtest engine that doesn't exist yet (today's engine only ever tests one ticker in isolation). Substantial build, not a quick add.
    - **Meta-labeling** — size up on higher-confidence signals instead of flat-sizing every trade equally; already logged as an idea in [[Enhancement Ideas]], not yet built.
    - **New asset classes with different return shapes** (futures, options premium-selling, eventually crypto) — real diversification but real new risk types too; already flagged elsewhere as deferred, bigger-project work.
    - **Explicit caution raised**: optimizing specifically to "beat SPY over this historical window" is exactly the kind of target that invites curve-fitting a strategy to have looked good in hindsight — the opposite of what the walk-forward/DSR/risk-gate machinery exists to prevent. The goal stays "genuinely validated, resilient edges," not "beat one benchmark."
    - **No decision made** — conversation paused here (user going to sleep) to continue "tomorrow morning." Nothing above has been scoped or built yet.

25. **Phase-1 portfolio blend simulator built and verified (2026-07-22)** — the next morning, picked the diversify-then-leverage path from item 24 to scope first. Three research passes over the real code confirmed there's no portfolio-level backtest engine today (everything is keyed to a single `(ticker, strategy_name)` pair) and building the real thing — shared capital allocation, new schema, portfolio-level walk-forward/DSR semantics — is a substantial build, not a quick add. Rather than commit to that blind, built a cheap first-pass check instead: `scripts/blend_backtest.py` (new file, nothing else touched) reconstructs an approximate blended equity curve *purely from already-stored per-combo backtest results* — no new schema, no re-backtesting — because `backtest_equity_curves` already stores true dollar equity curves that all start at exactly $10,000, so they can be rescaled by weight and summed directly.
    - **Real result**: equal-weight blend of `QQQ/sma` + `QQQ/breakout` + `XLP/bollinger` — Sharpe **1.01**, beating every individual leg (0.67 / 0.73 / 0.95); max drawdown **-9.5%**, shallower than every individual leg (-24.6% / -12.3% / -11.0%). Total return 83.8%, still trailing SPY's 264.3% pre-leverage, as expected — closing that gap is exactly what a future leverage step would need to test, not something this script answers.
    - **Grinold's Law holds on real data**: stacking these three already-uncorrelated bets produced a smoother ride than any one alone, confirming the diversification thesis is worth the further investment rather than just a nice theory.
    - **Deliberately no `--weights` CLI flag** — letting weights be searched over rather than fixed a priori would reintroduce the exact selection-bias problem Deflated Sharpe Ratio exists to guard against (DSR's correction is wired to "which parameter set won a grid search," not "which weighting won"), just aimed at portfolio weights instead of strategy params. Equal-weight was chosen in advance, not selected by trying several and picking the best-looking one.
    - **Nothing persisted** — prints to stdout only, not wired into `promotion.py`, `paper_trading.py`, or any DB table. A blended row has no honest home in the current schema (no weight/leg-mapping column exists), and faking one in with a `ticker="PORTFOLIO"` placeholder would silently corrupt the correlation gate's assumption that every row is an independent bet.
    - **Verified, not just plausible**: recomputed `QQQ/sma`'s own stored stats from its raw equity curve and matched total_return_pct/max_drawdown_pct to full float precision (Sharpe matches to ~0.02% — the small gap is because there's no `vbt.Portfolio` object for a blended curve to call the exact method on, only a pandas reconstruction of vectorbt's own formula); independently reproduced SPY's already-known +264.3%/-34.1% from raw price data (got +264.25%/-34.10%); confirmed all three legs share identical fees/slippage before blending them.
    - **Phase 2, not started**: if a leverage sanity-check (vol-targeting the blended curve toward SPY's realized vol) also looks good, the real engine would need a shared capital-allocation layer, new `portfolio_runs`/`portfolio_equity_curves`/leg-mapping schema, portfolio-level walk-forward semantics, a decision on where DSR would apply, and promotion-schema integration — each a separate future decision, none scoped in detail yet. Paper-trading a blended portfolio is known to be harder still (no shared capital pool, no equity time series stored today) and stays out of scope even then.

    **Leverage sanity-check run same day (2026-07-22)**: added `annualized_vol()`/`apply_leverage()` to `blend_backtest.py` — idealized leverage (no financing/borrow cost, plain return-scaling + recompounding). Blended portfolio's realized vol is 6.6%/yr vs SPY's 17.7%/yr, so matching SPY's vol needs **2.69x leverage**. Result: total return **+358.0%** (vs SPY's +264.3%), max drawdown **-24.1%** (vs SPY's -34.1%), Sharpe unchanged at 1.01 (verified leverage-invariant to 9 decimal places — a real math check, not assumed). **At matched volatility, the levered blend beats SPY on both return and drawdown** — the diversify-then-leverage thesis clears its most important bar.
    - **Honest caveat, not glossed over**: the phase-1 plan's original go/no-go bar was "shallower drawdown than every individual leg, sustained after leverage" — that part is *not* met. Post-leverage drawdown (-24.1%) is deeper than `XLP/bollinger` (-11.0%) and `QQQ/breakout` (-12.3%) alone, and roughly tied with `QQQ/sma` (-24.6%). Leverage amplifies drawback as well as return, as expected — the genuinely good news is specifically "beats SPY at SPY's own risk level," not "risk-free improvement."
    - **What this doesn't prove**: this is one idealized calculation over one historical window — the same curve-fitting-to-one-backtest caution from item 24 applies at full force here, now amplified ~2.7x. It also assumes leverage is free (no margin interest, no leveraged-ETF decay/tracking error) — a real implementation would do worse than this best-case number. Not a decision to actually lever anything; a data point for whether phase 2 (the real portfolio engine) is worth building.

    **Walk-forward-style out-of-sample check run same day (2026-07-22)**: added `out_of_sample_leverage_check()` — mirrors `validation/walk_forward.py`'s own convention exactly (`n_splits=4`, `train_ratio=0.7`, same window-slicing logic): the leverage factor is computed from *train data only*, then applied to the held-out *test* slice, so the test result never peeks at the data it's judged on. No DSR correction needed (there's no grid search/selection here — one leverage factor per window, not a "best of several" pick).
    - **Result: 3 of 4 out-of-sample windows beat SPY on return.** Windows: 2017-01→2017-11 (+18.6% vs SPY +12.9%, drawdown -6.0% vs -3.0%), 2019-11→2020-10 covering the COVID crash (+17.7% vs SPY +9.3%, drawdown **-19.9% vs SPY's -34.1%** — the standout result, since it beat SPY on both return and drawdown through the market's worst recent stress test), 2022-10→2023-08 (+30.1% vs SPY +23.8%, drawdown -12.2% vs -7.6%).
    - **The one miss, not glossed over**: the most recent window (2025-09→2026-07) lost on both counts — return +12.0% vs SPY's +15.6%, drawdown -11.5% vs SPY's shallower -9.1%. One window isn't a trend, but it's a real data point against the thesis, not just a near-miss, and it's the window closest to today.
    - **Why this is meaningfully stronger evidence than the single full-history number above**: this is genuine non-lookahead validation — the same standard every individual strategy in this platform already has to clear — not just "does the aggregate number look good," which any sufficiently long backtest can produce by chance. It doesn't resolve whether the edge persists as new data keeps arriving; only running more real research cycles over time can answer that.

    **Thorough honest review, same day (2026-07-22)**: user asked directly whether this was enough to build the real portfolio engine, explicitly asking for full honesty about anything still missing. Real gaps surfaced, not glossed over:
    - **Correlation checked precisely, not assumed**: `QQQ/sma` vs `QQQ/breakout` = **0.525** (same ticker, meaningfully not independent) vs `XLP/bollinger`'s 0.237/0.038 vs each. The diversification benefit is really riding on one genuinely uncorrelated leg, not three — closer to N≈2 "bets" than 3 for Grinold's Law purposes.
    - **No 2008 in the data** — full history is 2015-01-02 → 2026-07-21; the financial crisis, arguably the most important diversification stress test, isn't covered at all. COVID (2020) is in there and the result was good, but it's one crash of one kind.
    - **Correlations can spike toward 1 in a real crisis** — a known general risk of diversification, not specific to this build; the measured low correlations are from a mostly-calm window plus one crash.
    - **Leverage factor is unstable across windows** (2.29x/2.28x/3.60x/2.34x) — a real position sized off a stale, calm-period multiplier could be badly wrong-sized right as a regime shifts.
    - **Zero cost modeling** — no margin interest, no leveraged-ETF decay. Every number so far is best-case/frictionless.
    - **Only one weighting had ever been tried** (equal-weight) before this review — flagged as an open robustness question, addressed same day (see below).
    - **No portfolio-level risk gate beyond max drawdown**, and no live/paper validation loop exists for the blend as a whole — the 3 legs still paper-trade with independent $10k notionals each, not a shared, levered blend.
    - **Verdict at the time**: promising enough to keep investing cheap research effort, not yet strong enough to justify the substantial phase-2 build. Ranked next steps given: (1) inverse-vol weighting check [done same day, below], (2) realistic cost drag on the leverage number, (3) correlation-during-COVID specifically, (4) wait for a genuinely new low-correlation promoted combo, (5) only then greenlight phase 2.

    **Inverse-vol weighting check, same day (2026-07-22)**: added `inverse_vol_weights()` (still a fixed formula — weight ∝ 1/realized-vol — not a search) and re-ran both the full-history comparison and the walk-forward OOS check with it, alongside equal-weight, for direct comparison. Weights aren't wildly different from equal (`QQQ/sma`=0.24, `QQQ/breakout`=0.35, `XLP/bollinger`=0.40 vs equal 0.33 each), since none of the three legs' volatilities are extreme outliers.
    - **Full-history**: inverse-vol blend gets a modestly higher Sharpe (1.07 vs 1.01) and, after re-matching to SPY's vol (needing slightly more leverage, 2.90x vs 2.69x, since the inverse-vol blend is a touch calmer), a higher levered total return (+409.6% vs +358.0%) at a very similar drawdown (-24.6% vs -24.1%).
    - **Out-of-sample (the check that actually matters)**: inverse-vol weighting still beats SPY in **3/4 windows**, same as equal-weight — it doesn't fix the one losing window (most recent, 2025-09→2026-07), and is very slightly worse there on drawdown (-12.6% vs -11.5%). The real win is in the COVID window specifically: inverse-vol returned +24.0% there (vs equal-weight's +17.7%) with a shallower drawdown (-18.0% vs -19.9%) — a genuine, non-lookahead improvement during the platform's one real crisis test.
    - **Honest read**: a real, modest improvement worth keeping (higher Sharpe, better crisis-window behavior, still a fixed formula not a search), but not decisive — it doesn't change the 3/4 win-count or rescue the one losing window. Doesn't move the phase-2 go/no-go decision on its own; the bigger open items (cost modeling, more genuinely independent legs, the missing 2008-style stress test) remain exactly as before.

    **Realistic leverage cost modeling, same day (2026-07-22) — the single most important finding of this whole investigation.** Added `annual_borrow_rate` to `apply_leverage()`: the standard margin-interest model (a rate charged only on the *borrowed* fraction, `leverage - 1`, not the whole position), tested at illustrative rates 0%/3%/5%/8% (not a live quote — flagged as such in the script; real rates vary by broker/account size/rate environment and should be checked before trusting a specific number). Full-history result:

    | Rate | Equal-weight levered return | Inverse-vol levered return |
    |---|---|---|
    | 0% (idealized) | +358.0% | +409.6% |
    | 3% | +155.7% | +164.2% |
    | 5% | +73.4% (below the *unlevered* blend's own +83.8%) | +70.5% |
    | 8% | **-3.2%** | **-11.6%** |

    Out-of-sample win-count (of 4 windows, levered blend beating SPY) collapses just as fast: 3/4 at 0% cost → 2/4 (equal) or 1/4 (inverse-vol) at just **3%** → 1/4 at 5% → 0/4 (equal) / 1/4 (inverse-vol) at 8%.
    - **What this means**: the entire "beats SPY" result from earlier in item 25 was almost entirely an artifact of assuming free leverage. At any real-world margin rate — even a cheap institutional-grade 3%, let alone a typical retail 5-8% — the core thesis mostly falls apart. This is the kind of gap that would have been very easy to miss by only looking at the idealized number, exactly the "be honest about anything still missing" the user asked for.
    - **Why the effect is this large**: leverage of ~2.7-2.9x means borrowing ~1.7-1.9x capital; even a modest annual rate on that much borrowed capital, compounded daily over an 11+ year backtest, adds up to an enormous cumulative drag — small daily costs compound as relentlessly as small daily gains do, just in the other direction.
    - **Verified before trusting it**: cost-adjusted Sharpe degrades monotonically as the rate increases (checked explicitly, not just eyeballed), and the rate=0.0 case reproduces the original idealized numbers exactly (regression-checked) — the cost mechanism doesn't introduce any new bug, this is the real effect of realistic financing cost on a multi-year backtest.
    - **Bottom line**: this materially changes the "is this enough to build the real engine" answer from before. The diversification thesis itself (Grinold's Law, unlevered) still holds — the blend's Sharpe and drawdown genuinely beat every individual leg, no leverage or cost involved in that specific claim. But the *leverage* half of "diversify, then leverage, to beat SPY" does not survive realistic costs at anything but the cheapest financing. Beating SPY this way would need either much cheaper financing than assumed, a genuinely higher-Sharpe blend (more, better-diversified legs) that needs less leverage to match SPY's risk, or accepting the diversification benefit on its own (smoother ride, still lagging SPY on raw return) rather than chasing "beat SPY."

    **Correlation-during-crisis check, same day (2026-07-22)** — closes one of the open items from the thorough honest review above: does the low pairwise correlation actually hold up *during* a real crisis, or only look good averaged over mostly-calm history? Computed each pair's correlation specifically within the COVID out-of-sample window (2019-11-22..2020-10-05) versus the 3 calmer walk-forward windows:

    | Pair | Calm windows (range) | COVID window | Full history |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `QQQ/sma` vs `QQQ/breakout` | 0.58–0.78 | **0.429** (lower) | 0.525 |
    | `QQQ/sma` vs `XLP/bollinger` | -0.19–0.16 | **0.475** (much higher) | 0.237 |
    | `QQQ/breakout` vs `XLP/bollinger` | -0.16–0.05 | **0.018** (about the same) | 0.038 |

    **Mixed, not uniform**: the "correlations spike in a crisis" risk flagged earlier did materialize for one pair (`QQQ/sma`/`XLP/bollinger` roughly tripled), but not for the other two — `QQQ/sma`/`QQQ/breakout` actually got *less* correlated during COVID, and `QQQ/breakout`/`XLP/bollinger` (the single best-diversifying pair in the blend) barely moved. Likely explains why the blend's actual COVID-window result held up well (item 25's OOS table: +17.7%/-19.9% levered vs SPY's +9.3%/-34.1%) — the benefit wasn't riding on all three pairs staying uncorrelated at once, just on at least one holding. Still only one crisis in the data, and a different kind of crash (credit/liquidity-driven, like 2008, still absent from the dataset) could plausibly hit all three at once instead of just one — this check narrows the open question, it doesn't close it.

26. **Blended paper-trading view built — captures the unlevered diversification benefit in live state for the first time (2026-07-22)** — user asked directly whether unlevered diversification alone was still worth pursuing (yes — it doesn't depend on leverage/cost assumptions, and rests on close-to-structural math given the measured correlations, 0.038-0.525, are genuinely below 1). But the live paper-trading system doesn't capture it: the 3 combos each run as independent $10,000 paper accounts, so the smoother-ride benefit found in backtest analysis wasn't visible anywhere in live state. Scoped and built the smallest legitimate fix — a read-only, derived view, no leverage, no schema changes to the live system, manual-invoke only:
    - **`src/quant_platform/portfolio.py` (new)** — `blend_curves`, `stats_from_equity`, `annualized_vol`, `inverse_vol_weights` promoted out of `blend_backtest.py` (verbatim) into a shared module, since a second script now needs the same generic equity-curve math. `blend_backtest.py` now imports them; refactor verified byte-identical stdout before/after.
    - **`store/paper.py`'s new `reconstruct_equity_curve()`** — reconstructs a full daily mark-to-market equity curve from a combo's `paper_trade_events` log (the live-paper-trading equivalent of what `backtest_equity_curves` stores for real backtests), replaying closed round-trips and marking any open position to market using daily close prices.
    - **`scripts/blend_paper_trading.py` (new)** — reports the 3 combos blended (equal-weight headline, inverse-vol shown alongside, matching `blend_backtest.py`'s own no-`--weights`-flag convention). Strictly read-only: does not touch `paper_trading.py`, `paper_trade.py`, `daily_paper_trade.sh`, or the `paper_trade_events` schema.
    - **Real bug caught by this build's own verification, not shipped**: the first version reconstructed each combo's curve starting on *its own* first event date. Since different strategies fire their first trade on different days, blending curves with different start dates either truncates to whichever combo started last, or — worse — silently averages together curves already offset from each other's true starting points (caught via a synthetic test with staggered entry dates: blended start value came out to $10,025, not the expected $10,000). Fixed by adding a `since` parameter: every combo in a blend is padded flat at $10,000 from the *earliest* first-event date across the group (a strategy that hasn't traded yet is just sitting on its allocated cash, unchanged) — re-verified with a synthetic 3-combo test including one combo with zero events at all, blended start value now exactly $10,000 as required.
    - **Honest current state**: live `paper_trade_events` has **zero rows** — every daily check so far has been flat/HOLD for all 3 combos, so there is no real accumulated history to blend yet. Running `scripts/blend_paper_trading.py` today prints an explicit "nothing logged yet, nothing to blend" message rather than a fabricated number — verified this is the actual behavior against the live DB. This build is genuinely "ready-to-observe infrastructure for forward evidence," not something with an interesting result today; it starts becoming useful the first time any of the 3 combos actually fires a trade.

27. **Universe expanded to 26 tickers (78 combos) — XLY, HYG, LQD — no new promotions (2026-07-22)** — user asked what other tickers/strategies to look into; identified `XLY` (the one GICS sector SPDR missing from the existing 23-ticker set) and `HYG`/`LQD` (corporate credit-spread risk, genuinely different from `TLT`/`IEF`'s pure rate-risk exposure) as the cheapest, most concrete additions. Pulled full 2015-2026 daily data for all three (2,904 rows each, matching the existing universe's date range) and ran a full research cycle across the expanded universe.
    - **Result: no new promotions.** All 9 new combos (3 tickers × 3 strategies) came back HOLD. The existing PROMOTE set is unchanged: `QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `XLP/bollinger`. An honest negative result, recorded plainly rather than glossed over — this hypothesis (that a missing sector or a genuinely different risk factor would surface a new low-correlation combo) didn't pan out this round. Doesn't change anything about the still-open "only ~2 independent bets" gap from the earlier honest review — the search continues, but this attempt didn't move it.

28. **Universe expanded to 30 tickers (90 combos) — two real new promotions, TIP/bollinger and FXI/bollinger (2026-07-22)** — user's stated goal shifted to explicitly beating the market, and the ranked plan from that discussion started with "find more genuinely independent edges" as the highest-leverage, cheapest lever. Added `TIP` (inflation-linked bonds — a genuinely different risk driver from `TLT`/`IEF`'s nominal rates and `HYG`/`LQD`'s credit spread), `SLV` (silver, different industrial-demand dynamics than `GLD`), and single-country funds `EWJ` (Japan) / `FXI` (China) — distinct monetary-policy regimes from the mostly-US-centric universe and the broad `EFA`/`EEM` regional baskets. Pulled full 2015-2026 data for all four, ran a 90-combo research cycle.
    - **Result: two new promotions.** `FXI/bollinger` (return +25.8%, max drawdown -24.0%, DSR 0.562, 3/4 evaluable OOS windows, worst OOS Sharpe 0.611) and `TIP/bollinger` (DSR 0.512, 4/4 evaluable OOS windows, worst OOS Sharpe 0.749 — the best OOS margin of any current PROMOTE combo).
    - **Correlation checked immediately, not assumed** — this was the actual point of the exercise:

    | | QQQ/sma | QQQ/breakout | XLP/bollinger | TIP/bollinger | FXI/bollinger |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | TIP/bollinger | 0.075 | 0.030 | -0.000 | — | 0.023 |
    | FXI/bollinger | 0.304 | 0.058 | 0.223 | 0.023 | — |

    `TIP/bollinger` is essentially uncorrelated with every other promoted combo — the best diversifier found so far, and a real answer to the "only ~2 independent bets" gap flagged in the earlier honest review (item 25). `FXI/bollinger` is decent but carries a moderate 0.304 link to `QQQ/sma`.
    - **Honest wrinkle on `TIP/bollinger`, surfaced immediately rather than glossed over**: its full-history return using the platform's standard default Bollinger parameters (`window=20, num_std=2.0`, from `registry.py`) is actually **-3.8%** — it only cleared promotion because walk-forward's grid search found a *different* parameter set that scored well independently in each held-out test window. Real, statistically legitimate evidence (not a bug) — but per this platform's own established discipline (never let a search choose "the" deployable default, the same principle behind the XLF/sma investigation, item 19), there's no single obviously-correct parameter set ready to paper-trade with. This is an open decision, not yet resolved. `FXI/bollinger` has no such issue — its default-parameter full-history return is a straightforward +25.8%.
    - **Not yet added to paper trading or to the phase-1 blend** — matches the established "propose, a human decides" pattern (same as `XLF/sma` and `XLP/bollinger` before it). Next decisions pending: what parameters to actually use for `TIP/bollinger`, and whether to re-run the diversification/leverage analysis (`scripts/blend_backtest.py`) with these two added to see whether more independent legs meaningfully improves the picture from item 25's cost-reality-check.

    **5-leg blend test, same day (2026-07-22) — a real, non-obvious counter-finding.** Ran the same diversification/leverage analysis with `TIP/bollinger` and `FXI/bollinger` added (ad-hoc, using `blend_backtest.py`'s functions directly — did not modify the file's `DEFAULT_COMBOS`, since these two aren't part of live paper trading). Idealized (zero-cost) picture improved: **4/4 out-of-sample windows beat SPY**, up from 3/4 with 3 legs — even the previously-losing recent window flipped to a win. But everything else got worse: blend Sharpe dropped (1.01 → 0.90), leverage needed to match SPY's vol rose (2.69x → 3.37x), and the realistic-cost win-count collapsed further (2/4 at 3% cost → **0/4**).
    - **Why**: this test used each combo's *currently stored* equity curve, which for `TIP/bollinger` means the default-parameter curve (-3.8% return, -0.10 Sharpe) flagged above as an open problem — an asset that's perfectly uncorrelated but has a *negative* expected return drags a blend down rather than helping it, and needing more leverage to compensate makes the cost-drag problem worse, not better.
    - **The real lesson, not just a caveat**: near-zero correlation alone doesn't make an asset worth adding to a portfolio — it also needs a genuinely positive standalone edge, or it actively hurts risk-adjusted quality despite the diversification math looking great. This sharpens (doesn't resolve) the open `TIP/bollinger` parameter question from above: before including it in anything, its usable parameters need to be pinned down and shown to have a real positive standalone return — not just DSR-validated OOS windows under search-chosen params that were never fixed into a deployable default.

    **TIP/bollinger parameter investigation, same day (2026-07-22) — found a real fix, but it's structurally blocked.** Dug into what parameters would give `TIP/bollinger` a genuine positive standalone edge, using two non-cherry-picked sources of evidence rather than just scanning the grid for the best-looking full-history number (which would reintroduce exactly the selection bias DSR exists to prevent):
    - **The walk-forward search's own train-only param choices, across its 4 windows**: `(25, 2.0)`, `(40, 2.5)`, `(40, 2.5)`, `(30, 2.5)` — `num_std=2.5` won 3 of 4 windows, and `(window=40, num_std=2.5)` was the outright plurality pick (2 of 4), chosen independently on different training slices without ever seeing full-history return.
    - **The full 8-point grid's full-history numbers form a smooth, monotonic trend**, not an isolated lucky spike — tighter bands lose money, wider bands make money, in strict order:

    | window | num_std | return | Sharpe | maxdd | trades |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | 10 | 1.5 | -10.58% | -0.308 | -16.55% | 129 |
    | 15 | 1.75 | -10.11% | -0.292 | -14.85% | 71 |
    | 20 (current default) | 2.0 | -3.82% | -0.098 | -10.96% | 52 |
    | 25 | 2.0 | -2.41% | -0.054 | -12.33% | 46 |
    | 30 | 2.5 | +3.66% | +0.145 | -8.02% | 25 |
    | **40** | **2.5** | **+4.47%** | **+0.167** | **-6.57%** | **20** |
    | 50 | 3.0 | +4.19% | +0.208 | -6.57% | 5 |
    | 60 | 3.0 | +5.86% | +0.279 | -6.57% | 6 |

    The largest two rows have the best headline numbers but only 5-6 trades over 11.5 years — too thin a sample to trust over the plurality-backed `(40, 2.5)`, which has 20 trades and independent support from the walk-forward search itself. **This makes sense structurally, not just numerically**: TIP is a low-volatility inflation-linked bond ETF — tight Bollinger bands (`window=20`) mostly trade its normal day-to-day noise, racking up whipsaw losses, while wide bands only trigger on genuine dislocations.
    - **Why this isn't a simple fix**: `registry.py`'s `default` params are keyed by *strategy family*, not by ticker+strategy — one `bollinger` default applies to every ticker's full-history risk gate, including `XLP/bollinger`, which is currently live paper-traded. Checked directly whether `(40, 2.5)` could just become the new global default:

    | ticker | params | return | Sharpe | maxdd | risk gate |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | XLP (live paper-traded) | (20, 2.0) current | +85.16% | +0.950 | -10.96% | PASS |
    | XLP | (40, 2.5) proposed | +44.67% | +0.644 | -8.76% | PASS |
    | TIP | (20, 2.0) current | -3.82% | -0.098 | -10.96% | PASS |
    | TIP | (40, 2.5) proposed | +4.47% | +0.167 | -6.57% | PASS |
    | FXI (promoted, not yet live) | (20, 2.0) current | +25.78% | +0.286 | -24.04% | PASS |
    | FXI | (40, 2.5) proposed | +16.06% | +0.211 | -25.69% | **FAIL** |

    Widening the global default fixes TIP but nearly halves XLP's return/Sharpe (a live, working combo, made meaningfully worse for no reason of its own) and flips FXI's risk gate from PASS to FAIL (just barely — the -25% limit is a hard line, and -25.69% crosses it). **There is no single global bollinger default that serves all three tickers well** — confirms, with real numbers, exactly the reasoning `registry.py`'s own docstring already gives for keeping `default` static and human-curated rather than derived: different tickers genuinely need different params, and letting any one search or fix pick a global winner trades one ticker's problem for another's.
    - **Conclusion — not resolved, correctly not resolved**: `(window=40, num_std=2.5)` is a real, well-supported, non-cherry-picked fix for `TIP/bollinger` specifically, but applying it requires per-ticker parameter overrides — an architectural change `registry.py` doesn't support today (it only supports per-strategy-family defaults). That's a real feature to consider, not a quick edit, so nothing was changed. Until then, the honest, safe conclusion is: `TIP/bollinger`'s "PROMOTE" verdict is statistically real (the walk-forward search found a genuine edge), but it must **not** be added to live paper trading or the blend using today's global default params, since those are known-negative for TIP specifically. It stays "watch, don't deploy as-is."

    **Per-ticker override mechanism built, same day (2026-07-22) — resolves the fix, but doesn't change what's deployed.** Added `TICKER_OVERRIDES` (a `{(ticker, strategy_name): params}` dict) and `get_default_params(ticker, strategy_name)` to `registry.py`: returns the ticker-specific override if one exists, else falls back to the strategy family's shared `default`. Populated with exactly one entry — `("TIP", "bollinger"): {"window": 40, "num_std": 2.5}` — the plurality-backed fix found above. `run_backtest.py`, `paper_trade.py`, and `research_cycle.py`'s `_run_one_combo` all switched from reading `spec["default"]` directly to calling `get_default_params()`; `grid` (walk-forward's search space) is untouched, since it was never the problem.
    - **Verified**: `TIP/bollinger` now full-history backtests at `{'window': 40, 'num_std': 2.5}` — +4.47% return, Sharpe +0.17, max drawdown -6.57%, risk gate PASS (matches the investigation's numbers exactly). `XLP/bollinger` and `FXI/bollinger` re-ran with no override entry and are byte-for-byte unchanged (still resolve to the family default `(20, 2.0)`, same +85.16%/+25.78% returns as before). A scoped research cycle (`--tickers TIP --strategies bollinger`) confirmed the promotion verdict is unaffected (`TIP/bollinger` still `PROMOTE`, as expected — the verdict was always driven by walk-forward/DSR, not the default-param return sign) and correctly reflects the new params in the fresh `backtest_runs` row. A `paper_trade.py TIP --strategy bollinger` dry check also resolved correctly (HOLD, flat, no error).
    - **Still not deployed — that's a separate decision.** `TIP/bollinger` is not added to `daily_paper_trade.sh`, `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`, or the phase-1 blend. This build only fixes *what params TIP/bollinger would use if it were live* — whether to actually go live with it (and re-check the diversification/leverage picture with a genuinely positive-return TIP leg instead of the negative-return one used in the 5-leg blend test above) is the next open decision, not yet made.

    **5-leg blend test re-run with TIP's fixed params, same day (2026-07-22) — genuinely better, but still doesn't rescue the leverage thesis.** Re-ran the exact same ad-hoc 5-leg test (`QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `XLP/bollinger`, `TIP/bollinger`, `FXI/bollinger`), now picking up `TIP/bollinger`'s new `(40, 2.5)`-param equity curve automatically via `load_leg_curves()` (it reads the latest `backtest_runs` row per combo, which the override build's own verification already refreshed).
    - **Zero-cost picture improved further**: out-of-sample win-count is now **4/4** (up from 3/4 with 3 legs, and matching the earlier 4/4 seen with TIP's old broken curve — the previously-losing window flipped to a win either way).
    - **Realistic-cost picture is better than before, but still worse than the 3-leg baseline**: at 3% financing, win-count is **1/4** — better than the old negative-TIP result (0/4), but still below the original 3-leg blend's 2/4. At 5%+ it's 0/4 in both the 5-leg and old-TIP cases.
    - **Why fixing TIP's return wasn't enough, and it's a real second finding, not a rounding error**: the leverage needed to match SPY's volatility *increased* from 2.69x (3 legs) to 3.41x (5 legs) — `TIP/bollinger`'s new params make it a very calm asset (max drawdown only -6.6%), which lowers the blend's overall volatility, and lower blend volatility means *more* leverage is needed to hit SPY's vol target. Financing cost scales directly with leverage. Meanwhile both `TIP/bollinger` (Sharpe +0.17) and `FXI/bollinger` (Sharpe +0.29) have modest Sharpe next to the original three legs' 0.67-0.95 — so even at near-zero correlation and now-positive return, averaging in two lower-Sharpe legs still pulls the blend's own Sharpe down slightly (1.01 → 0.93), and the extra leverage needed to compensate costs more than the added diversification saves.
    - **One genuine bright spot, unlevered**: the 5-leg blend's max drawdown is shallower than the 3-leg blend's (-7.9% vs -9.5%) for almost no Sharpe cost (0.93 vs 1.01) — as a pure "smoother ride, no leverage" play, adding the now-fixed `TIP/bollinger` and `FXI/bollinger` is a small, real, unlevered win. It's specifically the leverage-to-beat-SPY thesis that stays broken.
    - **The sharpened lesson, now confirmed twice**: near-zero correlation plus a positive standalone return still isn't sufficient to help a *leveraged* blend — an added leg's own Sharpe needs to be reasonably competitive with what's already in the blend, or it lowers blend volatility enough to demand more leverage than its own quality can pay for in financing cost. Diversification for its own sake (unlevered) and diversification as a leverage enabler are different bars, and this universe's newer combos (`TIP`, `FXI`) clear the first but not the second.

    **`TIP/bollinger` and `FXI/bollinger` added to live paper trading, same day (2026-07-22).** With the leverage/blend question settled (both help unlevered, neither belongs in any leveraged plan), asked directly whether they're worth paper-trading as independent bets — same bar every other combo here was added on (`PROMOTE` verdict, acceptable correlation, non-degenerate standalone stats), not contingent on the blend/leverage thesis. Both clear it: `TIP/bollinger` is the least-correlated combo found all session and now has a genuinely positive fixed-param edge; `FXI/bollinger` has a solid standalone return (+25.8%, Sharpe +0.29) and only moderate correlation (0.304 with `QQQ/sma`).
    - Added both to `daily_paper_trade.sh` and `research_cycle.py`'s `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` (now 5 combos total). Ran `paper_trade.py` once manually for each first, matching the rollout precedent set by `XLP/bollinger` (item 22) — both came back clean (HOLD, flat, exit 0).
    - **One flag carried forward, not resolved**: `FXI/bollinger`'s max drawdown (-24.0%) sits close to the platform's hard -25% risk limit — worth watching its live results more closely than the other four, the same kind of "not a reason to exclude it, but watch it" flag already on `XLP/bollinger` (item 22, thin OOS margin).
    - **Scope stayed disciplined**: neither combo was added to the phase-1 blend (`blend_backtest.py`/`blend_paper_trading.py`'s `DEFAULT_COMBOS` are both still the original 3) — the blend/leverage analysis above is exactly why not. This is standalone paper trading only, each combo still its own independent $10,000 account, no leverage anywhere in the live system.

29. **New strategy family (RSI mean reversion) + two new asset classes (crypto, volatility) — one real new promotion, `XLRE/rsi` (2026-07-22)** — after several ticker-search rounds showing diminishing returns (13 new tickers across items 27-28 produced only 2 promotions), the higher-leverage move was a genuinely new *strategy* family rather than another ticker batch, since every ticker so far had only ever been tested against the same 3 strategies.
    - **`RsiReversionStrategy`** (`src/quant_platform/strategies/rsi_reversion.py`) — mean reversion built from the Relative Strength Index (ratio of average recent gains to average recent losses, a bounded 0-100 oscillator) rather than Bollinger's price-vs-rolling-std-dev bands. Deliberately a different underlying mechanism, not a relabeled Bollinger, even though both are "mean reversion" in spirit. Wired into `registry.py` as a 4th family (default `window=14, oversold=30`, 8-point grid); no other file needed changes since `run_backtest.py`/`paper_trade.py`/`research_cycle.py` all already iterate `STRATEGIES.keys()` generically.
    - **Two new tickers, two new asset classes**: `BTC-USD` (crypto — no equity/bond/commodity/currency mechanism links it to anything else in the universe; note it trades 365 days/year rather than the ~252-day equity calendar, which needed no code change since `promotion.py`'s correlation check already aligns on shared dates via `dropna()` before computing correlation) and `VXX` (a volatility/"fear gauge" product, usually negatively correlated with equities — flagged honestly that VXX has a well-known structural decay from contango roll cost, a real risk to any promoted VXX combo's apparent edge, not just a footnote).
    - **Result**: ran the full 32-ticker × 4-strategy (128-combo) research cycle. Exactly one new promotion — **`XLRE/rsi`** (real estate sector SPDR, RSI mean reversion): +33.55% full-history return at default params (no `TIP`-style parameter mismatch), Sharpe 0.45, max drawdown -20.17%, DSR 0.62, worst evaluable OOS Sharpe **1.27** — the strongest OOS margin of any promoted combo this session. One walk-forward window was correctly excluded from the average as degenerate (a chosen param set that never traded in that test period, the same `sufficient_oos_evidence` gate from item 19 working as designed on the new strategy family).
    - **`BTC-USD` and `VXX` produced zero promotions across all 4 strategies** — an honest negative result, recorded plainly rather than glossed over. Neither new asset class turned up anything on this pass.
    - **Correlation checked before treating this as a real find**: `XLRE/rsi`'s correlation with the 5 already-paper-traded combos is 0.206 (`QQQ/sma`), 0.044 (`QQQ/breakout`), 0.370 (`XLP/bollinger`), 0.127 (`TIP/bollinger`), 0.248 (`FXI/bollinger`) — all comfortably under the 0.7 correlation-gate threshold, confirmed by the promotion evaluator's own gate (it still cleared `PROMOTE`). The 0.370 link to `XLP/bollinger` (both touch "defensive" sectors — real estate, consumer staples) is the one worth remembering, not treating this as a fully independent bet from that one.
    - **Added to live paper trading, same day (2026-07-22)** — cleared the same bar `TIP/bollinger`/`FXI/bollinger` were added on (item 28): `PROMOTE` verdict, acceptable correlation, non-degenerate standalone stats. Added to `daily_paper_trade.sh` and `research_cycle.py`'s `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` (now 6 combos total). Ran `paper_trade.py` once manually first, matching the established rollout precedent — came back clean (HOLD, flat, exit 0). Not added to the phase-1 blend (`DEFAULT_COMBOS` in `blend_backtest.py`/`blend_paper_trading.py` stay the original 3) — that would need its own blend/leverage re-check first, same discipline as `TIP`/`FXI`.

30. **Long/short symmetry added to the backtest/research pipeline — zero new promotions, an honest and informative negative result (2026-07-22)** — asked directly what would most raise profitability; the top pick was that every strategy here is long-only (a down-trend or overbought signal just exits to cash, never profits from the decline), so adding a short side roughly doubles the opportunity set of strategies already proven to work, for no new data cost.
    - **`Strategy` base class** (`base.py`) gained an opt-in `allow_short: bool = False` class attribute and a concrete (non-abstract) `generate_short_signals()` defaulting to "never shorts" — every existing strategy instance is unaffected unless it explicitly opts in.
    - **All 4 strategies got a mirrored short condition** — the structural opposite of their existing long logic: SMA crossover shorts the downward cross (its old `exits` condition) and covers on the upward cross (its old `entries`); Bollinger shorts a new `upper` band breach and covers at the mean from above; Donchian breakout shorts a break below the channel low and covers on reclaiming the channel high; RSI shorts a new `overbought` threshold (mirrored as `100 - oversold` by default, keeping the grid 2-dimensional) and covers at the mirrored exit level. Each strategy also got a thin marker subclass (e.g. `SmaCrossoverLongShortStrategy`, `allow_short = True`) purely so `registry.py` can register a distinct `strategy_name` (`sma_ls`, `bollinger_ls`, `breakout_ls`, `rsi_ls`) without changing the long-only class's default behavior at all.
    - **A real vectorbt constraint found and fixed during the build, not before**: `vbt.Portfolio.from_signals`'s `SizeType.Percent` (used here so position size is a fraction of equity) cannot resolve a same-bar long-to-short reversal — vectorbt's own docstring: *"SizeType.Percent does not support position reversal using signals."* This is the normal case here, not an edge case — a strategy's short-entry condition is often *literally* its old exit condition (e.g. SMA crossover's), so a flip attempts to close-and-reverse on the identical bar. Fixed in `backtest/run.py`'s `run_backtest()`: whenever an opposite-direction entry coincides with today's exit, it's deferred to the next bar — guarantees one flat bar at every flip instead of erroring. A minor, honestly-documented simplification, the same spirit as this module's existing same-bar signal/execution note.
    - **Deliberate scope boundary, held throughout**: this only touches the backtest/research/promotion pipeline. `paper_trade.py`, `daily_paper_trade.sh`, `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`, and the phase-1 blend are untouched — extending live paper trading to shorts would need a real rewrite (a `direction` column in `paper_trade_events`, a 3+-state decision machine in `paper_trading.py`, sign-corrected return math in two places in `store/paper.py`), and it wasn't yet known whether any long/short variant would even promote. Speculative effort avoided; if that changes, it's a natural, separately-scoped follow-up. Made loud, not silent: `check_and_update()` now raises `NotImplementedError` immediately if ever invoked against an `allow_short=True` strategy, rather than silently paper-trading only the long half.
    - **Result: ran the full 32-ticker × 8-strategy (256-combo, 4 long-only + 4 long-short) research cycle. Zero long/short combos promoted (0/128), versus 6/128 for the long-only families (unchanged from item 29).** Two concrete, illustrative examples:
      - `QQQ/sma_ls`: strictly worse everywhere — return 95%→35%, Sharpe 0.67→0.31, max drawdown -24.6%→**-32.5%** (now fails the risk gate). QQQ has trended up roughly 9x over the 11-year backtest window; shorting into a persistent uptrend adds losses and volatility rather than diversification.
      - `XLRE/rsi_ls`: the opposite pattern — full-history numbers actually look *better* than the long-only version (return 34%→61%, Sharpe 0.45→0.56, risk gate still passes), but walk-forward correctly catches that this doesn't hold up out-of-sample (one test window's Sharpe goes negative, failing `all_oos_windows_positive`) — a clean, concrete illustration of exactly why this platform validates out-of-sample instead of trusting a single full-history number.
      - Aggregated across all 128 long/short combos vs. their 128 long-only counterparts: risk-gate failures nearly doubled (82% vs 48%), and DSR failures rose too (52% vs 34%) — shorting adds real, measurable risk across this mostly-bull-market 2015-2026 universe faster than it adds validated edge.
    - **Honest interpretation, not a dead end**: this result is specific to *this* historical window (predominantly rising markets across equities, bonds, and most sector ETFs) and to *these* simple mirrored conditions (the same window/threshold search as the long side, not independently tuned for shorting). It doesn't mean shorting can never work here — it means the naive mirror-image approach, tested against 11 years that were mostly a bull market, doesn't currently clear this platform's bar. A different historical stretch (a real bear market), or short-specific parameter tuning, could change this — neither has been tried yet.

31. **Regime gating — a genuine win, more than doubling the promoted set (6→13), plus a real, actionable side effect (2026-07-22)** — asked what to build next; picked regime gating over the bigger, longer-deferred portfolio engine because it's cheap (no new data/tickers) and directly improves strategies already proven to work: every strategy fires in every market condition today, so a trend-following signal fires even in a choppy market (whipsaw risk) and a mean-reversion signal fires even during a real trend (catching a falling knife).
    - **`RegimeGatedStrategy`** (new `src/quant_platform/strategies/regime.py`) — a generic wrapper, not four re-implementations: masks any inner strategy's *entries* (never exits — an open position must always be able to close on its own signal) using Kaufman's Efficiency Ratio (net directional price change over a window, divided by the sum of that window's day-to-day absolute changes — near 1 means genuinely trending, near 0 means the movement mostly canceled out as noise). Chose ER over the more "industry-standard" ADX specifically because it's one rolling computation, not a multi-stage smoothing chain — a better fit with every other strategy here, and easier to verify correct (confirmed on synthetic data: pure monotonic trend → ER=1.0 exactly, pure oscillation → ER=0.0 exactly). Trend-following families (`sma`, `breakout`) are gated to only trade when trending; mean-reversion families (`bollinger`, `rsi`) are gated to only trade when not. 4 new registry entries (`sma_regime`, `bollinger_regime`, `breakout_regime`, `rsi_regime`), same fixed `default`/`grid` as their base family — the regime window/threshold are fixed defaults, not searched, same reasoning as every other "fixed a-priori formula" choice this session (blend weights, leverage factors, per-ticker overrides).
    - **Deliberately scoped to the 4 long-only families only** — not applied to the `_ls` long/short variants (avoids a 3-way combinatorial explosion before knowing whether regime-gating helps on its own), and **not wired into paper trading or the phase-1 blend**, regardless of result — same "propose, human decides" pattern as every prior new combo.
    - **Result: ran the full 32-ticker × 12-strategy (384-combo) research cycle. 8 new promotions from regime-gated combos** — `SPY/breakout_regime` (worst OOS Sharpe 0.97 — the strongest since `XLRE/rsi`, and notably `SPY` itself has never promoted on any strategy before this session), `EWJ/rsi_regime` (0.86), `MTUM/rsi_regime` (0.45), `FXI/bollinger_regime` (0.28), `XLE/bollinger_regime` (0.12), and three thinner-margin ones worth flagging the same way `XLP/bollinger`'s thin OOS margin already is: `XLP/bollinger` itself (0.061, unchanged), `QQQ/bollinger_regime` (0.032), `XLY/rsi_regime` (0.039), and `GLD/bollinger_regime` (0.017) — all technically clear the threshold but by a thin margin, worth watching more closely than the rest if any of these are ever deployed.
    - **A real, actionable side effect, not just a new-combo list**: `FXI/bollinger` — currently **live in paper trading** — was demoted from `PROMOTE` to `HOLD` by this same cycle. Confirmed via `promotion_snapshot_history`, not assumed: it's correlated 0.82 with the new `FXI/bollinger_regime` (essentially the same underlying trades, just entry-filtered), which now has a higher DSR (0.604 vs 0.562) — the correlation-aware promotion gate (built earlier this session, item 15/32-ish) correctly recognized these as the same bet and kept only the better version. This is exactly the kind of event `research_cycle.py` was built to flag loudly.
    - **Swapped, same day (2026-07-22)**, after pulling up both combos' standalone stats side by side: `FXI/bollinger_regime` wins on full-history return (+38.7% vs +25.8%), Sharpe (0.44 vs 0.29), max drawdown (-20.0% vs -24.0%, meaningfully further from the -25% risk limit), and win rate (78.4% vs 70.6%) — but has a genuinely weaker worst-case OOS Sharpe (0.28 vs 0.61) and fewer trades (37 vs 51). A real trade-off, not a clean win, but judged worth it: regime-gating's whole mechanism is declining whipsaw-prone entries, so the trade-count drop mostly reflects that correctly, not just a thinner, less-trustworthy sample. Swapped in both `daily_paper_trade.sh` and `research_cycle.py`'s `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`; ran `paper_trade.py FXI --strategy bollinger_regime` once manually first (clean, HOLD/flat, exit 0), matching the established rollout precedent. Old `FXI/bollinger`'s `paper_trade_events` history is untouched (append-only) — just no longer checked daily going forward. 6 combos remain live in paper trading, one swapped: `QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `XLP/bollinger`, `TIP/bollinger`, `FXI/bollinger_regime`, `XLRE/rsi`.
    - **Correlation checked across the full 13-combo promoted set**, not just the new ones vs. the old: nothing crosses the 0.7 gate threshold (closest: `QQQ/bollinger_regime` vs. `XLY/rsi_regime` at 0.664), confirming the gate's own logic already enforced this, not just a spot-check.

32. **"Why not just buy SPY?" answered honestly with real numbers, then the actual thesis tested for the first time (2026-07-22)** — user looked at the full-history numbers and asked directly: if SPY buy-hold (+264%, CAGR 11.8%, Sharpe 0.72) beats every one of the 6 live combos on both return and Sharpe except `XLP/bollinger` (0.95 Sharpe, a third of the drawdown), what's the point of any of this? Answer given without spin: **QQQ/sma and QQQ/breakout are worse than doing nothing** — worse total return *and* worse Sharpe than just holding QQQ — because trading in and out of an asset that goes essentially straight up for 11 years can only lose to holding it; that's arithmetic, not a subtle flaw. The full 2015-2026 backtest window is one of the strongest bull decades on record, and this platform's thesis was never "beat a raging bull" (the leveraged-blend attempt in item 25 already found that doesn't survive real financing costs) — it's that a diversified/tactical book should hold up *better than buy-and-hold when the market doesn't go up*. That claim had never actually been tested.
    - **Data constraint stated up front, not glossed over**: `ohlcv_daily` starts 2015-01-02 for every ticker in this universe — confirmed by querying `min(date)` per ticker, not assumed — so 2000-2002 and 2007-2009 are simply not in this database and cannot be tested. Found the only three SPY drawdowns >15% that *are* in the available history by inspecting real closes: 2018 Q4 selloff (2018-09-20→2018-12-24, SPY -20.2%), the COVID crash (2020-02-19→2020-03-23, SPY -34.1%, the fastest bear market on record), and the 2022 bear market (2022-01-03→2022-10-12, SPY -25.4%, QQQ -34.6%).
    - **Built `scripts/bear_market_test.py`** — slices each of the 6 live combos' already-stored full-history equity curves (plus a fresh equal-weight blended "book" via the existing `blend_curves()`, plus reconstructed SPY/QQQ buy-hold curves) down to each window and computes stats on the slice only. Deliberately reuses stored continuous curves rather than re-running a truncated backtest — slicing preserves whatever position each combo was actually in when the crash started (what would really have happened), and `stats_from_equity()` already only depends on a raw dollar Series, so this required no new backtest engine.
    - **Result: decisive, and in the platform's favor for the first time.** In all three windows the blended book lost dramatically less than either benchmark:

      | Window | Book | SPY buy-hold | QQQ buy-hold |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | 2018 Q4 selloff | **-2.3%** (DD -2.9%) | -20.2% | -22.3% |
      | COVID crash | **-7.5%** (DD -7.5%) | -34.1% | -28.1% (DD -28.6%) |
      | 2022 bear market | **-3.6%** (DD -6.0%) | -25.4% | -34.6% |

      No single leg was consistently the best defender — `TIP/bollinger` protected best in the COVID crash (+3.4%) and did nothing in 2018 (flat, no signal fired); `XLP/bollinger` protected best in the 2022 grind (+7.5%) but was middling in COVID (-0.8%); `QQQ/sma`'s slow-moving signal lagged the COVID crash's speed and took the worst single-leg hit (-19.0%) that window specifically. **The robustness comes from the blend, not from any one strategy reliably working in every bad market** — exactly the diversification argument (Grinold's Law) this session has leaned on, now confirmed against real drawdowns instead of just correlation coefficients.
