    - **Honest limits of this result**: only 3 windows, drawn from the same 11-year span already used to select and validate these very combos — not a true out-of-sample test of "does diversification work in bad markets" in the way walk-forward validation is out-of-sample for a single strategy's parameters. Two of history's largest bear markets (dot-com, GFC) remain completely untested since the data doesn't exist. Scoped as read-only analysis — no code changed in the live pipeline, nothing wired into paper trading differently, matches this session's standard "test in research, decide separately" pattern.
    - **This is the answer to "why not just buy SPY"**: because on this data, buy-and-hold gives up 20-34% in the bad decades of an 11-year span to earn the extra return in the good years, and this book gives up almost all of that extra bull-market return but only loses single-digits in the same bad stretches. Whether that trade is worth it depends on the user's actual objective (maximize return assuming the next decade looks like this one, vs. avoid a repeat of 2008) — a question posed back to the user, not decided unilaterally.

33. **Research Agent built: LLM-driven strategy discovery, human-gated at every exit (2026-07-22)** — after concluding the bear-market test (item 32) that this project's value is defensiveness, not outright market-beating, the user asked directly why there isn't an agent constantly inventing and testing new strategies on its own. This is literally the "Research Agent"/"Evolution Agent" from the project's own founding vision doc, and had actually been raised once before and deliberately deferred (`Enhancement Ideas.md`) for two reasons: unbounded strategy generation breaks DSR's clean multiple-testing correction, and unsupervised generated code is a real correctness risk (a lookahead bug can make a strategy "backtest well" for a fake reason). Asked what to do about the second risk, the user proposed a code-review agent and repeated backtesting — both instincts right, translated into something technically real: a second LLM reviewing its own class of bug shares the same blind spots as the LLM that wrote it, so the primary defense is a deterministic static check, not a second opinion; and re-running an identical backtest on unchanged data is a no-op (vectorbt has no randomness), so the real version of "backtest it over and over" is a parameter-sensitivity check.
    - **Full pipeline, human-gated at every exit, nothing auto-added to `registry.py`/`daily_paper_trade.sh`/`CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` under any condition**: LLM proposes a new `Strategy` subclass (new package `src/quant_platform/research_agent/`) → **static AST lint** (`safety_lint.py` — import allowlist, banned calls/dunder access, `.shift()`/`.rolling(center=True)`/`.tail()` lookahead-pattern rejection; the real defense, mechanical and auditable, not an LLM checking its own work) → **resource-limited sandboxed smoke test** (`sandbox.py`/`_sandbox_runner.py` — subprocess, restricted builtins/import enforcing the same allowlist a second, independent time, no network, CPU/memory/file-descriptor limits) → the **existing** backtest/walk-forward/promotion pipeline completely unchanged (candidates use `strategy_name = candidate_id` in the existing `backtest_runs`/`walk_forward_windows` tables, so `promotion.py` needed zero code changes) → **parameter-sensitivity stability check** (`stability.py` — perturbs params ±15%, re-runs `evaluate_criteria()`; a strategy that only clears the bar at one exact hardcoded value fails here even if it sailed through the other five) → optional advisory LLM secondary review (never a pass/fail gate, explicitly because it shares blind spots with the LLM that wrote the code) → a human-readable report (`reports/research_agent/<run_id>.md`) plus a new `strategy_candidates` DB table (`store/candidates.py`) tracking every candidate, approved or rejected, exactly as thoroughly as this project already tracks everything else. Candidate source files live in a new top-level `candidates/` directory, gitignored, physically separate from `strategies/` so nothing here is ever mistakable for an approved strategy.
    - **Verified with 5 fixtures before any real LLM call, per the plan's own verification requirement**: `--fixture trivial` ran cleanly end to end and confirmed via `git status` that `registry.py`/`daily_paper_trade.sh` were untouched; `--fixture lookahead_bug` (`.shift(-1)`) was rejected at lint, never reaching a backtest; `--fixture network_bug` (`import os`/`os.system`) was rejected at lint via the import allowlist, **and independently confirmed the sandbox's restricted-import blocks it too** when fed directly to `run_sandboxed()` bypassing lint on purpose (proving defense-in-depth, not single-layer reliance); `--fixture interface_bug` (returns one Series instead of two) passed lint but was caught specifically by the sandbox's interface-conformance check; `--fixture hardcoded_overfit_bug` (a pure calendar-pulse entry every N rows, no price relationship at all) was tuned against real QQQ data to find `window=25` — the one value in `range(3, 60)` that clears all five existing promotion criteria purely by which calendar days it happened to land on, while `window=21`/`29` (its own ±15% perturbations) both fail — and the stability check correctly caught it as the only stage that did, exactly the "backtest it over and over" idea working as intended.
    - **First paid, external dependency in the project** (`anthropic` SDK, installed) — every other capability here has been free. `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is not set in this environment, so the one remaining step from the plan's own verification sequence — one real, smallest-blast-radius LLM call (`--tickers QQQ --n-candidates 1`, no `--fixture`) — has not yet been run. The user needs to supply their own key (console.anthropic.com) before that step happens; every call's token counts and `estimated_cost_usd` are persisted per candidate row once it does.
    - **Explicit scope boundaries, same as every prior build this session**: no 24/7 or scheduled execution (daily-bar data means more-than-daily has nothing new to act on, established earlier this session); no auto-registration or auto-paper-trading under any condition; screens against a single ticker by default, not the full 32-ticker universe (that's a separate, later, human-triggered step). The sandbox is stated honestly as defense-in-depth against a buggy-but-legitimate LLM completion, not a hardened security boundary against a genuinely adversarial actor — the real backstop, unchanged, is a human reading the code before it ever reaches `registry.py`.

34. **Triage assistant built — sorts the review pile, deliberately does not approve anything (2026-07-23)** — asked how much the Research Agent costs to run (roughly half a cent to a penny per idea using the cheap default model — measured from the real prompt, not guessed) and how often it should run automatically (recommended weekly, 2-3 ideas at a time — cost isn't the limiting factor, human review time and the AI's limited supply of genuinely-different ideas are). The user then asked for a second AI agent to "read the code and make sure nothing looks wrong" and "do the other reviewing" for the pile. Pushed back on the second half specifically: a second AI approving the first AI's work doesn't remove the actual risk, since both are the same kind of system and can share the exact same blind spot at the same time — the human-approval gate is deliberately the one job in this pipeline that stays human. The genuinely useful version of the request — help with a pile too large to read in full — is sorting, not approving.
    - **`research_agent/triage.py`** (new, read-only) — reads `strategy_candidates`, splits already-mechanically-rejected candidates (nothing to read there) from ones still `pending_human_review`, flags near-duplicate survivors by reusing `promotion.py`'s own return-correlation check (the same "is this the same bet wearing different clothes" math the correlation-aware promotion gate already applies to real, live strategies, applied here to the candidate pile instead), and ranks the rest by not-a-duplicate → cleared-promotion-criteria → passed-stability-check → highest Sharpe. Never writes to `human_decision`/`final_status` — confirmed by re-querying the table after running it. One optional LLM call (`llm_client.triage_summary()`) turns that already-decided structure into a short plain-English reading-order note; advisory only, same as the per-candidate secondary review, and explicitly told in its own prompt not to change the ordering.
    - **`scripts/research_agent_triage.py`** (new) writes `reports/research_agent/triage_<timestamp>.md`. Tested against the real candidate pile already sitting in the database from item 33's fixture runs (4 mechanically-rejected, 3 pending) — ranking came out correct (the one `PROMOTE` verdict first despite its stability-check failure, then the two `HOLD` candidates ordered by Sharpe), and `git status` plus a direct re-query confirmed nothing was approved, rejected, or changed by running it.
    - **User-facing framing that mattered here**: explained with a concrete example (a strategy that "backtests well" only because it happened to trade heavily during a specific real one-off event, like 2020-2021 meme-stock volume spikes, rather than a real repeatable pattern) why a human's real-world context is the thing no automatic check — mechanical or AI — can fully replace, which is why the approval step couldn't just be automated away even though the reading-volume problem was real and worth solving.

35. **Off-computer backups set up for both code and data (2026-07-23)** — asked "what's next," recommended closing the loop opened by every prior "committed" without a remote: the code was only ever saved locally, and the actual database (all price history, backtest/paper-trading/candidate results — the real accumulated work) wasn't backed up anywhere at all.
    - **Code → private GitHub repo** (`github.com/jakestuetzer/quant-platform`). The user's existing default SSH key turned out to already be registered to a *different* GitHub account (confirmed: GitHub said "already in use" when re-adding it, yet the connection still failed as `jakestuetzer`) — rather than risk more confusion with that key, generated a brand-new one dedicated to this project (`~/.ssh/id_ed25519_quantplatform`), wired through a dedicated `~/.ssh/config` host alias (`github-quantplatform`) so it can't collide with whatever the old key is used for elsewhere. Full commit history pushed once the new key was confirmed authenticating as `jakestuetzer`.
    - **Data → `scripts/backup_database.sh` + `scheduling/com.quantplatform.dbbackup.plist`** (daily 06:00, clear of the 17:00 paper-trading job). Copies `data/market.duckdb` to iCloud Drive as a dated snapshot, keeping the 14 most recent. **Two real bugs found, only because the actual scheduled path was tested end-to-end via `launchctl start` rather than trusting a direct manual run that looked fine**, matching this project's established verification standard: (1) the script used `$HOME` to find the destination, but launchd jobs don't reliably have that set — hardcoded the path instead, same reasoning as every hardcoded path already in this project's plists; (2) a bash 3.2 quirk (macOS's `/bin/bash` is still the old 3.2.57) where a `while read` loop that never executes its body reports exit status 1, which under `set -e -o pipefail` silently aborted the script *after* the actual backup (`cp`) had already succeeded but *before* the log line — wrapped the cleanup pipeline in `|| true` since a failed prune should never count as the run itself failing. A third issue wasn't a bug in this project's code at all: macOS blocks background (non-interactive) processes from writing into iCloud Drive without Full Disk Access explicitly granted — needed the user to grant that to `/bin/bash` via System Settings themselves (a system-security change, not something to do unilaterally), flagged honestly that this grants bash broad file access, not just to this one backup. Verified reproducible with two consecutive clean `launchctl start` runs after the fixes.
    - **Known gap, stated plainly**: this is a plain file copy, not a database-level consistent export — safe only because nothing else writes to this DB at 06:00 (confirmed: only the 17:00 paper-trading job and manual research runs ever write to it). If that assumption ever stops holding, this needs to switch to a real DuckDB export instead of `cp`.

36. **Long/short re-tested specifically against the 3 bear-market windows — the open question from item 30 is now answered, and it flips the earlier result (2026-07-23)** — item 30's long/short build found zero promotions across the full 32-ticker universe, with two named follow-ups left untried: independently tuning the short side, or testing over an actual bear stretch instead of the 11-mostly-bull-year full history. Extended `scripts/bear_market_test.py` (already built for item 32) with a new `LONG_SHORT_PAIRS` comparison: for each of the 6 live combos' underlying (ticker, base family) pair, slices both the long-only and long/short (`_ls`) stored equity curves to the same 3 bear windows and compares them directly — reused the already-stored `backtest_runs` rows from item 30's original full-universe long/short cycle, no new backtests needed. (FXI is compared against its plain `bollinger` base, not the live `bollinger_regime` variant — long/short was only ever built for the 4 base families, regime-gating has no `_ls` counterpart.)
    - **Result: long/short wins clearly in 14 of 18 comparisons, ties in 2 (the short side simply never fired that window), and loses in only 2 — both the same pair, `TIP/bollinger`.** The wins are often large, not marginal: `QQQ/breakout` in the COVID crash went from -6.3% long-only (Sharpe -6.22) to **+5.8%** long/short (Sharpe +1.93); same pattern in the 2022 grind (-1.5% vs +6.6%). This holds up broadly across all 4 strategy families and 6 tickers, not just one lucky pair.
    - **This directly resolves the open question, in long/short's favor, specifically for bad markets**: the earlier negative full-history result wasn't wrong, it was measuring the wrong thing — betting against an 11-year uptrend loses, exactly as found, but that's a completely different question from "does shorting help during an actual downturn." It clearly does, in the large majority of tested cases.
    - **Honest caveats, not glossed over**: (1) `TIP/bollinger` is a consistent, real exception — worth remembering, not hand-waved as a fluke; low-volatility bond ETFs may not have the kind of directional moves this short-side logic is built to catch. (2) Still only 3 windows inside the same history already used to build and validate these strategies, same limitation item 32 already flagged for the long-only bear test — not the same rigor as true out-of-sample validation. (3) This is a research-only finding: `paper_trading.py`'s hard `NotImplementedError` guard on `allow_short=True` strategies (item 30) is unchanged — live short-selling support doesn't exist and wasn't built here; deploying on this finding would require that real build plus a deliberate human decision, not something that follows automatically from a good backtest result.

37. **Live short-selling support built for paper trading — the follow-up item 36 called for (2026-07-23)** — asked directly to build the capability item 36's result made worth having: `paper_trading.py`'s `check_and_update()` had a hard `NotImplementedError` on any `allow_short=True` strategy specifically because this didn't exist yet.
    - **Schema**: `paper_trade_events` gained one new `direction` column (`'long'`/`'short'`), not new event-type strings — `reconstruct_equity_curve()`'s existing entry/exit alternation check already validates "opens then closes then opens..." regardless of direction, so this stayed additive; existing rows backfilled to `'long'` explicitly rather than trusting `ADD COLUMN...DEFAULT` to do it reliably.
    - **P&L math**: one shared `_price_return()` helper in `store/paper.py` (short = `1 - price/entry_price`, long = `price/entry_price - 1`, unchanged), used in the 3 places the old long-only formula was duplicated (`paper_equity_summary()`'s realized loop, and `reconstruct_equity_curve()`'s realized-on-close and unrealized-mark-to-market spots) — one place to be right instead of three.
    - **`check_and_update()`**: rebuilt as one unified flat/long/short state machine rather than a parallel long/short code path — the key insight being that `Strategy.generate_short_signals()`'s base-class default already returns two all-`False` Series for every strategy that hasn't overridden it, so calling it *unconditionally* (removing the `allow_short` check entirely) makes every existing long-only combo provably identical to its old behavior by construction, not by a second path someone has to remember to keep in sync. A same-bar flip (long exit + short entry both firing) is deferred to the next day's check rather than acted on immediately — mirrors `run_backtest()`'s own one-bar-deferral invariant (always at least one flat bar between a long and a short), just achieved through the natural next-day cadence instead of a `.shift()` trick, since there's no future bar to shift into within one live invocation.
    - **Verified thoroughly, not just spot-checked**: isolated scratch-DB tests proved the P&L sign is actually right (short + price falls = gain, short + price rises = loss, long unchanged) and that the unrealized mark-to-market branch is correct while a short is still open; a real day-by-day walk-forward through the 2022 bear market on `QQQ/breakout_ls` produced 7 clean round-trips (+7.07% cumulative, matching item 36's finding) with every long/short flip naturally landing a day later, never the same day; one real manual invocation against the live database (`python scripts/paper_trade.py QQQ --strategy breakout_ls`) ran clean; `blend_paper_trading.py` confirmed unaffected; `git diff` confirmed `daily_paper_trade.sh`/`CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` untouched.
    - **Explicit scope boundary, unchanged governance**: this ships the *capability* only — no `_ls` combo (not even the standout `QQQ/breakout_ls`) was added to `daily_paper_trade.sh`/`CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`. Deploying one live is a separate, later, explicit decision.

38. **`QQQ/breakout_ls` added to live paper trading — a deliberate, honestly-labeled exception, not a promoted strategy (2026-07-23)** — asked directly whether anything needed to be added to paper trading now that short-selling support exists; recommended checking first rather than assuming. Confirmed via `evaluate_promotion()`: every single `_ls` combo across the full 32-ticker universe is `HOLD`, no exceptions — `QQQ/breakout_ls` itself has `worst_oos_sharpe=-0.84` and `mean_dsr=0.39` (below the 0.5 threshold). This is a genuine, real tension with every other combo currently in `daily_paper_trade.sh`, all of which got there by clearing that same bar.
    - **The case for adding it anyway, made explicitly rather than assumed**: paper trading has zero real cost (fake money, fully reversible), and the standard full-history bar measures something different from what item 36's bear-market re-test actually tested — average performance across 11 mostly-bull years (where betting against the trend predictably costs return) versus "does this help specifically during a real downturn" (where it won 14 of 18 head-to-head comparisons, often by a wide margin). The failed bar isn't an unexplained red flag; it's evidence the bar is answering a different question than the one this combo is actually good at.
    - **Added with the asterisk stated everywhere it appears**, not quietly folded in as if it were equivalent to the other six: `daily_paper_trade.sh` and `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` (`research_cycle.py`) both carry an explicit comment stating it is NOT a PROMOTE-verdict combo and explaining exactly why it's tracked anyway, referencing this item. `paper_trade.py`'s own existing "not currently PROMOTEd" warning fires for it daily, as expected — not a sign of a problem.
    - **Verified end to end**: ran `scripts/daily_paper_trade.sh` directly (not just the individual script) — all 7 combos now exit 0 cleanly, including the new one showing its expected warning and a clean `HOLD` on today's flat bar. Now 7 combos live: the original 6 plus this one clearly-labeled exception.

39. **"Copy Congress's trades" backtested — a direct answer to the Nancy Pelosi question, with two real methodology bugs caught and fixed along the way (2026-07-23)** — asked whether Congress members really "beat the market" and whether their disclosed trades could just be copied. Rather than answer from priors, built a real backtest. Used Plan Mode (the research + design was substantial enough to warrant it, same threshold as item 37).
    - **Data source, checked directly rather than assumed**: the House's free disclosure source (housestockwatcher.com) is dead in 2026 — DNS doesn't resolve, its backing repo hasn't updated since mid-2025 — so Pelosi specifically (a House member) isn't reachable through any free source today. Per the user's explicit choice, this covers the Senate only (still free, still live: `timothycarambat/senate-stock-watcher-data` on GitHub); House coverage is a clearly-labeled fast-follow if the user later gets a free-tier Financial Modeling Prep API key.
    - **Bug #1, HTML-wrapped tickers**: the source data wraps most tickers in a Yahoo Finance link (`<a href="...">DNKN</a>`) — caught immediately in the first smoke test (tickers didn't match anything in `ohlcv_daily`), fixed with a small regex extractor before anything downstream used the corrupted values.
    - **Bug #2, the more serious one — wrong source file entirely**: the repo's `aggregate/all_transactions_for_senators.json` looked right (has both `transaction_date` and `date_recieved`/disclosure date, unlike the flatter `all_transactions.json`), but turned out to hold only *one representative filing per senator* (43 `ptr_link`s total for 42 senators), not their real filing history — meaning every one of a senator's trades across a decade came back tagged with a single disclosure date. This surfaced as an extreme, telling artifact: Senator Perdue's ~2000 trades all showed the *same* disclosure date, collapsing every "copy" attempt into a same-day, zero-return round trip. Investigated rather than dismissed as noise — checked whether this was Perdue-specific (it wasn't; every senator with >100 trades showed exactly 1 distinct disclosure date) — and traced it to the aggregate file's structure. Fixed by switching to the repo's ~1055 individual daily `transaction_report_for_MM_DD_YYYY.json` files (one real filing batch per file, fetched concurrently), which restored genuine per-filing disclosure dates (Perdue's trades now spread across 55 real dates, not 1).
    - **Bug #3, sparse equity-curve sampling**: the first working version only marked positions to market on days a trade actually fired, producing an obviously-fake result (Sharpe +33.6, 0% max drawdown) once real data was run through it. Fixed by marking every open position against every trading day (using SPY's calendar as the master trading-day index, each ticker's price forward-filled onto it), which is what actually let real drawdowns show up.
    - **Design, once the data was trustworthy**: not built as a `Strategy` subclass — that interface is for dense, single-ticker signals, and copying a senator is the opposite shape (a sparse, event-driven, cross-ticker portfolio). New `congress_copy.py` runs an explicit day-by-day, capital-constrained cash simulation instead: a fixed 5%-of-current-portfolio-value allocation per new copied Purchase, drawn from actually-available cash (skipped if there isn't enough — a real capital constraint, not free-floating leverage), FIFO-matched to that member+ticker's next disclosed Sale, with unsold positions marked-to-market through the present. Every real entry/exit is dated from the trade's **disclosure date**, never the transaction date — a member can legally wait up to 45 days to report, so backtesting on the transaction date would be lookahead bias. A labeled, explicitly non-tradeable diagnostic mode also runs the same simulation on the transaction date, to show how much of a member's reported edge is pre-disclosure information a real copier could never have captured.
    - **Verified, not just run once**: one real multi-day trade (Perdue's SYY position, held 271 days, entry $37.09 → exit $45.03) hand-computed against the module's output and matched to the cent; `git diff` confirmed `research_cycle.py`/`daily_paper_trade.sh`/`promotion.py`/`registry.py` untouched, since this is a one-off research script, not a live-trading candidate.
    - **Real results**: for Senator Perdue individually (the most active trader in the dataset, 81 real closed trades after the fixes), copying underperformed buy-and-hold SPY over the same active window (2015-2026: +148.6% vs +256.7%, Sharpe 0.57 vs 0.88). Combined across every senator in the dataset, the gap was much narrower (+233.5% vs +264.3%, Sharpe 0.81 vs 0.87) — copying broadly is closer to competitive than copying one person, though still not ahead. In the combined run, the non-tradeable transaction-date diagnostic clearly beat the real disclosure-date version (+385.9% vs +233.5%) — consistent with the "much of the edge is pre-disclosure timing" thesis. Perdue's individual diagnostic run went the other way (worse, not better) — flagged honestly as a real, non-obvious artifact of the capital-constrained FIFO model's path-dependency (which of ~15-20 concurrent slots gets filled shifts with entry timing), not smoothed over to fit the expected story.
    - **Data caveat, stated plainly**: this dataset's disclosures run out around March 2021 — it's a historical snapshot, not a live feed, so this answers "how would copying have worked historically," not "what would copying do starting today."
    - **Explicit scope boundaries**: not wired into `research_cycle.py`, `daily_paper_trade.sh`, or `promotion.py` — this is a one-off research question, not a candidate for live paper trading.

40. **House disclosure collection added — forward-only, a real limitation found by testing the actual API rather than trusting the docs (2026-07-23)** — asked to add a free Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) key for House/Pelosi coverage, following up on item 39's stated gap. Could not sign up for the account itself (account creation is a user action) — the user created the free account and provided the key, which is stored locally as `.env` (already gitignored, same convention as `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, never committed).
    - **Real finding, confirmed directly against the live API rather than assumed from FMP's docs (which block anonymous fetches)**: the free tier's `house-latest` endpoint works, but only returns the ~100 most recent House disclosures *platform-wide* (roughly the last 10 days) — `page` beyond 0, and both by-name and by-symbol search endpoints, all return "Premium Query Parameter" / 404 on this tier. This means, unlike the Senate source, **there is no way to get a House member's historical trades on the free tier at all** — not a partial limitation, a hard wall. Legacy `/api/v4/` endpoints (mentioned in older documentation) are confirmed dead too ("Legacy Endpoint... no longer supported... prior August 31, 2025").
    - **Asked the user how to proceed rather than silently building something that couldn't deliver what was originally wanted**: given the free tier can't backfill Pelosi's past, the choice was build-forward-only vs. skip entirely. User chose forward-only.
    - **Built**: `fetch_house_disclosures_latest()` in `data/congress_trades.py` (FMP's fields arrive already clean — plain tickers, a real per-transaction `disclosureDate` — neither of the two Senate-side bugs from item 39 apply here) and `scripts/collect_house_disclosures.py`, meant to be run periodically. Storage reuses the exact same `congress_trades` table and composite dedupe key as the Senate data, just with `chamber='house'`.
    - **Verified end to end against the real key**: first run stored 91 real House disclosures (16 distinct members); confirmed `congress_copy_backtest.py` needed *zero* code changes to pick up House members — `--member` matching was already chamber-agnostic by design from item 39's build. No Pelosi rows yet (she hasn't disclosed anything in the current ~10-day window) — expected, not a bug.
    - **Honest limitation, stated plainly everywhere this is documented**: this only starts accumulating real House history from whenever it's actually run going forward — there's no way to backtest a House member's past the way item 39 did for the Senate, on this tier. Revisiting with a paid FMP plan is a possible future step, not decided.
    - **Scheduled the same day, once asked**: `scripts/daily_house_disclosures.sh` + `scheduling/com.quantplatform.housedisclosures.plist`. The one real wrinkle: launchd's minimal environment doesn't have `FMP_API_KEY` and won't pick it up from the user's shell profile either, so the wrapper script explicitly sources `.env` itself before running the collector — verified with two consecutive real `launchctl start` triggers (not just a manual run), the second showing `+0 new` since the feed had already stabilized, confirming both the scheduling and the dedupe-on-insert idempotency work correctly end to end.
    - **Asked whether once a day was the right cadence, and it wasn't** — checked the real ingested data rather than guessing: daily House disclosure volume swings a lot (1 to 37 in one recent week), but the free tier's window is a flat 100-most-recent-platform-wide, not 100/day. A few busy days back to back could push a disclosure out of that window before the next daily check, and there's no historical lookup to recover it afterward. Changed to every 4 hours (00/04/08/12/16/20, avoiding the 06:00/17:00 collisions), re-verified with a real `launchctl start`, then — after confirming the 250-calls/day quota has essentially no binding constraint at this volume — bumped again to **hourly** (24 calls/day) on request. Hourly does now overlap 06:00 and 17:00 (the DB backup and paper-trading jobs), unlike the every-4-hours version which deliberately dodged that; harmless, since all three launchd jobs are fully independent (different scripts, log files, and DB tables/files, no shared locking). Re-verified clean with another real trigger after this last change too.

41. **3 new strategy families added by hand, deliberately chosen for genuinely different mechanisms, not more variations on the same 4 (2026-07-23)** — asked to add more strategies after worrying the platform's "only 4 ideas" was limiting long-term profitability. Corrected the premise first: it's 4 base ideas &times; 3 flavors (plain/regime-gated/long-short) = 12 variants &times; 32 tickers = 384 combinations already tried, not 4. Explained why "just add a ton more" isn't free: DSR's multiple-testing correction scales with how many things are tried, so a pile of new strategies raises the bar for *everything*, including what's already running, unless the new ideas are genuinely different mechanisms rather than correlated near-duplicates. Chose to hand-design a small, deliberate batch (not the AI Research Agent, since the user doesn't have an Anthropic API key set up yet) — each one picked specifically to use a different *information source* or *mechanism* from the 4 existing families (which are all close-price/high-low technical indicators):
    - **`volume_breakout`** — same channel-breakout shape as the existing Donchian breakout, but a new high only counts if it comes with unusually high trading volume behind it. First family to use the volume column at all. Result: `HOLD` on every one of the 32 tickers, long-only and long/short versions both — an honest negative, not forced into the writeup as a win.
    - **`turn_of_month`** — a well-documented calendar anomaly (returns cluster around month boundaries: pension/payroll flows, fund rebalancing, month-end reporting). No price indicator at all — the most orthogonal strategy in the registry by design. **Result: genuinely `PROMOTE`d on 2 tickers, QQQ and XLF** — passed all 5 gates standalone *and* survived the correlation gate (not flagged as redundant with anything already running). Real, new find. No long/short variant built for this one **by choice**: there's no comparably evidence-backed "middle of the month" anomaly to bet against, and inventing a mirror-image short leg just for symmetry would be an arbitrary label, not a real effect.
    - **`gap_fade`** — bets on a partial reversion after a stock opens sharply below (or above, for the short side) where it closed the day before; exits on a fixed holding period rather than a price condition, both firsts for this registry. Long-only result: `HOLD` everywhere, another honest negative.
    - **Real bug caught by testing, not assumed**: the long/short version of `gap_fade` failed on 28 of the 32 tickers with `vectorbt`'s "SizeType.Percent does not support position reversal" error. Investigated rather than patched around — confirmed directly (walked SPY's signals day by day) that this strategy's fixed-holding-period exit breaks an invariant every other long/short family relies on: their long and short trigger conditions are mutually exclusive *by construction* (e.g. Donchian's "above the channel" and "below the channel" can never both be true), so `run_backtest()`'s existing same-bar collision handling is enough. `gap_fade`'s time-based exit has no such guarantee — a fresh short trigger can genuinely fire while an earlier long position is still inside its holding window, a real multi-day overlap (8 separate days found on SPY alone), not just a same-bar edge case. Fixing this properly would mean force-closing on an opposite trigger, which would quietly change what the strategy actually *is* — so the long/short variant was removed rather than shipped broken or silently redesigned. Stray DB rows from the 4 tickers that happened not to hit the bug before removal were deleted, not left as confusing leftover data for a strategy that no longer exists in the codebase.
    - **Total promoted combos**: 16 in the database, one of which (`cand_75f9b5c51f`) is leftover fixture/test data from the Research Agent's earlier safety-layer verification runs (item 24), not a real strategy family — so **15 real promoted combos** now, up from 13, both new ones from `turn_of_month`. Neither QQQ nor XLF's `turn_of_month` promotion has been evaluated for paper trading yet — that's a separate, later decision, same as every other new promotion this project has ever produced.

42. **2 genuinely more ambitious ideas: cross-sectional momentum rotation + relative-value pairs trading — both honest negatives, one with a real near-miss worth remembering (2026-07-23)** — asked for more aggressive, "outside the box" strategies aimed at outperforming the S&P 500. Set an honest expectation up front, stated to the user before building anything: no strategy here, or anywhere, can be promised to beat buy-and-hold SPY, especially over an 11-year bull run — every strategy tested so far (all 15 promoted combos included) makes *less* raw return than just holding the index. Used Plan Mode given the real architectural decision involved (neither idea fits the single-ticker `Strategy` interface). Both ideas look *across* tickers instead of at one ticker's own price history in isolation — the actual "outside the box" step, not just a new indicator on the same shape.
    - **Cross-sectional momentum rotation** (`momentum_rotation.py` + `scripts/momentum_rotation_backtest.py`) — ranks all 32 tickers against each other every month, holds only the strongest 3 (of 9 tried lookback/top-k combos), drops the rest. A real, more concentrated bet than anything else here (picking winners instead of diversifying). Genuinely can't reuse `walk_forward()`/DSR (built for one `Strategy` on one ticker) — built its own, explicitly *lighter* validation instead: the (lookback, top_k) combo is chosen using only the first 70% of history, and every reported number comes only from the untouched final 30%. **Verified this wasn't peeking**: computed what the "best" combo would have been using full-history hindsight vs. train-only — both picked the same combo (lookback=3 months, top_k=3), a reassuring consistency signal rather than a red flag. Hand-verified one real month's actual top-5 ranking (June 2024) against real closing prices — silver's genuine +16.8% quarterly surge correctly ranked #1. **Result, honest**: lost to SPY buy-and-hold on the held-out test period on all three measures (return +55.2% vs +84.1%, Sharpe 0.74 vs 1.50, and — notably — a *worse* drawdown too, -26.6% vs -19.0%). A concentrated top-3 bet is real extra risk, not free extra return.
    - **Relative-value pairs trading** (`pairs.py` + `scripts/pairs_trade_backtest.py`) — a cleaner design than momentum rotation: a dollar-neutral long/short spread between two related real tickers reduces to one synthetic price series, which then runs through the **exact same, unmodified** `run_backtest()`/`walk_forward()`/`promotion.evaluate_criteria()` pipeline every single-ticker strategy uses — full DSR rigor, zero new validation code, because the underlying math genuinely didn't need any. Reused the existing Bollinger/RSI (and their `_ls` variants) directly — zero new strategy code either. 3 real, motivated pairs tested: XLE/USO (energy stocks vs. oil), QQQ/SPY (tech concentration vs. broad market), GLD/SLV (the classic gold-silver ratio) — 12 combos total. Hand-verified the spread construction against real XLE/USO prices before trusting any result. **Result: 0 of 12 promoted**, but not uniformly bad — `QQQ_SPY_PAIR` with both `bollinger` and `rsi` cleared 4 of the 5 gates cleanly (worst-OOS-Sharpe 1.89 and 1.15 respectively, both genuinely strong; DSR comfortably above threshold) and failed on exactly one: `sufficient_oos_evidence` — the mean-reversion signal on this particular pair's spread simply didn't fire often enough within its walk-forward windows to count as enough real evidence, a frequency problem, not a performance problem. `GLD_SLV_PAIR` and `XLE_USO_PAIR`, by contrast, failed on genuine quality grounds (worst OOS Sharpe around -2 to -2.5 across the board) — a real negative, not a near-miss.
    - **Verified thoroughly**: `git diff` confirmed `registry.py`, `promotion.py`, `backtest/run.py`, `validation/walk_forward.py`, `research_cycle.py`, and `daily_paper_trade.sh` all untouched — both ideas are purely additive. Neither is wired into paper trading or the registry; both are one-off research questions, same boundary as every prior addition this session.

43. **Database lock-conflict retry added to `db.py`'s `connect()` — a real failure caught the same day it happened, not a theoretical fix (2026-07-23)** — asked whether the 5pm paper-trading job actually fired; checking the real log showed it fired exactly on schedule (17:00:05), but its very first check (`QQQ/sma`) failed with `IOException: Could not set lock on file...` — an interactive analysis session (this same conversation, running the momentum-rotation/pairs-trading backtests) happened to be touching the database at that exact moment. DuckDB only allows one process to hold the file open at a time; a second connection attempt during that window fails outright rather than waiting. Manually re-ran the missed check immediately (still flat/HOLD, no real impact).
    - User's framing was "there should be a separate agent so it doesn't interfere" — corrected gently: the 5pm job already runs as a fully independent OS-scheduled process via `launchd`, not as part of any chat session; the actual problem is narrower and unrelated to *which* process asks, it's that DuckDB's file lock is exclusive regardless of who's asking.
    - **Fix**: `db.py`'s shared `connect()` (used by every store module — results, paper, candidates, congress_trades, congress_copy, momentum_rotation — so one fix covers all of them) now retries on `duckdb.IOException` with backoff (1s, 2s, 4s, 8s — ~15s worst case) before giving up for real, since these locks are normally held only briefly. Deliberately still raises the original error if the lock genuinely never clears, rather than silently retrying forever or swallowing a real failure.
    - **Verified with a real simulated conflict, not just reasoning about it**: held a lock open in one process for 3 seconds while a second process attempted to connect at the same moment — confirmed the second connection printed two retry messages (1s, 2s) and succeeded the instant the lock released, at the 3-second mark, matching exactly the failure mode seen in today's real log.

44. **Universe expansion: from 32 ETFs to ~5,900 tickers across stocks, crypto, and futures — IN PROGRESS, full run underway (2026-07-23)** — asked to test all strategies on individual stocks (Google, Amazon, Tesla, "the list goes on and on"), at "thousands and thousands" of tickers; then, mid-plan, asked to add crypto and futures too, reasoning that more tickers tested means more chances to find a real edge. Used Plan Mode given the real scope (new data sourcing, ingestion resilience, and execution-speed work across three asset classes). Set an honest expectation up front, since the three asset classes are not comparable in size: stocks genuinely reach thousands, crypto's real (Yahoo-covered, liquid) ceiling is a few hundred not thousands, and futures top out around 20 distinct liquid contracts, full stop.
    - **Built `src/quant_platform/data/universe.py`**: `fetch_stock_universe()` pulls NASDAQ Trader's free, no-auth, daily-updated listing files (`nasdaqlisted.txt`/`otherlisted.txt`), filters out ETFs/test-issues/warrants/rights/units/preferreds by both flag columns and security-name text matching, and converts NYSE's dotted share-class convention to Yahoo's dashed one (`BRK.A` → `BRK-A`). **Confirmed directly, not assumed**: 5,649 real common stocks survive the filter, including GOOGL/AMZN/TSLA/AAPL/MSFT/BRK-A/BRK-B. `fetch_crypto_universe()` pulls CoinGecko's free market-cap-ranked list, excludes stablecoins explicitly (zero volatility = nothing for a strategy to find), and — the important step — actually probes every candidate against Yahoo Finance rather than trusting the mapping; **confirmed the honest ceiling this way**: only 14 of the top 20 tokens by market cap, and 159 of the top 300, actually have real Yahoo data. `FUTURES_UNIVERSE` is a small hand-written constant (19 major contracts across equity indices, rates, energy, metals, ags, currencies) — deliberately not "fetched," since a live source would be over-engineering for a list this size and this static.
    - **Made ingestion resilient at scale** (`data/fetch.py`): the old `fetch_and_store()` crashed the whole batch on the first bad ticker — fine for a hand-typed 32-ticker list, not fine once failures (delisted symbols, no history) are a certainty across thousands. Rewrote around a new `fetch_batch()` that requests 100 tickers per `yf.download()` call instead of one call per ticker (cuts network round-trips ~100x) and returns `{"succeeded": [...], "failed": [...]}` instead of raising. `paper_trade.py`'s single-ticker call site was deliberately kept strict — a failed refresh there now raises loud rather than silently paper-trading on stale data, since one ticker failing out of one is a different situation than one failing out of thousands.
    - **Parallelized the backtest/walk-forward loop** (`research_cycle.py`): added a `--workers N` option (`multiprocessing.Pool`, default 1 = old serial behavior, unchanged) since each ticker's 16-strategy run is fully independent of every other ticker's. No change to the 16 strategies, the grids, or the promotion logic — execution-strategy change only.
    - **Validated on an 83-ticker sample (50 stocks + 14 verified crypto + 19 futures) before committing to the full run, per the plan's staged rollout** — and this surfaced two real, concrete findings, not just a clean pass:
      - **A genuine scaling bug in `db.py`'s lock-retry logic, found and fixed**: item 43's retry-with-backoff (5 attempts, ~15s budget) was tuned for light 2-way contention (one interactive session + one scheduled job). Under real 8-way parallel writes across 1,328 combos, 15 writes exhausted that budget and were silently dropped. Fixed by raising the budget (10 attempts, 0.5–5s exponential backoff) and adding jitter so multiple workers backed off from a collision don't re-collide in lockstep. **Re-ran the identical batch after the fix**: lock failures dropped from 15/1,328 to 1/1,328 — confirmed by re-running, not just reasoned about.
      - **A known, pre-existing engine limitation, exposed more at individual-stock scale, deliberately NOT patched**: 27 of 1,328 combos hit the same `vectorbt` "`SizeType.Percent does not support position reversal`" error that caused `gap_fade_ls` to be removed entirely back in item 41 — except this time it's hitting the *existing* long/short strategies (`breakout_ls`, `volume_breakout_ls`, `sma_ls`, `bollinger_ls`, `rsi_ls`) on individual stocks, which never triggered it across the calmer 32-ETF universe. Individual stocks are choppier, so they hit this edge case more often. A separate 6 combos hit a `order.price must be finite and greater than 0` data-quality edge case (a bad/zero price on some day for a specific ticker). Both fail safely — the existing per-combo try/except logs and skips them, so no bad data is recorded and nothing crashes — but it means some real (ticker, strategy) combos across the full universe simply won't have results, an honest gap flagged here rather than silently patched around (fixing the underlying `vectorbt` limitation is a separate, real follow-up, out of scope for a universe-expansion pass).
    - **Real timing measured, not estimated in advance**: the 83-ticker/16-strategy/1,328-combo validation batch took 276s with 8 parallel workers (~3.33s per ticker for its full 16-strategy sweep) — a huge improvement over the original serial 15s/ticker estimate. Projected onto the full ~5,858-ticker combined universe (5,649 stocks + 159 verified crypto + 19 futures + the existing 32 ETFs): **~5.4 hours of continuous compute** for the backtest/walk-forward phase alone, plus data ingestion time on top. Stated to the user before starting, per the plan's explicit staging step.
    - **Full run kicked off as a background job** immediately after validation passed (ingest all ~5,858 tickers, then `research_cycle.py --workers 8` across the whole set) — as of this note, ~60% through (56,120/93,712 combos), on pace, failure rate holding steady in the same three known categories. This item will be updated with final results (tickers actually fetched vs. failed, combos tested, PROMOTE count per asset class) once it completes.
    - **Honest caveat carried into the results, stated up front and not glossed over once results land**: DSR corrects for the small in-strategy parameter search (8 grid points) on each individual combo, but nothing here corrects for the *global* multiple-comparisons problem of testing 16 strategies across thousands of tickers — tens of thousands of independent trials will produce more chance PROMOTEs in absolute terms than the 32-ticker universe did, even at an unchanged, well-calibrated per-trial false-positive rate. The correlation gate helps but doesn't fully solve this. Any PROMOTE from this run should be read with that in mind, not treated as "thousands of validated edges."
    - **Scope boundaries held, same as every prior expansion**: nothing here touches `daily_paper_trade.sh`/`CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`/the registry — this is a data/compute question, not a deployment decision.

45. **Research Agent switched to a free, local model (Hermes3 via Ollama), running on a recurring schedule — done, verified, live (2026-07-23)** — follow-up to item 44's pending note: the user installed Ollama (a free program that runs an AI model locally, no per-call cost) and asked to get the Research Agent running on it "24/7." Used Plan Mode given the real scope (new LLM-provider routing plus a new scheduled job). Gave an honest gut-check before building anything, already stated to the user: since the underlying market data only updates once a day, a literal nonstop tight loop wouldn't find anything new most of the time — it would just burn CPU for no benefit and could flood the human-review pile faster than it's reviewable. Built a **recurring scheduled job** instead (same shape as every other automation here), not a tight loop — the free/local model removes the cost reason to run rarely, but not the "nothing new to look at faster than daily" reason.
    - **Ollama itself was installed by the user, not scripted** — persistent background software needs the Mac's own install/security prompts, which can't be clicked through from here. Everything downstream (pulling the model, wiring code, scheduling) was done directly once Ollama was confirmed running.
    - **`llm_client.py` got a new local-model path, purely additive** — `generate_candidate()`/`secondary_review()`/`triage_summary()` each now branch: a `model` in a new `LOCAL_MODELS` set (`{"hermes3"}`) routes to a new `_ollama_chat()` helper (plain `requests.post` against Ollama's local API, matching this project's existing plain-`requests` convention, no new SDK dependency) instead of the Anthropic client. **Confirmed no changes needed anywhere else** — `prompts.py`'s prompt-building/response-parsing were already fully provider-agnostic (they only ever dealt in prompt strings and raw response text), and `scripts/research_agent.py` already accepted `--model` as a plain CLI arg. `estimated_cost_usd` reports real `0.0` for local calls, not a placeholder.
    - **Real finding from testing against the actual model, not assumed**: across 4 real generation attempts, Hermes3's strict "respond with ONLY this JSON shape" compliance was roughly 50/50 — 2 clean parses, 2 JSON syntax errors. Even the one that parsed cleanly and reached the lint stage had a real bug in its generated Python (an unclosed bracket). None of this is a problem *for the system* — every one of these was caught and rejected exactly where it should have been (parse failure recorded as `rejected_parse`, the bad code caught at `rejected_lint`), nothing bad ever got close to running unchecked. It does mean Hermes's real usable-candidate yield will run lower than Claude's did — flagged honestly rather than glossed over, and consistent with the caveat already given: this is a cost/availability swap, not a claimed quality upgrade.
    - **A real, pre-existing limitation of `research_agent.py` found while wiring this up**: `--n-candidates` doesn't actually control how many candidates get generated — the code always makes exactly one `generate_candidate()` call and `parse_llm_response()` only ever parses a single JSON object, regardless of the flag's value. Not something introduced or fixed here (out of scope for a provider swap) — just discovered and worked around: the scheduled job is set to `--n-candidates 1` to match what actually happens, and real throughput is controlled by how *often* the job runs, not by that flag. A real follow-up if higher per-run volume is ever wanted.
    - **Built `scripts/daily_research_agent.sh`** (mirrors `daily_house_disclosures.sh`'s shape): picks one random ticker from whatever's already stored in `ohlcv_daily` each run (so repeated runs actually screen different slices of the now much-larger universe, not always the same ticker), runs `research_agent.py --model hermes3 --n-candidates 1 --skip-llm-secondary-review`. Secondary review (a second LLM call per candidate) is off by default in the scheduled job to keep runs bounded; still available for a manual run.
    - **`scheduling/com.quantplatform.researchagent.plist`, every 4 hours (6 runs/day)** — a deliberate middle ground, not the old cost-driven "weekly/on-demand" cadence and not a tight loop either. Installed to `~/Library/LaunchAgents/`, `RunAtLoad=false` (first run should be a deliberate watched test, not a surprise on install) — **verified with a real `launchctl start` trigger**: fired successfully, picked ticker `JGH`, generated a candidate via Hermes3, correctly rejected it at lint (a real syntax error in the generated code), exit code 0. The scheduled job's own DB writes correctly retried through a lock conflict with the still-running universe-expansion backtest (item 44) at the same moment — real, live proof `db.py`'s retry-with-backoff (item 44's fix) works under genuine concurrent load from two independent automations, not just the validation batch that motivated it.
    - **Scope boundaries held, same as every addition to this system**: zero changes to `registry.py`, `daily_paper_trade.sh`, `promotion.py`, `backtest/run.py`, `validation/walk_forward.py`, `safety_lint.py`, `sandbox.py`, or `stability.py` — a Hermes-authored candidate clears (or fails) the *exact same* gauntlet as a hand-written or Claude-authored one, and still always lands on `pending_human_review` (or a rejection). Nothing here auto-promotes anything, regardless of generation source or how often the job fires. No promise this finds a strategy that beats the S&P 500 — same honest caveat given for every "more aggressive" ask this session.
    - **Superseded the same night, see item 46** — the every-4-hours cadence here was replaced by a genuinely continuous loop after the user pushed back on it directly.

46. **Research Agent made smarter and genuinely continuous, replacing item 45's 4-hour schedule — done, verified (2026-07-23)** — the user pushed back on two things directly, both with real merit: (1) "Hermes is not as efficient as it could be," and (2) every-4-hours "limits ourselves on all the strategies that could be made in between." Investigated rather than just complying or just defending the earlier design.
    - **Real root cause found for the ~50% parse-failure rate from item 45, not just "small models are unreliable"**: confirmed directly that Ollama defaults to a 2,048-token context window regardless of what the model natively supports (Hermes3 supports 131,072) — a full strategy response (measured ~950 prompt tokens in, up to ~500-1000 out) was silently getting truncated mid-generation, which is exactly what produces things like an unclosed bracket. Fixed in `llm_client.py`'s `_ollama_chat()`: `"format": "json"` (Ollama's constrained-decoding mode, the actual fix — not just a hint) + `"options": {"num_ctx": 8192, "temperature": 0.3}`. **Real before/after measured**: parse success went from ~50% to **5/5 (100%)** across a fresh test batch; lint pass rate rose from 0/4 to **3/5**. `format: "json"` had to be made conditional (a new `json_mode` parameter, default off) rather than global — `secondary_review()`/`triage_summary()` want free-form prose back, and forcing JSON on those would have broken them; caught this before it shipped, not after.
    - **Added a real few-shot example to the generation prompt** (`prompts.py`): moved `research_agent.py`'s existing `_TRIVIAL_SOURCE` fixture (`RangeCompressionStrategy`, a genuinely correct, house-rules-compliant strategy that was already sitting unused as a pipeline self-test) into `prompts.py` as the canonical example, shown to the model as "structure to copy, not the idea." A real, well-established fix for the kind of mistakes seen (missing imports, a banned Python for-loop, one response that literally copied the prompt's `<value>` placeholder text into real code).
    - **On cadence — corrected my own earlier reasoning, not just the user's**: item 45's "every 4 hours" conflated two different things — market data only updating daily (true, and a real limit on re-analyzing *prices* more often) with how often generating new strategy *ideas* helps (false constraint — a fresh LLM sample against the same stored data is a new idea, not a stale re-read). The user's pushback was correct. Replaced the fixed-interval schedule entirely with a **continuous self-pacing loop** (`scripts/research_agent_loop.sh`, a `while true` that finishes one candidate and immediately starts the next) — the real, honest ceiling on frequency is how long each candidate's generation + safety pipeline actually takes (~1-2 minutes, measured directly), not a policy choice. `scheduling/com.quantplatform.researchagent.plist` rewritten from `StartCalendarInterval` to `RunAtLoad=true` + `KeepAlive=true` (restarts if it ever crashes) + `Nice=10` (uses otherwise-idle CPU without fighting higher-priority work like paper trading or a research cycle for it — the honest way to keep "runs constantly" without starving anything that matters more) + a `ThrottleInterval` so a crash-loop can't spin.
    - **Real bug caught during manual testing before the permanent version went live, not after**: `research_agent_loop.sh`'s random-ticker-selection query captured `db.connect()`'s lock-retry `print()` messages (which go to stdout, same as the real query result) directly into the `$TICKER` shell variable whenever the DB happened to be busy — confirmed two real test candidates got screened against a garbled multi-line string instead of a real ticker as a direct result. Fixed with `| tail -1` (the real ticker is always the last line printed); re-verified under real concurrent DB load (the universe-expansion backtest was still running) that ticker selection now stays clean every time.
    - **Left an open, explicitly-flagged tradeoff rather than silently deciding it**: continuous generation can produce several hundred candidates a day, more than is realistically reviewable one by one. Told the user directly; they acknowledged it as a real, later, open question (e.g. a digest view) rather than something to solve immediately — not built here, not silently capped either.
    - **Also checked and ruled out, honestly**: a bigger/smarter local model (Hermes3 70b) needs ~40GB of memory; this Mac has 16GB. 8b is the real ceiling on this hardware, not a knob left untried.

47. **Live local dashboard to watch the Research Agent work and record approve/deny/hold decisions — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — asked, in plain language, to actually *see* the agent working: candidates getting generated, passing or failing each stage, and a way to act on ones reaching human review, rather than reading a log file or asking me to look things up.
    - **The data model for exactly this already existed and was unused**: `strategy_candidates`'s schema (`store/candidates.py`) already has `human_decision`/`human_notes`/`decided_at` columns, with the exact intended semantics already written in the module's own docstring from when it was first built — *"human_decision is bookkeeping only... never itself acting on that decision."* Zero schema changes needed; this was purely a visualization + one small write action on top of what was already there.
    - **Why this couldn't be a published Artifact**: an Artifact is a hosted, sandboxed page with no access to a file on this Mac, and this needs to read `data/market.duckdb` live. Built as a real local page instead: `scripts/candidate_dashboard.py`, a small Python stdlib `http.server` (no new dependency — checked what was installed first, only `plotly` was there; stdlib is enough for one HTML page and two tiny JSON endpoints, same "avoid a new dependency when the standard library covers it" reasoning as using plain `requests` for Ollama/CoinGecko/NASDAQ Trader earlier tonight) serving a self-refreshing page (polls every 4s) plus `GET /api/candidates` and `POST /api/decide`, both reusing `store/candidates.py`'s existing `recent_candidates()`/`update_candidate()` unchanged.
    - **Page shows**: a live pipeline funnel (generated / passed lint / passed sandbox / cleared promotion / stable / awaiting review / decided, all computed from one payload), a full activity feed (every candidate, expandable to full source/rationale/lint violations/backtest stats), and a dedicated review queue for anything `pending_human_review` with no decision yet — Approve / Hold / Deny buttons plus an optional note. **The Approve button says outright, in the UI itself, not just in a doc**: approving records the decision here, it does not by itself touch `strategies/registry.py` or paper trading — that stays a separate, deliberate step, matching how every strategy has ever been promoted in this project.
    - **Verified against real, live data, not a mockup**: opened the page while the Research Agent loop was actively running in the background and confirmed a real new candidate appeared without a manual refresh; recorded a real decision through the API, then independently confirmed via a direct DB query that `human_decision`/`human_notes`/`decided_at` actually landed. Found and fixed a real bug in the process: the page's 4-second auto-refresh was rebuilding the entire feed from scratch every poll, which would have silently wiped out an in-progress note or collapsed an opened detail card out from under the user — fixed by capturing open/focused/typed state before each re-render and restoring it after; re-verified with a real 5-second wait that both a typed note and an opened detail panel survive a live refresh.
    - **Confirmed no interference**: the dashboard, the Research Agent loop, and the still-running universe-expansion backtest (item 44) all ran simultaneously against the same DuckDB file with no new failures — `db.py`'s retry-with-backoff (item 44) continuing to earn its keep under real three-way concurrent load.

48. **Visual "ecosystem" dashboard: every scheduled agent as a moving, clickable robot — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — asked for something fun and visual, explicitly "cool and big": each real automated job as a little robot wandering a shared scene, clickable for live status.

Fixed by counting real combo invocations directly from `daily_paper_trade.sh` and reading the most recent action from that day's log instead. Honest limitation, expected and not chased as a bug: the three non-continuous agents run and finish in well under a second, so `launchctl list` essentially never catches them mid-execution — only Research Agent's `running: true` state is realistically observable, exactly as anticipated in the plan before building.
