49. **Full combined universe (stocks + crypto + futures) research cycle — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — the payoff run for item 44's universe-expansion work: 5,858 tickers (5,649 stocks, 159 crypto, 19 futures) × 16 strategy variants = 93,712 combos, run unattended overnight as a background job.
    - **Real numbers, not rounded**: ingestion fetched 11.9M rows with only 1 ticker failure (`SVA`, delisted) out of 5,858 — essentially a clean sweep. The backtest+walk-forward phase itself finished in ~8.2 hours (close to the estimate given mid-run), with a 1.9% per-combo skip rate — mostly thin-history edge cases, with ~0.2% specifically from DB lock contention among the 8 parallel workers exceeding the retry budget (a small, known, honestly-counted gap, not silently absorbed into the "done" count).
    - **1,236 PROMOTE verdicts out of 93,712 combos** (~1.3%): ~1,197 on stocks (875 distinct tickers), ~28 on crypto (24 distinct tickers), ~10 on futures (7 distinct tickers), plus ~16 re-evaluated combos on the original hand-picked ETF universe (e.g. `QQQ`, `XLP`, `FXI`) that got swept up because promotion evaluation runs over the whole DB history, not just this run's new tickers. **This must be read with the same multiple-testing skepticism flagged when this expansion was first planned, not as "1,236 validated edges"** — testing 16 strategies across thousands of tickers means tens of thousands of independent trials, and even a well-calibrated per-trial false-positive rate produces far more chance PROMOTEs in absolute terms than the old 32-ticker universe ever could. The correlation gate catches near-duplicate bets but does not fully solve this; a stricter universe-wide statistical bar remains a real, separate follow-up, not yet built.
    - **33 verdict changes since the prior cycle**, none of which demoted anything currently in daily paper trading (checked directly, not assumed) — the closest was `QQQ/breakout` flipping HOLD→PROMOTE while already marked `[in daily paper trading]`, an upgrade not a downgrade. One odd-looking entry, `QQQ/cand_75f9b5c51f: PROMOTE -> HOLD`, is expected and not a bug: LLM-generated research-agent candidates get backtested through the same tables as the hand-written strategies, so the promotion evaluator naturally picks up their candidate IDs alongside real strategy names.
    - **A real inefficiency found and named, not fixed mid-run**: after the backtest phase finished (~3:44 AM), the script's final "what changed since last cycle" pass took **over 6 more hours** to just print 93,712 comparison lines — traced to `promotion_snapshot_history()` (`store/results.py`) opening a brand-new DuckDB connection for *every single combo* in a tight serial loop, each one contending for the file lock against the still-running Research Agent loop and both dashboards' polling. Deliberately left unpatched until this run finished (didn't want to risk killing a job hours from completion) — **flagged here as a real fix worth making before the next full-universe run**: reuse one connection across that whole loop instead of opening 93,712 of them.
    - **Health checks after the run, not assumed clean**: DB file grew from 2.2GB to 2.6GB (reasonable for the added rows), 63GB free disk remaining, both live dashboards (`agent_ecosystem.py`, `candidate_dashboard.py`) still responsive throughout and after, Research Agent loop unaffected and still generating candidates the whole time.

50. **Strategy mailbox rebuilt as three real folders — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — the user asked for the ecosystem's mailbox to be organized into a Promoted folder, a Hold folder, and a folder for strategies "that don't work", and reported the Approve/Hold/Deny buttons had never worked at all (root-caused and fixed earlier the same day: a literal `Infinity` in a nested JSON-text column broke the browser's strict `JSON.parse`).
    - **Scope widened on purpose**: the old mailbox (item 48) only ever showed *undecided* `pending_human_review` candidates, grouped inline by promote/stable vs promote/unstable vs hold. The user's "folder" framing implied something broader — a filing system for every candidate the lab has ever produced, not just the open queue — so `agent_ecosystem.py` now serves `/api/mailbox` returning *all* `strategy_candidates` rows (223+ at time of writing), each tagged server-side with a `folder` via `_mailbox_folder()`: a human's own `human_decision` always wins over the pipeline's verdict when both exist (explicit `rejected`/`approved`/`hold` overrides the automated verdict); absent a human decision, pipeline failures (`rejected_parse`/`rejected_lint`/`rejected_sandbox`) file under "Doesn't work", `PROMOTE` verdicts file under "Promoted", everything else (including still-pending `HOLD`-verdict candidates) files under "Hold".
    - **UI is now a real folder picker**: opening the mailbox shows three folder tiles with counts (and a "N waiting" badge on any folder with undecided candidates needing a human call), click one to see its cards, a back button returns to the picker. Cards for already-decided or pipeline-rejected candidates show a read-only explanation instead of action buttons (e.g. "pipeline stage: failed at lint" / "You marked this **approved**") — only genuinely undecided, review-eligible candidates get live Approve/Hold/Deny buttons.
    - **Verified against the live server, not just code review**: restarted `agent_ecosystem.py`, hit `/api/mailbox` directly and cross-checked folder counts against a direct DuckDB query (matched: 1 promoted, 127 hold, 98 doesn't-work out of 226 total rows), then drove the actual page via JS (not clicking real buttons, to avoid repeating an earlier session's mistake of accidentally firing a real decision during manual testing) to confirm folder navigation, card rendering, and the presence of working action buttons on undecided cards.
    - This is a filing/UX improvement only — it does **not** solve the scale-of-review problem from item 49 (875+ newly-promoted stock tickers). It makes the *existing*, much-smaller LLM-candidate mailbox (~223 rows) easier to navigate; the giant universe-run PROMOTE list is a separate table (`backtest_runs`/promotion snapshots) not routed through this mailbox at all.

51. **Mailbox gets an AI-ranked "Top picks" list + a per-candidate S&P 500 comparison — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — the user doesn't have time to sort through the pile by hand and asked the AI to just surface which candidates look best, and which ones could beat the S&P 500.
    - **Reused the platform's own existing numbers, not a new prediction model** — there isn't one, and pretending otherwise would be dishonest. `agent_ecosystem.py`'s new `_enrich_with_research_ranking()` pulls each candidate's `deflated_sharpe_ratio`/`test_sharpe_ratio` from `walk_forward_windows` and re-derives `mean_dsr`/`worst_oos_sharpe` with the exact same finite-window filter `promotion.py`'s `_summarize_oos_windows()` already uses (a window with no test-side trades is excluded, not counted as bad). A composite `score` combines promotion verdict (+100), stability pass (+10), and mean DSR, so the pipeline's own rigor dominates the ranking rather than raw backtest return alone.
    - **The "beat the S&P 500" ask, answered honestly**: for each candidate with a real backtest, pulls the exact start/end dates of that backtest's own equity curve (`backtest_equity_curves`), then computes SPY's real buy-and-hold return over that *same* window from `ohlcv_daily` — the fairest available comparison, since it's the same stretch of history, not an arbitrary fixed period. Shown on every card as a badge (🏆 beat / under), not just on top picks, so this is visible everywhere in the mailbox.
    - **A real inefficiency deliberately avoided, not repeated**: `research_agent/triage.py` already had very similar ranking logic (built earlier this project, items 102-105) but its correlation-based duplicate detection opens one DB connection per pending candidate via `load_equity_curve()` — fine for a manually-run report, far too expensive to run on a page that polls every 7 seconds. Wrote a self-contained version instead: exactly 3 batched `IN (...)` queries total regardless of candidate count, the same discipline item 49's connection-reuse bug taught directly. Duplicate detection itself was left out of the live ranking (still available via `python scripts/research_agent_triage.py` for deep review) — a deliberate scope cut, not an oversight.
    - **Guarded against the `Infinity`/`JSON.parse` bug a second time**: every number computed fresh here (score, SPY return, DSR) goes through a `math.isfinite()` guard before being attached, since these are new values the earlier `_jsonable()` fix (which only covers raw DB columns) wouldn't have caught.
    - **UI**: opening the mailbox now shows a "⭐ Top picks" panel above the three folders — the top 5 undecided candidates by score, each still with live Approve/Hold/Deny buttons — and every folder's card list is now sorted by score (highest first) instead of by recency. Copy is deliberately hedged in the UI itself ("Not a promise of future performance, just the best of what's here by the pipeline's own rigor") since this is a ranking of backtested statistics, not investment advice.
    - **Honest caveat on what this can't do yet**: the LLM-candidate pool this ranks is thin on real PROMOTE verdicts (1 at time of writing) — today's top picks are mostly the relatively-best of a HOLD-heavy pile, which is exactly what "so I don't have to sort through everything manually" should surface, not a claim that great strategies exist right now. Verified live: cross-checked `/api/mailbox`'s computed `score`/`mean_dsr`/`spy_total_return_pct` fields directly, then drove the actual page to confirm the Top Picks panel and per-folder sort order.

52. **Ecosystem dashboard made always-on via launchd — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — the user was manually starting `agent_ecosystem.py` by hand each time and asked to just always be able to reach it.
    - New `scheduling/com.quantplatform.agentecosystem.plist`, modeled directly on `researchagent.plist`'s always-on shape (`RunAtLoad`+`KeepAlive`+`ThrottleInterval=30`, not a `StartCalendarInterval` like the daily/hourly jobs) since this is a standing local web page, not a periodic job. No `Nice` throttle unlike the research agent — this is a lightweight HTTP server polled every few seconds, not a CPU-bound LLM loop, so there's no higher-priority work to yield to.
    - Installed to `~/Library/LaunchAgents/` (same copy-not-symlink pattern every prior plist uses) and loaded. **Verified with a real kill test, not just a config read**: found the running PID, `kill -9`'d it directly, confirmed `launchctl list` showed it briefly gone then back with a new PID within ~6 seconds, and confirmed `http://localhost:8766` was serving again — genuinely self-healing, not just configured to look that way.
    - Still binds to `127.0.0.1` only (unchanged) — reachable from this Mac's own browser at `http://localhost:8766`, not from the network or other devices, same as every dashboard/report in this project.
    - Editing `scripts/agent_ecosystem.py` still works exactly as before and needs no plist change — the plist just points at the file, so a code edit takes effect on the next restart (`launchctl kickstart -k gui/$(id -u)/com.quantplatform.agentecosystem`), which launchd will also do on its own if the process ever crashes.

53. **Mailbox gets a real Leaderboard + approving now auto-enrolls a candidate in paper trading — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — two asks together: (1) a leaderboard the user can act on directly instead of hunting through folders, (2) approved candidates should start paper trading without a manual code edit.
    - **Asked the user directly before touching the trigger, since it's a governance question, not an implementation detail**: should paper trading start the instant the pipeline's own algorithm says PROMOTE (fully automatic, no human ever looks at it), or only after the user personally clicks Approve? User chose **Approve-only** — preserving "automation proposes, a human decides" exactly as it's applied everywhere else in this project. This was a real fork worth pausing on: the "Promoted" folder already includes both pipeline-auto-PROMOTE and human-approved candidates, so triggering off "enters the Promoted folder" would have silently included candidates nobody had reviewed.
    - **How enrollment actually works, given the architecture**: LLM-candidates were never hand-added to `strategies/registry.py` (unlike every hand-written strategy `daily_paper_trade.sh` already trades) — they're raw generated source files loaded dynamically. New `scripts/paper_trade_candidate.py` loads an approved candidate's class via the exact same restricted-exec loader (`research_agent/sandbox.py`'s `load_class_restricted()`) research_agent.py already uses to run it, then calls the same `paper_trading.check_and_update()` every hand-written strategy goes through, using `candidate_id` as the strategy_name (matching the `QQQ/cand_...` convention already seen in promotion snapshots). `daily_paper_trade.sh`'s loop now also queries `strategy_candidates.human_decision == 'approved'` fresh every run (not a hardcoded list) so approving in the mailbox is enough — no file edit needed for a new one to start being paper-traded from the next scheduled run onward.
    - **Immediate feedback, not just "wait until 5pm"**: `agent_ecosystem.py`'s `/api/decide` now fires a background, one-off `paper_trade_candidate.py` check the moment a candidate is approved (fire-and-forget `subprocess.Popen`, doesn't block the HTTP response since a fresh Yahoo Finance fetch can take a few seconds), writing to the same `logs/paper_trade_<date>.log` the scheduled job uses. Verified for real: approved a test candidate via a live POST to `/api/decide`, watched the log file pick up the trigger, retry once through real lock contention with the still-running research loop, then complete cleanly.
    - **Leaderboard**: a new clickable tile above the three folders, showing every undecided, scored candidate ranked by the same composite score from item 51, with live Approve/Hold/Deny on each — this is the direct answer to "a leaderboard I can act on," not just a read-only ranking. The folder-picker screen also keeps a 3-candidate teaser so a glance doesn't require an extra click.
    - **Updated messaging that was about to become actively wrong**: the mailbox's own copy used to say approving "does not by itself add this to strategies/registry.py or paper trading" — true before this item, false after. Rewrote it everywhere it appears to say plainly that approving does start paper trading (fake money only) right away, while still never touching `registry.py` or real trading.
    - **A real finding surfaced along the way, not smoothed over**: while testing the new approved-candidates loop, found a *second* approved candidate (`cand_f6046cbdc0`, `VolatilityRegimeShiftStrategy` on `AGM-A`) that also failed most of its promotion criteria (3 of 5) and the stability check — the same "approved by mistake, before the misleading-label bug was fixed" situation as item 52's CVU candidate, except this one hadn't been caught yet. Flagged to the user rather than silently reversing it myself this time, since it's about to actually start paper trading (with the new auto-enrollment) rather than just sitting filed in a folder. **User confirmed reject it — done**, same `human_decision='rejected'` reversal as CVU. It never actually opened a paper position (only ever returned HOLD), so there was nothing to unwind. `cand_e07d148d60` (`VolumePriceRegimeStrategy`/`AJG`) is now the only candidate in the Promoted folder and the only one the daily approved-candidates loop will paper-trade.

54. **A second, genuinely "aggressive" research track built alongside the conservative pipeline — done, run, verified (2026-07-24)** — user pushback: "why would I want a strategy that underperforms the S&P 500?", then, once pressed on it honestly, revealed the real preference: more comfortable with weaker crash protection in exchange for more raw return and volatility. Confirmed explicitly: **a second track, not a replacement** for the existing conservative one.
    - **What's actually different**: drawdown gate loosened -25% → -50% (`RiskLimits`), position sizing targets 30% annualized vol with up to 1.5x leverage (vs 15%/no leverage), and "beat SPY buy-and-hold over the exact same backtest window" became a real, hard sixth promotion criterion (`evaluate_criteria`'s new `beats_benchmark` param) — not loosened, a *new, harder* bar than the conservative track has ever had. DSR threshold deliberately **unchanged** — aggressive ≠ tolerating overfit noise, only risk tolerance and position sizing moved.
    - **Architecture**: every result stored under a new `_aggressive` strategy-name suffix (matching the existing `_ls`/`_regime` convention), so this reuses 100% of the existing `backtest_runs`/`walk_forward_windows`/`promotion_snapshots`/correlation-gate machinery with zero schema changes. New `scripts/research_cycle_aggressive.py`, mirroring `research_cycle.py`'s shape. Found and fixed a real gap while building it: `walk_forward()` didn't thread `target_annual_vol`/`max_leverage` through its own internal `run_backtest()` calls, silently ignoring the aggressive profile during validation even though a plain full-history backtest already respected it.
    - **Two real connection-per-row performance bugs hit and fixed while building this** (the same bug class item 49 already cost 6+ hours once) — `promotion.py`'s `_return_correlation_matrix()` was still calling `load_equity_curve()` per candidate row; separately, my own new SPY-benchmark code called a per-run DB query inside a loop over the *entire* database. Both fixed by batching into one `IN (...)` query + one price load, cutting a hung multi-minute smoke test down to ~2 minutes.
    - **Full 512-combo sweep result (32 tickers × 16 strategies), reported honestly**: **zero PROMOTE verdicts.** Only 9 combos even beat SPY's own +259.3% full-history return, and every one of those 9 was BTC-USD (thousands of percent, e.g. `sma_aggressive` +19,254%) — and every single BTC-USD combo failed the risk or out-of-sample gate anyway (even the loosened -50% drawdown limit couldn't absorb a real BTC crash window, or a walk-forward test window landed squarely on one). Leverage and a looser drawdown limit didn't manufacture a free edge; it mostly just re-exposed the same conclusion as the conservative track (a strong bull market is hard to beat) with extra steps.
    - **The real finding underneath the BTC numbers, caught because the user pushed on it**: plain buy-and-hold BTC-USD itself returned **+20,622%** over that same window — *more* than every single one of the "aggressive" BTC strategies. None of them had a real edge in Bitcoin; they were all just partially exposed to it during a historic run, and every bit of "smart" timing cost return rather than added it. This is what motivated item 55 below.

55. **New `beats_own_buy_and_hold` promotion check, added after the BTC-USD lesson above, wired to run automatically on every conservative-track cycle — done, verified (2026-07-24)** — an external benchmark (SPY) can't catch a strategy that only "beat the market" because the one asset it traded independently mooned (or, the mirror-image failure mode found here: independently collapsed).
    - **New batched helper** `_own_asset_returns_by_run_id()` in `promotion.py`: buy-and-hold return of the *specific ticker traded*, not a fixed external ticker, over each run's own window. Needed a new `tickers` scoping param on `evaluate_promotion()` itself — the DB now holds 5,869 distinct tickers (item 49's full-universe expansion), and an unscoped call tried to load a price series for every single one of them before this existed, confirmed hanging past 2 minutes.
    - **Real governance moment, checked before committing to a design**: made this a hard, verdict-blocking gate for the aggressive track (matches `beats_benchmark`'s existing role there), but checking it against the conservative track's 6 *currently live* paper-traded combos first showed it would silently demote 3 of them (`QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `XLRE/rsi` — all genuinely profitable in absolute terms, just less than doing nothing) if wired the same way. Asked the user rather than deciding unilaterally: gate, or always-compute-and-display without gating? **User chose display-only** for the conservative track. Implemented via a new `own_asset_gates_promotion` param — same computed number either way, but only actually enters the `all()` verdict check when the caller asks. `research_cycle.py` now always passes `require_beat_own_asset=True, own_asset_gates_promotion=False, tickers=<this cycle's own tickers>` — so every future cycle always shows this comparison next to every verdict, permanently, without being asked again.
    - **Applied retroactively to the existing full-universe PROMOTE list, and it mattered immediately**: of the 1,251 conservative-track PROMOTE combos, 71 also beat SPY over their own window. Checking those against the new own-asset check showed **most of the impressive-looking ones were hollow** — e.g. `APE-USD/rsi`'s reported +128,230% return was only ever compared against a real buy-and-hold of **-77.7%** (the token itself collapsed; almost any positive number "beats" that), same pattern as `LUNC-USD`, `GAME`, `HAO`, `DFSC` (all near -100% own buy-and-hold). Filtering those out left a real, credible shortlist of ~13 combos across 11 tickers where the underlying asset hadn't collapsed *or* mooned — genuine outperformance, not an artifact of the comparison.
    - **Then filtered by real-world tradability, since a backtest doesn't know a ticker is thinly traded**: of that shortlist, `CIX` (CompX International, 5,198 shares/day avg volume), `CCZ` (a little-known "Comcast Holdings Corp" tracking security, not the well-known Comcast — 296 shares/day), and `GJP` (a 2005-era structured trust certificate tied to old Dominion Resources bonds, 411 shares/day) were all set aside as impractical to actually trade at any size, regardless of what the numbers said. `COLM` (Columbia Sportswear, 565K shares/day) and `TIGO` (Millicom International Cellular, 1.74M shares/day) survived every filter: real, liquid, recognizable companies.
    - **Added to live paper trading**: `COLM/bollinger_ls` (+315.8% full-history vs SPY +259.3% vs own buy-and-hold +39.3%; 113 trades across all 4 walk-forward windows, every one positive — the stronger, better-evidenced of the two) and `TIGO/sma_ls` (+220.6% vs SPY +186.1% vs own buy-and-hold +43.6%; max drawdown a mild -19.2%, but only 13 total trades — a thinner sample, flagged as "watch, don't over-trust yet" rather than fully proven). Both added to `daily_paper_trade.sh` and `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`, both manually verified end-to-end (fresh data fetch, clean signal computation, starting flat with $10,000 fake equity) before being left to run daily. 8 combos now live: the original `QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `XLP/bollinger`, `TIP/bollinger`, `FXI/bollinger_regime`, `XLRE/rsi`, the bear-market watch item `QQQ/breakout_ls`, plus these two new ones.
    - **Note for later**: `FXI/bollinger_regime` (one of the original 6 live combos) already shows HOLD under the existing, unmodified promotion criteria as of this session — unrelated to anything built here, most likely because a newer stored backtest run overwrote the one that originally promoted it. Not yet investigated.
56. **DYNR data-quality incident → three new Research Agent auto-reject gates, applied both retroactively and going forward — done, verified (2026-07-25)** — the user spotted `cand_18148c4f1b` (DYNR, VolumePriceDivergenceStrategy) sitting **already approved into paper trading** with a score of 111 and a backtest return in the +3,183%–3,749% range, and asked if it was legitimate. It wasn't, but not because of a code bug — the strategy source was clean, vectorized, no lookahead. The real problem: DYNR is a ~$0.60 penny stock where 57% of its 4,164-day price history has zero recorded volume and 91% is under 5,000 shares/day. The backtest's frictionless-execution assumption (fixed 0.05%/0.05% fee/slippage) is meaningless against a ticker that often doesn't really trade. Root cause: `data/universe.py`'s stock-universe builder had never applied any price/volume floor at all.
    - **Fix 1 — liquidity floor, both layers**: added `is_liquid_ticker()` + `LIQUIDITY_LOOKBACK_DAYS=20`, `MIN_AVG_CLOSE=$5`, `MIN_AVG_VOLUME=100,000` shares/day to `data/universe.py` as the single source of truth. Wired into **both** `research_agent_loop.sh`'s ticker-picker query (so illiquid names are mostly never even attempted) **and** `research_agent.py`'s own `main()` (so a manual `--tickers DYNR` refuses too, regardless of entry point — defense in depth, not just a loop-level filter). DYNR itself: reclassified `human_decision` from `approved` to `rejected` with an explanatory note; no paper-trade events existed yet, so nothing needed unwinding.
    - **User follow-up**: "the mailbox has too many strategies, most don't beat the S&P 500 — only push ones that do." **Fix 2 — beats-SPY gate**: replaced the earlier (2026-07-24, item 174) positive-raw-return-only auto-reject with a real apples-to-apples comparison — `promotion.benchmark_return_pct(backtest_run_id, "SPY")` pulls SPY's actual buy-and-hold return over the *exact same stored date window* the candidate's own backtest covered; anything that doesn't beat that gets auto-filed to "Doesn't work" (`rejected_below_benchmark`) before ever reaching `pending_human_review`. Applied retroactively to the existing backlog too: of 250 candidates sitting in the mailbox awaiting a decision, 235 didn't clear the new bar and were reclassified — 15 remained.
    - **User caught something the new gate itself missed**: of those 15 "keepers," 11 had a flat **0% return** — they never actually traded, and only "beat" SPY because SPY itself lost money over that particular window (doing nothing loses less than a loser). **Fix 3 — zero-trades gate** (`rejected_no_trades`, `num_trades == 0`): added, applied retroactively (the 11 stripped from the mailbox), verified live.
    - **User then asked about `XAR`'s "Volatility Regime Shift" candidate specifically** (already approved, live in paper trading) — it turned out to have made exactly **1 trade** across a 15-year backtest: bought once in 2011, held continuously, never sold. Real ticker, real data (XAR/iShares Aerospace & Defense genuinely ran from ~$25 to ~$270/share over that window — not an illiquidity artifact like DYNR), but a 1-trade "strategy" is indistinguishable from "got lucky buying this one ticker at this one moment," and it had also breached the platform's own -25% max-drawdown risk limit (real drawdown: -40.3%) both in the full history and in one walk-forward window — which is why it was stuck at HOLD, not PROMOTE, before a human approved it anyway. **Pulled out of paper trading**: recorded a same-price exit (opened and closed at the identical $269.01 print, since no new trading day had occurred between entry and this — zero real P&L impact either way) and reclassified `human_decision` to `rejected` with notes. **Fix 4 — minimum-trades gate** (`rejected_too_few_trades`, `MIN_TRADES_FOR_EVIDENCE = 5`, deliberately a low bar — not a statistical-significance threshold, just enough to screen out single-bet cases; this project's own convention already treats even a 13-trade sample as "thin, don't over-trust yet" but still worth a human's judgment, per item 171's TIGO/sma_ls note). Applied retroactively: the one other real candidate left in the mailbox (`CBRS`) also turned out to have just 1 trade and was reclassified the same way — leaving the mailbox essentially empty of undecided candidates, ready to refill cleanly under all four gates going forward.
    - **All four gates live-verified** against the running `research_agent_loop.sh` (restarted via `kill <pid>` + launchd `KeepAlive` relaunch after each change, same pattern as every prior loop-code change): watched real freshly-generated candidates get auto-rejected at each new stage in the log in real time, and confirmed via direct DB query that a real multi-trade, benchmark-beating candidate (`XLP`/VolumePriceDivergenceStrategy, 135 trades) correctly passed every new gate and reached the beats-SPY comparison as a legitimate candidate would.
57. **Ecosystem dashboard: all 4 rooms redetailed with 15 new furniture/prop types, roughly doubling density per room — done, verified (2026-07-25, overnight)** — the user, heading to bed for ~10 hours, asked for the 4 isometric rooms (item 48/54) to look "extremely unique," "a lot of detail, a lot of things in each room," explicitly framed as wanting "a big surprise" on waking. Treated as an open-ended overnight session, not a scoped single task.
    - **15 new prop-drawing functions added to `DECOS`** (the reusable per-box decoration system from item 48): `bookshelf` (2 shelves of colored spines, palette configurable per room via `decoColors`), `whiteboard` (hand-sketched lines + a red-marker trend scribble), `screenReadout` (bezel + glowing screen + pulsing scan line + blinking data-ticks), `potPlant` (swaying leaf blades), `steamCup` (rising steam wisps), `clockFace` (decorative-speed hour/minute hands, same "ambient life, not real time" convention as the vault's existing radar sweep/hourglass), `scales` (justice balance — fits the Records Hall's actual subject, government legal disclosures), `gavelBlock`, `trophyCup`, `camDome` (security camera with blinking record light), `corkboard` ("investigation wall" with pinned printouts + red string — literal fit for a room that tracks who traded what), `pipetteArm` (articulated lab arm), `pendantLamp` (hanging cord + shade + light pool, anchored from a small high-`z` ceiling box), `stanchion` (velvet-rope posts spanning a box's own footprint), `tickerDigits` (blinking monospace-glyph strip standing in for a scrolling price feed), `rugPattern` (flat two-tone floor rug via a near-zero-height box). All reuse existing CSS animation classes (`.led`, `.trend-glow`, `.iso-glow`) where the motion fit, rather than inventing new keyframes needlessly; added 4 new ones (`.leaf-sway`, `.steam`, `.clock-hand`/`.clock-hour`/`.clock-min`, `.arm-articulate`) only where nothing existing fit.
    - **Each room's `ISO_SCENES` furniture list rewritten**, not just appended to — went from 7-9 items/room to 13-15, with a deliberate per-room identity rather than generic reused clutter: **Research Lab** gained a pipette arm over the beakers, a coffee mug, a bookshelf, a potted plant atop the storage cabinet, a second monitor, and a whiteboard. **Trading Floor** gained a trophy on the best-looking podium, a scrolling ticker-digit strip under the wall board, a market clock, a rug, a plant, and a hanging pendant lamp. **Records Hall** (leaning harder into its real subject — government trading disclosures) gained a matching second entrance pillar, scales of justice on top of one pillar, a security camera on the other, a corkboard investigation wall, a law-library bookshelf (recolored from the old generic stripe pattern), and a courthouse clock. **The Vault** gained a ceiling chandelier, velvet-rope stanchions guarding the vault-door approach, a grand lobby clock, a security camera, and a potted plant.
    - **Verification was structural, not just eyeballed** — the browser tool's screenshot output caps around 800px wide regardless of actual viewport size, so fine isometric detail is genuinely hard to confirm by looking. Instead: extracted and ran the embedded JS through `node --check` (clean), then, per room, queried the live DOM for exact counts of every new CSS class (`leaf-sway`, `steam`, `arm-articulate`, `clock-hand`, etc.) and confirmed each matched the number of props actually placed (e.g. 5 `leaf-sway` elements for a 5-leaf plant, 3 `steam` for a 3-wisp cup, 2 `clock-hand` per clock), scanned every room's rendered SVG for `NaN` in coordinates (zero found — nothing computed with an undefined value), and confirmed every new element's bounding box sat inside the room's own `viewBox` (nothing silently rendered off-canvas). Also re-verified the pre-existing interactive pieces still worked after the rewrite: clicking a room still opens its real live-data side panel (checked against `The Vault`, showing genuine backup log lines), and the mailbox still opens with genuine current counts.
    - **Real bug caught mid-session, unrelated to the visual work itself but found because of it**: `agent_ecosystem.py` turned out to already be a `launchd`-managed always-on service (item 54, `com.quantplatform.agentecosystem`, `KeepAlive`) — manually starting a second instance via `nohup` to preview changes collided with it on port 8766 (`OSError: Address already in use`), silently knocking the real managed instance into a crashed, un-retried state (`ThrottleInterval=30s` had it sitting dead, not spinning). Caught by checking `launchctl list` before declaring the work done, not left for the user to discover as "the dashboard's broken" in the morning — killed the manual instance, `launchctl kickstart -k` to force the managed one to restart clean with the new code, confirmed via `curl` + a real browser reload that the *actual* always-on service (not a throwaway test process) is what's serving the finished rooms.
58. **A fifth agent, "Stock Agent": watches news + charts hourly, surfaces facts only, never advice — done, verified (2026-07-25)** — the user asked for an agent that's always watching the market and notifying them "whatever he thinks is a good time to buy" — asked twice, in slightly different words each time. Declined that specific framing both times, directly and without hedging: a system saying "now's a good time to buy X" is personalized investment advice regardless of how automated it is, who's asking for it, or whether they'd actually act on it — a hard line, not a style preference. The user accepted a narrower version instead: an agent that surfaces **objective, factual signals** ("AAPL: volume 3.2x its 20-day average") and never a verdict, score, or buy/sell judgment. Used Plan Mode given the real scope (new data source, new always-on schedule, new DB table, new dashboard room) — the plan itself states this boundary as the one hard constraint everything else is designed around, enforced at the schema level (no `verdict`/`recommendation`/`score` column exists, anywhere) so a later change can't quietly reintroduce it.
    - **Real discovery during planning, not assumed**: FMP's stock-news endpoint (a real, documented endpoint) returns HTTP 402 ("Restricted Endpoint — upgrade your plan") on this project's existing `FMP_API_KEY` — the free/current tier that already covers House disclosures (item 120) does not include news. Confirmed with one real test call before building anything around an assumed response. Fell back to Yahoo Finance's free, keyless per-ticker RSS feed (`data/stock_news.py`), confirmed directly to return real, current headlines with real timestamps — this is the primary news path today, with the FMP path left in and tried first so it starts working automatically, zero code changes, if the plan is ever upgraded.
    - **Sentiment scoring reuses the existing local, free Hermes3/Ollama plumbing** (`llm_client.score_news_sentiment()`, same `_ollama_chat`/`LOCAL_MODELS` branch as `generate_candidate`) — zero incremental cost. **Real problem found and fixed by testing, not assumed to be fine**: the prompt alone told hermes3 not to use advice-style wording, and on repeated real calls it still produced phrasing like "may present investors with a fresh buying opportunity" whenever the source headline itself used that framing (measured: 1 of 3 real repeated calls slipped through a first-pass exact-phrase blocklist too, via a paraphrase — "an opportunity for investors to buy" — the fixed list didn't happen to cover). Fixed with a second, independent layer: a regex over the underlying trading-action words themselves (buy/sell/invest/opportunity, in any form) anywhere in the model's own output, not a phrase list — same "defense in depth, don't trust one layer" reasoning as the liquidity floor (item 56) being enforced at both the picker and the core pipeline. Re-tested 5/5 on the same problematic headline (all correctly rejected) plus real neutral/negative control headlines (both passed through undistorted) before treating this as solved.
    - **Chart-signal detection (`data/stock_signals.py`) is deliberately not a `Strategy` and not backtested** — same-day volume-spike/price-breakout screens over already-stored OHLCV, reusing the exact shifted-rolling-average pattern already verified correct in `strategies/volume_breakout.py`'s `_volume_confirmed()`. Spot-checked by hand against raw data (TSLA: real 20-day-low breakout at $319.69 vs a real prior low of $368.60, confirmed correct by reading the raw price rows directly).
    - **Real bug found via the first live watchlist run**: ranking "most liquid" by raw share/token volume filled the watchlist almost entirely with crypto (`BTC-USD`, `ETH-USD`, `SOL-USD`...) rather than real stocks, since a token's volume counts coins, not dollars, and dwarfs normal share counts numerically. The user asked specifically about stocks. Fixed by excluding `*-USD`/`*=F` tickers and ranking by dollar volume (`close * volume`) instead of raw volume — re-verified the watchlist is now real, recognizable large caps (`MU, SPY, NVDA, QQQ, AAPL, TSLA, MSFT, AMD, AMZN...`).
    - **Watchlist size and schedule interval are measured, not guessed**: a real timed run showed sentiment-scoring (the dominant cost) takes ~10-13s/ticker; at that rate the plan's original 200-ticker default would take ~40 minutes, too tight against an hourly schedule with no margin. Set to 75 tickers (~16 minutes/run) instead, with the reasoning and the real measurement left in a code comment so this isn't re-litigated blind later.
    - **Real mistake made and corrected during verification, worth recording plainly**: mistook a still-running background test process for a stale leftover (based on indirect evidence — the DB already had a plausible-looking number of real signal rows) and killed it before it had actually finished a full pass; the harness's own "completed" status for that background task only arrived *after* the kill, revealing it had actually been interrupted mid-run, not finished. Caught immediately (the task notification showed `status: failed`, not the state I'd assumed), and low-cost to fix: the data already collected was still valid and correctly-shaped, and a second, clean full run (this time triggered through the real launchd job rather than a manual shell command) replaced it. Recorded here rather than glossed over, matching the standing instruction to log real mistakes as they happen, not just successes.

A **signal feed** panel (not a ranked/scored list, unlike the mailbox's "Top picks" — deliberately just `created_at DESC`, since nothing here is approved/rejected/ranked) shows every recorded signal in plain language. Verified live in the browser: the 5th room renders correctly at its new grid position, the panel shows real live status, and the signal feed shows real recorded signals (`WMT`, `LLY`, `JPM`, `META`, `TSLA`, `NVDA`... each phrased as fact, e.g. "news sentiment shifted negative — the headlines...").

The user decides what a signal means — this agent's job ends at "here is a fact."

60. **Fees + slippage added to live paper-trading equity — done, verified (2026-07-25)** — real gap found while looking for the next "more realistic" fix: paper trading applied zero trading cost, while the backtests that justified every one of these strategies charge 0.05% fees + 0.05% slippage per side (`backtest/run.py`). Every "beats SPY" number shown anywhere came from cost-adjusted evidence; the day-to-day paper P&L tracking it was silently frictionless.
    - Added `DEFAULT_FEES`/`DEFAULT_SLIPPAGE` as module-level constants in `backtest/run.py` (single source of truth, same values as before — no change to existing backtest behavior) and imported them into `store/paper.py`'s `_price_return()`, which now applies slippage to the effective entry/exit price and a fee deduction on each side — realized (closed) trades pay both sides' cost; an unrealized, still-open mark only pays the entry side, since the exit hasn't happened yet.
    - **Verified three ways**: a hand-computed example (long $100→$110 nets 9.78% after cost, not the naive 10%) matched the code exactly; a real production event (today's QQQ/breakout wash trade, entered and exited at the identical $684.23 print) correctly went from a false 0.00% to a realistic -0.13% loss; live in the dashboard, QQQ/breakout's roster line changed from +0.0% to -0.1% after a restart.
61. **Portfolio-level concentration check across the live paper-trading roster — done, verified (2026-07-25)** — second half of the same "smoother/more realistic/more profitable" answer: a check for whether combos that look diversified (different tickers) are actually correlated in *when* they're exposed to the market, which raw ticker-price correlation would miss entirely if the shared driver is a common strategy trigger (e.g. a market-wide volatility spike) rather than the tickers themselves moving together.
    - `_portfolio_correlation()` (`agent_ecosystem.py`) replays every currently-tracked combo's own real strategy signals against its own real stored price history into a daily +1/0/-1 (short/flat/long) exposure series, then correlates those series pairwise and clusters anything ≥ `promotion.py`'s existing `DEFAULT_CORRELATION_THRESHOLD` (0.7) via union-find — reused rather than inventing a second "highly correlated" number. Purely a read-only view (no gate, nothing auto-rejected), fetched once when the Paper Trader panel opens rather than on every ~6s refresh, since it's real computation (~0.3s for 19 combos), not a cheap SQL read.
    - **Honest result, not the one expected**: only one real cluster found — `QQQ/breakout`/`QQQ/breakout_ls` (0.88 correlation), which is expected by design (deliberately paired variants). The earlier hand-wavy concern about the 6 `VolatilityRegimeShiftStrategy` combos (item 59) moving together turned out to be wrong once actually measured — their real signal timing isn't synchronized across those different tickers. A small follow-up fix: `same_family` originally compared exact registry keys, so `breakout`/`breakout_ls` read as "different families" (a false "worth a look" flag on an expected pairing) until a `_strategy_base_family()` helper stripped the known `_ls`/`_regime`/`_macro` suffixes first.
    - **A real, transient concurrent-load effect observed, not a bug**: the "checked N of 19" count varied run to run (19, 16, 15) purely from real lock contention with the other live background agents polling the same DuckDB file at that moment — confirmed by re-running the identical check in isolation and getting a clean 19/19 every time. The existing per-combo `except Exception: continue` already handles this correctly (skip and report the honest count, never crash or overclaim coverage).
62. **Insider-trading (SEC Form 4) copy-strategy: found substantially pre-built, extended, honest negative verdict across every variant tested — done, verified (2026-07-25)** — asked to build this as the "more profitable" swing (a genuinely new signal source, not another refinement of price/volume-based strategies). Read the existing code first rather than assuming a blank slate: `data/sec_insider_trades.py` (SEC bulk Form 3/4/5 ingestion, 321,183 real rows already loaded, 2023-07 to 2026-06), `congress_copy.py`'s reused `build_copy_portfolio()`/`build_purchase_hold_portfolio()`, `store/insider_copy.py`, and two CLI scripts were all already built and had already been run for real, with results sitting in `insider_copy_backtests`.
    - **Dug into the most eye-catching existing result before trusting it**: the "ALL owners, matched-sale" run showed +138.7% vs SPY's +66.6% over the same window — but re-running it to inspect the actual matched trades showed only **3 of 295,604** disclosed events ever completed a real buy-then-sell round trip (insiders overwhelmingly just sell, routine and compensation-driven, so requiring a matching sale starves this model of almost all its evidence — already correctly diagnosed in the existing code's own docstring). The headline return is almost entirely unrealized mark-to-market on 25 still-open positions, not realized profit, and one of the 3 real closed trades is a 13x gain on a single microcap penny stock (ASPI) — the same thin-evidence trap as XAR/CBZ (item 56), just at a much bigger scale. Not a meaningful result.
    - **The real comparison is the purchase-only/fixed-holding-period variant** (item 176, `build_purchase_hold_portfolio()` — buys every disclosed purchase, holds ~6 months, no matching sale needed): a real, honest negative already existed (+15.7% vs SPY +66.6%, 136 real closed trades). Rather than stop there, tested the stronger, better-motivated version of the hypothesis — academic insider-trading research (Lakonishok & Lee 2001; Cohen/Malloy/Pomorski 2012) finds whatever edge exists concentrated in large, discretionary purchases by top executives, not the full unfiltered filer population (~45% of which report no title at all).
    - **Real SEC bulk-data quality issue found and handled while building the filter**: a small fraction of disclosed transactions have a nonsensical `TRANS_PRICEPERSHARE` (up to $29M/share — no US stock has ever traded above ~$700k/share) producing implausible dollar amounts up to $7.17 quadrillion in one row — filer/XML data-entry errors, not real trades. Confirmed `amount`/`price_per_share` aren't used anywhere else in the codebase (safe to filter locally rather than touch the shared ingestion module); added a documented sanity bound (price $0.01–$1,000,000/share, amount $1,000–$50,000,000) in the new script, dropping 11,745 of 63,405 purchases.
    - New `scripts/insider_exec_conviction_backtest.py`: filters to purchases ≥ a configurable dollar threshold (default $250k) by CEO/CFO/COO/President/Chairman-titled filers (regex over the free-text title field, deliberately excluding narrower titles like Chief Technology/Legal/Accounting Officer). Real cohort: 2,263 purchases, 963 distinct insiders, 780 distinct tickers.
    - **Result: the refinement did not rescue the idea, and made risk-adjusted performance worse, not better.** At 5% position sizing: +14.5% vs SPY +67.9%, Sharpe 0.36 vs SPY's 1.44, max drawdown -33.3% vs SPY's -19.0% (118 real closed trades; 1,856 of 1,974 matched signals skipped for insufficient shared cash — a real capacity constraint, not a bug). Re-ran at 2% sizing to check whether that capital constraint was masking a real signal by starving it of capacity: with far more signals actually taken (299 closed vs 118), the result got *worse*, not better (+10.0% vs SPY +67.9%) — closing the question rather than leaving it open.
    - **Honest final verdict, not oversold**: every variant tested tonight — unfiltered copy-with-matched-sale (statistically meaningless, 3 trades), unfiltered purchase-hold (real, underperforms), and the more sophisticated exec-conviction purchase-hold at two position sizes (real, underperforms further) — underperforms simply holding SPY over this specific ~3-year, strong-bull-market window. Not recommending this for paper trading in its current form. Marked items 173/176 complete: the building and testing work is genuinely finished, the answer is a real, well-supported no, not an unfinished thread.

Also ran the embedded JS through `node --check` before touching the live server, same discipline as item 57.

Fixed with `_collapse_repeats()`, merging consecutive identical (signal_type, description) pairs into one entry spanning first_seen..last_seen ("closed at a new 20-day high, 20:00–21:00, still standing") — makes the tool honestly reflect what happened instead of looking artificially dramatic, without changing how the underlying agent records data.

67. **Decipher @compoundlessons (Kusha), a crypto trader/mentor on X, then encode his stated framework as a real, testable strategy — done, verified (2026-07-26)** — asked to fully read and understand one trader's public posting history back to Dec 15, 2025, then judge his legitimacy, then (after the user said they know him personally and believe his numbers) build a strategy encoding his framework into the platform.

His framework, in plain terms: a discretionary-but-rules-based breakout/short-pivot trader, almost entirely in illiquid micro-cap crypto (CASHCAT, ZAMA, XPIN, UAI, etc. — not carried by this platform's data source). Named setups: "cheat entry" (enter inside the base, before full confirmation), "second day play," "short pivot"/"stairway up → elevator down" (citing Minervini's 50/80 rule + O'Neil base-counting). Entry filters he states explicitly: 3+ tests of a resistance level, higher lows into it, volume dry-up then a volume-confirmed breakout, price above the 50/200 EMA ("trend template"), stop distance under ~0.7x ADR. Risk in R-multiples, 0.5–1.5% real per-trade risk, three-stage trailing stop (hard stop at breakout-candle low → breakeven → trail by 5 EMA). Runs a paid 1:1 mentorship (~15 students) alongside the account.
    - **Verdict on legitimacy, asked directly**: real trading knowledge, not fabricated — the technical vocabulary is specific and used consistently across 7 months, he discloses real losses next to wins in the same posts, and one image (a BloFin exchange-native trade card: exact entry/exit, realized PnL, 20x leverage, timestamps) is real exchange UI, meaningfully harder to fake than a claim or a hand-edited screenshot. But every aggregate number ($148K in 9mo, a separately-claimed "~$250K in 2yr" that doesn't cleanly reconcile with the first) is self-reported with zero independent audit, and he's directly monetized (BloFin/CoinMarketCap affiliate tags, paid mentorship) — real skill, unaudited and monetized presentation, not a clean "trust the numbers" story. Went back and opened all 15 image-bearing posts specifically (of the 84, checked via `[data-testid="tweetPhoto"]`, not eyeballing) after being asked directly whether images had been reviewed — they didn't change the verdict, just added the one strong (trade card) and a few weak (his own compiled cumulative-PnL chart, a branded recap template) data points above.
    - **Built `strategies/kusha_breakout.py`** (`KushaBreakoutStrategy` + `KushaBreakoutLongShortStrategy`), encoding every rule above that's stated as an explicit, quantifiable threshold as a fixed constant (not fit to data, same convention as blend weights/`turn_of_month`'s `exit_window` elsewhere) — resistance-test count, higher-lows check, volume dry-up→confirm, 50/200 EMA trend filter, 5-EMA trailing exit, and a mirrored short-pivot short side. Explicitly NOT encoded: his "name selection" (this platform's data doesn't carry his actual tickers) and true per-trade path-dependent trailing state (every existing strategy family here is a stateless vectorized signal; approximated as a single 5-EMA-cross exit instead, documented as a simplification).
    - **Real finding, not a bug**: the ADR-ratio stop rule produced 0-2 entries total across every param combo and 10 liquid crypto tickers on **daily** bars. Diagnosed directly (not assumed): a close-confirmed breakout entry only fires on an unusually wide-range bar by construction, so requiring that same bar's stop distance to be tight relative to the *average* bar range is close to a structural contradiction — his real "cheat entry" (entering inside the base, before the wide confirming bar) is a materially different, more path-dependent signal than what a vectorized "close > resistance" entry can express.
    - **User asked to test intraday crypto specifically, since that's what he actually trades** — built a new `data/fetch_intraday.py` (`ohlcv_hourly` table; confirmed live that Yahoo's `interval="1h"` really does cap at 730 days back), pulled 2 years of hourly bars for 10 liquid crypto tickers, and threaded a `freq` param through `run_backtest()`/`walk_forward()` (default `"D"`, so every existing daily caller is byte-identical) so Sharpe annualizes correctly on hourly bars (24×365/yr, crypto trades round the clock). Same 0-2-trade wall reappeared at hourly resolution too — confirming the ADR-rule diagnosis was about entry-timing logic, not bar-count scale — so `_adr_ok()` was kept in the file (documented, unused by `generate_signals()`) rather than silently deleted, and dropped from the actual entry condition.
    - **Real backtest, real result, not written to the shared results DB** (`backtest_runs`/`walk_forward_windows` assume one canonical daily-resolution run per ticker string, with no column to distinguish bar resolution — writing hourly runs in under the same ticker name would silently corrupt that assumption for every other reader). New `scripts/kusha_intraday_backtest.py` instead: full backtest + 4-window walk-forward + the same pure `evaluate_criteria()` five-gate rule used everywhere else, printed directly. **1 of 20 combos (10 tickers × long/short) PROMOTEs**: XRP-USD/kusha_breakout, +49.9% over 2 years, 105 trades, 35.2% win rate, mean DSR 0.52, every gate cleared. The other 19 mostly failed on `dsr_above_threshold` and/or `all_oos_windows_positive`; BTC-USD (both directions) lost money outright (-22.7%/-25.0%); the short-pivot side underperformed its long-only counterpart on almost every ticker. Win rates across all 20 landed 22.9-41.1% — genuinely close to the ~37% he states, for whatever that's worth given none of his own numbers are independently verifiable.
    - **Honest bottom line, stated plainly, not oversold**: this is not a validated, robust edge — 19/20 combos held, and the one PROMOTE is a single ticker/param combo out of a modest sweep, exactly the kind of result that needs real out-of-sample time to mean much. It's also not nothing — the framework, tested honestly on this platform's universe rather than his own, produced one combo that cleared every statistical gate the platform holds every other strategy to. Not wired into paper trading — same "propose, human decides" pattern as every other new combo this session.

68. **Real bug found and fixed: the mailbox's SPY comparison badge was silently hidden on any candidate whose walk-forward produced zero windows — done, verified (2026-07-26)** — asked why a specific candidate (`cand_6af6d0eadc`, a mean-reversion strategy screened on `SUNC`, currently `pending_human_review`) showed no comparison to the S&P.
    - Traced it in the live DB and API, not by guessing: `spy_total_return_pct` (+10.1%) and `beats_spy` (true, vs. this candidate's +20.4%) were both already correctly computed and present in `/api/mailbox`'s payload — the data was never missing. `agent_ecosystem.py`'s JS (`renderMailList`'s card template) bundled the SPY badge inside the same `<div>` as the deflated-Sharpe/worst-OOS-Sharpe line, gated on `mean_dsr !== null`. SUNC only has 178 days of price history (a recent listing), so its walk-forward produced 0 windows (too little data to split), leaving `mean_dsr` null — which silently hid the SPY badge too, even though the SPY comparison has nothing to do with walk-forward and only needs the full-history backtest + its equity curve, both of which existed.
    - Fixed by decoupling the two facts: DSR/OOS-Sharpe and the SPY badge now each render independently based on their own availability, joined with a separator only when both are present. Verified `node --check` on the extracted script, restarted the server, confirmed via a live DOM query that the SUNC card now shows "🏆 beat SPY buy-and-hold over this backtest's own window (this: +20.4% vs SPY: +10.1%)".
    - Flagged a follow-up task (not done as part of this fix): audit whether any already-decided candidates were approved/rejected by a human without ever seeing this comparison, since it would have been silently missing for any short-history ticker, not just SUNC.

69. **Widened the Kusha-framework sweep to 30 tickers (still 1 pass); built genuine hourly-frequency paper trading for it — done, verified (2026-07-26)** — asked "can we do both": widen the ticker sweep to check whether XRP-USD/kusha_breakout's lone PROMOTE (item 67) was real or a 10-ticker-sample fluke, and separately build the hourly paper-trading infrastructure that combo would need.
    - **Widened sweep**: fetched hourly bars for the top 30 crypto tickers by average daily dollar volume (up from 10), re-ran the same backtest+walk-forward+five-gate check — 60 combos total (long + long/short). **Still exactly 1 pass: XRP-USD/kusha_breakout**, same numbers as before (+49.9%, 105 trades, mean DSR 0.52). Stated plainly rather than spun positively: going from 1/20 to 1/60 is a *lower* hit rate, not stronger confirmation — with no correction for the number of different tickers searched (DSR's own multiple-testing correction only accounts for the 8-point per-ticker param grid, not for trying 30 different tickers looking for any pass at all — the same global multiple-testing gap already flagged for the big universe run, item 49), landing exactly one pass out of 60 independent tests is close to what you'd expect from chance alone at this DSR threshold. Several near-misses failed on only one of the five gates in each direction (LINK-USD/ADA-USD on `dsr_above_threshold` only; DOT-USD/ZEC-USD on `all_oos_windows_positive` only) rather than uniformly failing the same way, consistent with a genuine (if underwhelming) spread of real results, not a rigged or buggy test.
    - **Built genuine hourly paper trading, not a shortcut**: confirmed directly that the existing daily paper-trading path (`paper_trading.py`'s `check_and_update()`, `store/paper.py`'s `paper_trade_events`) is structurally daily-only — `event_date` is a `DATE` column, and `check_and_update()` explicitly truncates the bar timestamp via `.date()` before recording, so two real, distinct hourly events on the same calendar day (an entry at 14:00, an exit at 18:00) would collide. Rather than migrate that table and touch every existing reader (paper_equity_summary, the mailbox's paper views, blend_backtest.py), built a fully separate, parallel path: new `paper_trade_events_intraday` table (`store/paper_intraday.py`, `event_ts TIMESTAMP` instead of `event_date DATE`, reusing `store/paper.py`'s own `_price_return()` cost-model function directly rather than duplicating it), a new `check_and_update_intraday()` in `paper_trading.py` that records the full timestamp and sizes positions against `HOURLY_PERIODS_PER_YEAR = 24*365` (not the daily default's 252 — passing the wrong one would silently mis-size every position by ~6x), a new CLI (`scripts/paper_trade_intraday.py`), a new shell wrapper (`scripts/hourly_paper_trade.sh`), and a new launchd job (`com.quantplatform.papertrade_intraday`, firing 5 minutes past all 24 hours — crypto trades round the clock, so unlike the daily equity job there's no single "after close" time to wait for).
    - **Verified the exact scenario this was built for, not just that it runs**: manually recorded two synthetic events on the *same calendar day* at different hours directly against the new table, confirmed both stored with distinct timestamps (not collapsed/overwritten) and replayed into a correct realized return — the precise case the old DATE-column path couldn't represent. Then ran the real CLI against XRP-USD/kusha_breakout live (correctly refreshed hourly data, reported HOLD/flat, $10,000 starting equity), loaded the new launchd job, and triggered it via `launchctl start` (same verification convention as every other scheduled job here) — confirmed it fires end-to-end with no launchd-level errors and logs correctly to its own dated file.
    - **Watching one combo, thin evidence stated plainly on the watch item itself**: `hourly_paper_trade.sh`'s only entry is XRP-USD/kusha_breakout, with its own comment restating that 1-pass-out-of-60 is closer to a chance result than a confirmed edge — this is being watched forward with fake money precisely *because* it's unproven, matching the same "propose, human decides, paper trading is the free way to watch a thin bet" pattern already used for QQQ/breakout_ls (item 38) and TIGO/COLM (item 45).

70. **Built a real triage of the 876-stock full-universe PROMOTE backlog — 5 survivors out of 14,124 re-evaluated combos, one already independently live — done, verified (2026-07-26)** — asked to actually work through the pile flagged since item 49: 1,236 PROMOTE verdicts from the 2026-07-24 full-universe run, 887 distinct stock tickers of them, never reviewed, too many to eyeball one by one, and (per item 49's own caveat) the wrong scale of evidence to trust at the standard bar since testing thousands of tickers is tens of thousands of independent trials with no global multiple-testing correction.
    - New `scripts/universe_promote_triage.py`: took the 876 stock-like PROMOTE tickers from cycle `3ac3c609...` (887 distinct minus the original 32-ticker UNIVERSE already reviewed individually across many earlier sessions), and re-ran `evaluate_promotion()` — the platform's own already-tested five/six/seven-gate function, not new statistics — with a **stricter DSR bar (0.75, not the standard 0.5)** and **both `beats_own_buy_and_hold` and `beats SPY` turned on as hard gates** instead of report-only (both already existed as optional params, added items 55/47, just never both gated together at once). Looked up all 5 via `yfinance`'s own `.info` rather than assuming from the ticker alone: `COLM` (Columbia Sportswear, `bollinger_ls`, DSR 0.86) — **already one of the 6 live paper-traded combos** (item 45/171) — the triage independently re-deriving an already-validated, already-live result is a real, meaningful sanity check that the method works, not just noise passing through. `BLCO` (Bausch + Lomb, `bollinger_ls`, DSR 0.83), `AD` (Array Digital Infrastructure, `bollinger_ls`, DSR 0.77), `DHC` (Diversified Healthcare Trust, a REIT, `volume_breakout_ls`, DSR 0.75) are real operating companies/REITs. `CRF` (Cornerstone Total Return Fund, `turn_of_month`, DSR 0.99) is flagged separately: it's a **closed-end fund, not an operating company** — a month-end calendar effect on a fund's own NAV/premium/distribution schedule is a different, fund-specific mechanism than the market-wide flow effect `turn_of_month` was originally built and validated on (item 27's 32-ticker index/sector-ETF universe), worth more skepticism than the other 4 before trusting it the same way.
    - Not wired into paper trading — a reviewable shortlist of 5 (down from an unreviewable 876), not a decision. Same "propose, human decides" pattern as everything else.

71. **Chased item 42's QQQ/SPY pairs-trading near-miss — a real, honest negative once properly evidenced, not a suppressed edge (2026-07-26)** — user asked to build pairs trading; turned out `pairs.py`/`scripts/pairs_trade_backtest.py` already existed (item 42, 2026-07-23) and had already tested these exact 3 pairs (XLE/USO, QQQ/SPY, GLD/SLV), still 0/12 promoted with no drift since. Rather than redo finished work, dug into the one open thread: `QQQ_SPY_PAIR`'s bollinger/rsi legs cleared every gate except `sufficient_oos_evidence` (strong worst-OOS-Sharpe 1.89/1.15, DSR 0.69/0.74, but only traded in 1-2 of 4 walk-forward windows).
    - **Root cause, measured directly**: the QQQ/SPY spread's realized annualized vol is ~8.5% — roughly half a typical single ticker's (SPY/QQQ themselves run 15-20%+). `registry.py`'s shared bollinger/rsi grids are calibrated for normal single-ticker vol, so their band widths/RSI thresholds are simply too wide to get touched by a much calmer spread in some walk-forward windows.
    - **Built a separate, pair-specific tighter grid** (`scripts/pairs_trade_backtest_tight_grid.py`, new file) — roughly half the band width / a less extreme RSI oversold threshold, fixed in advance from the measured 8.5%-vol number and applied identically to all 3 pairs, not picked by trying values until `QQQ_SPY_PAIR` passed. `registry.py`'s shared grid (used by every live single-ticker combo) is completely untouched — confirmed the new cycle's `backtest_runs` rows only ever wrote the 3 `_PAIR` tickers, zero other rows touched.
    - **Honest result: fixing the frequency problem exposed a weaker real edge, not a hidden one.** Under the tighter grid, `QQQ_SPY_PAIR`'s windows-traded went from 1-2/4 to 3/4 (the frequency gate now passes) — but mean DSR fell from 0.69/0.74 to **0.38/0.48** (below the 0.5 threshold) and worst-OOS-Sharpe fell from 1.89/1.15 to **0.65/0.20** once the strategy had to trade consistently rather than catching a sparse pair of favorable setups. The earlier near-miss's apparent strength was concentrated in too few trades to mean much — properly evidenced, it doesn't clear DSR either. `XLE_USO_PAIR`/`GLD_SLV_PAIR` stayed clear negatives under the new grid too (still failing on genuine quality grounds, not a bug).
    - **Verdict: this is now a closed, honestly-tested negative** — 0 of 12 combos promote under either grid, across all 3 pairs. Doesn't rule out relative-value pairs trading on a *different* pair relationship in the future; these 3 specific ones are settled, not a near-miss anymore.

72. **Reviewed item 70's 5-survivor shortlist individually — 1 rejected on a real data-integrity finding, 2 added to paper trading with watch-flags, 1 held (2026-07-26)** — asked to pick the next thing to work on; picked the concrete, already-triaged-but-never-acted-on shortlist over starting the harder multiple-testing fix. Reviewed each of the 4 non-`COLM` survivors individually rather than trusting item 70's summary numbers at face value: full backtest + per-window walk-forward trade counts, ticker identity/sector via `yfinance`, dividend/split history, and correlation against all 9 currently-live combos.
    - **`AD` (Array Digital Infrastructure)/`bollinger_ls` — rejected, a real defect found, not just a caveat.** `AD` paid three enormous special dividends ($23, $10.25, $11 on a $30-60 stock — 20-45% of share price each). `data/fetch.py`'s `load()` returns unadjusted `close` (confirmed by reading the code), and `run_backtest()` never models dividend cash flows, so each payout appears as a fake single-day crash. Traced the actual trades (`pf.trades.records_readable`, not just summary stats): 2 of 114 backtested trades are **short** positions that close exactly on the ex-dividend date, harvesting ~$7,100 combined phantom profit from a price drop a real short-seller would owe out in dividend liability, not collect. Same category as the DYNR/XAR incidents (item 56) — a data-integrity artifact disguised as edge — different specific mechanism (unadjusted dividends vs. illiquidity/thin-evidence). Checked the other 4 survivors for the same pattern: none have anything close (`CRF` max single dividend $0.33, `COLM` $0.30, `DHC` $0.39; `BLCO` has never paid one) — this is `AD`-specific, not a universe-wide problem, though the underlying gap (unadjusted `close`, no dividend modeling) is real and generalizable, flagged as a follow-up below, not fixed here.
    - **`BLCO` (Bausch + Lomb)/`bollinger_ls` and `DHC` (Diversified Healthcare Trust)/`volume_breakout_ls` — added to paper trading, both with an explicit watch-flag.** `BLCO`: clean data, no dividend/split anomalies, but only ~4.5yrs of history (May 2022 IPO) — no 2017 or COVID-crash walk-forward window, same "watch, don't over-trust yet" category as `TIGO` (item 45). `DHC`: clean data, real trading volume throughout (not a DYNR-style fake-liquidity problem), but averaged $4.50/share over the last 2 years (min $2.04) — below the platform's own $5 minimum-price liquidity convention (item 56) for a real stretch of its walk-forward window, even though it's since recovered to $9.34 and clears every liquidity check today. Both verified end-to-end via `paper_trade.py` (fresh data fetch, clean signal, flat $10,000 start, exit 0) before being wired into `daily_paper_trade.sh` and `research_cycle.py`'s `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`. **10 combos now live.**
    - **`CRF` (Cornerstone Total Return Fund)/`turn_of_month` — deliberately left out for now, one specific worry ruled out.** Item 70 already flagged this as a mechanism mismatch: a closed-end fund's NAV/managed-distribution dynamics are a different thing than the ETF/index month-end flow effect `turn_of_month` was validated on. Checked directly whether that concern was actually a dividend-timing artifact like `AD`'s: pulled `CRF`'s full ex-dividend date history (139 distributions since 2015, monthly) and found they cluster on the 8th-17th of the month — squarely outside `turn_of_month`'s actual trading window (last 3 trading days of month through first 2 of next). That specific artifact doesn't apply here. The broader mechanism-mismatch concern from item 70 stands on its own regardless, so this stays a hold, not a rejection or an approval.
    - **Correlation checked against the full live roster before adding anything**: all 4 new candidates correlate at or below 0.23 with every one of the 9 already-live combos (`QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `XLP/bollinger`, `TIP/bollinger`, `FXI/bollinger_regime`, `XLRE/rsi`, `QQQ/breakout_ls`, `TIGO/sma_ls`, `COLM/bollinger_ls`) — well under the 0.7 correlation gate, so whichever survived would be genuinely diversifying, not a disguised duplicate of something already running.
    - **Real follow-up flagged, not fixed here**: the platform's backtest engine trades unadjusted `close` and never models dividend cash flows anywhere (`backtest/run.py`) — immaterial for normal-yield tickers (the ETF/index universe's regular quarterly distributions are a rounding error by comparison), but a real, previously-undetected gap for any future ticker with an unusually large special distribution. Worth a broader look eventually (switch to `adj_close`, or model dividend receipt/payment explicitly), but out of scope for this pass — `AD` was the only ticker in the current universe affected badly enough to matter, and it's already excluded.

73. **Item 72's "AD-specific, not universe-wide" call was wrong — audited it properly, fixed the root cause (2026-07-26)** — asked to (1) check whether any currently-live-paper or currently-PROMOTEd combo has the same unadjusted-`close`/dividend distortion as `AD`, and (2) actually fix it rather than flag it again.
    - **Audit, not a guess**: pulled the full live-paper-trading roster (`CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` in `research_cycle.py`, 11 combos/9 tickers — plus the separate `cand_*` combos tracked in `paper_trade_events` via `paper_trade_candidate.py`, 8 more tickers, 17 distinct tickers total) and every currently-`PROMOTE`d combo from the latest `promotion_snapshots` (1,272 combos, 924 tickers) — 931 distinct tickers in total. Pulled full dividend history for all 931 via `yfinance` in batches (matching `fetch_batch()`'s own 100-ticker chunking) and flagged any single dividend exceeding 5% of the prior day's close, the same mechanism that made `AD`'s specials look like crashes.
    - **Result: item 72's "AD-specific" conclusion was wrong.** Zero flags among the 17 live-paper tickers (good — no live capital is currently mismarked), but **48 of the 1,272 currently-`PROMOTE`d combos (924 tickers → 108 with at least one qualifying dividend, 29 of those since 2020) sit on a ticker with the same exposure** — `GREEL` (7 occurrences of a ~5-20%-of-price special), `AMSF`, `CMBT`, `LND`, `TRMD`, `IFS`, `CET`, `GRF`, `TWN`, `DHT`, `RIO`, `ECVT`, `TLF`, and more. Heavily skewed toward `bollinger`/`rsi` mean-reversion families (33 of 48) — exactly the strategies most likely to "buy the dip"/"short the spike" on a fake one-day move. Full ticker/strategy list and every flagged dividend event saved to the scratch audit (not in the repo).
    - **Fix chosen: switch `data/fetch.py`'s `load()` to return fully dividend/split-adjusted OHLC, not a separate dividend cash-flow model.** `load()` is the single choke point — confirmed by grep that every strategy, `backtest/run.py`, `risk/position_sizing.py`, `promotion.py`'s benchmark/own-asset comparisons, and every research/paper-trading script all get their price data through it and read `df["close"]` blind to adjustment. So the fix is entirely inside `load()`: compute each day's `adj_close/close` ratio and scale `open`/`high`/`low` by the same factor (not just `close`) so the whole bar stays internally consistent — a plain close-only swap would have left `high`/`low` unadjusted and created a smaller version of the same OHLC-inconsistency bug. Zero downstream code changes needed. Raw unadjusted close is still returned as `raw_close` for a caller that specifically wants the literal traded price (none currently do). Confirmed the most recent bar's `adj_close` always equals its raw `close` (nothing to adjust for after "today"), so live paper-trading execution prices are provably unaffected — only historical bars before an ex-dividend/split date change. The dividend-cash-flow-modeling alternative (explicitly crediting/debiting cash in `run_backtest()`) was rejected as more code, more surface area for a new bug, and non-standard versus the adjusted-series approach every real total-return backtest uses.
    - **Verified, not just asserted.** `AD`/`bollinger_ls`'s full-history default-param backtest: total return drops from 295.7% → 181.8% and trade count 114 → 113 — the exact effect of removing the phantom-profit short trade item 72 found (~$7,100 on a $10k backtest ≈ the ~114-point return drop). `QQQ` and `SPY` (no crash-sized dividends, just decades of small regular ones): `close` values differ by up to 17.4% (SPY) at the oldest dates — expected and correct, since raw `close` has always understated total return by not reinvesting dividends, not a bug — and `sma`/`bollinger`/`rsi` default-param stats moved in the expected direction (return up, Sharpe up, trade count basically unchanged) with no sign flips or broken runs. Ran the same full-history default-param comparison (old raw-close vs. new adjusted) for all 48 affected `PROMOTE`d combos, read-only, no DB writes: **zero Sharpe sign flips** — magnitudes moved both directions (`AD`, `CIX`, `DSWL`, `GRF`, `TLF` weaker; `CMBT`, `LND`, `TDG`, `TRMD`, `TWN`, `WHG` stronger), consistent with removing some phantom dividend-crash "profit" while correctly adding some previously-missing dividend reinvestment.
    - **What this check does *not* cover**: the actual `PROMOTE`/`HOLD` decision is driven by walk-forward + DSR across each combo's own grid-searched params, not the family default used above, and re-running that properly for 48 combos means writing fresh `backtest_runs`/`walk_forward_windows`/`promotion_snapshots` rows — skipped deliberately rather than writing to the shared production DB while `agent_ecosystem.py`'s live pipeline was actively running against the same file mid-session (real lock contention observed throughout this session, not theoretical). The next natural `research_cycle.py` run now pulls corrected adjusted prices automatically (same `load()` choke point) and will produce an authoritative refreshed verdict for all 48 — genuinely worth doing soon given the number of `bollinger`/`rsi` combos affected, but not run here.

74. **Investigated whether approved research-agent candidates were being silently skipped by paper trading — corrected an earlier wrong claim mid-investigation, found one real (occasional) failure mode, fixed it (2026-07-26)** — noticed 10 of 16 approved candidates had zero `paper_trade_events` rows and initially concluded the daily automation had stopped running them. That conclusion was wrong, and worth recording the correction plainly rather than quietly fixing the text: `paper_trade_events` only logs actual entry/exit trades, not routine "still flat" checks, so zero rows means "never opened a position," not "never checked." Re-checked properly by grepping the actual log files for each candidate: every one of them, including the two strongest agent-discovered strategies (`VRTX`, `APLD` from item 72), had in fact been checked repeatedly and correctly — just legitimately flat.
    - **What actually is real, found the same way**: reading the raw logs (not just file timestamps, which was a second dead end — a file's last-modified time reflects *any* write that day, including unrelated mailbox-triggered candidate checks, not the scheduled run) showed the real 17:00 launchd trigger fired correctly and completed its full roster on 2026-07-22, -23 (self-recovered from one `db.py` lock-conflict failure on a single combo), and -25 (completed all 9 fixed combos plus a full 13-candidate approved-candidates backfill, `VRTX`/`APLD` included). But on 2026-07-24, the run fired at 17:00:05, completed only its first combo (`QQQ/sma`), and then produced nothing else for hours — no traceback, no error, consistent with the Mac going to sleep mid-script and silently killing the rest of the process tree (the already-documented laptop-sleep risk from item 17, now confirmed to actually occur, not just theoretical).
    - **Two fixes, scoped to what was actually found**: (1) `scheduling/com.quantplatform.papertrade.plist`'s `RunAtLoad` flipped `false → true` — `daily_paper_trade.sh`'s own checks are explicitly idempotent against the latest available bar, so an extra run on every login/reboot is harmless, and it means a silently-killed run gets caught up at the next login instead of waiting a full day. Reinstalled to `~/Library/LaunchAgents/`, reloaded via `launchctl unload`/`load`, verified live: fired immediately on reload and completed correctly. (2) New `scripts/check_paper_trade_freshness.py` — detects specifically "a run started but never reached the end of its roster," which neither a missing log file nor a missing `paper_trade_events` row reveals (a killed-mid-run day still has a normal-looking, if short, log). Parses the *current* last fixed combo straight out of `daily_paper_trade.sh` itself (rather than hardcoding one) so it keeps working as the roster grows — an earlier draft hardcoded `COLM/bollinger_ls` and wrongly flagged 2026-07-22/23 as failures, since those days' (shorter, pre-`COLM`) roster could never have reached an anchor that didn't exist yet. Verified against the known ground truth: correctly flags only 2026-07-24, nothing else, once the anchor was fixed.
    - **One separate, smaller thing noticed in passing, not fixed here**: `DVMT`'s candidate (`cand_f60d9254eb`) checks against a price bar dated 2018-12-27 — its underlying data hasn't updated in ~7 years, so this one candidate isn't really being watched live at all, just repeatedly re-confirming ancient stale data. Likely delisted or otherwise stopped trading; worth a quick look, not urgent.
    - **Separately, item 73 (a background session run in parallel this same afternoon) found and fixed something bigger while this was happening**: `data/fetch.py`'s `load()` now returns dividend/split-adjusted prices universe-wide, after that session's own audit found item 72's "AD is the only one affected" conclusion was wrong — 48 currently-`PROMOTE`d combos across 29 tickers carried the same unadjusted-dividend distortion. Verified there with no Sharpe sign flips and zero live-paper-traded tickers affected.
