A `research_cycle.py` re-run against the now-corrected data is still pending (flagged in "Suggested next steps") and will produce the authoritative refreshed verdict for those 48 — genuinely worth doing soon.

75. **Built the multiple-testing correction flagged since items 49/168 — and it reveals current backtest-only validation can't prove anything at this search scale, real strategies included (2026-07-26)** — the "harder statistical fix," picked over more strategy-building as this session's second half. DSR (`validation/deflated_sharpe.py`) corrects each combo for the small parameter grid searched *within* it, but has zero awareness that thousands of *other* (ticker, strategy) combos were also searched for any pass at all — the gap named repeatedly but never built.
    - **New `validation/multiple_testing.py`**: `benjamini_hochberg(p_values, q)` implements Benjamini-Hochberg false discovery rate control (the standard fix for exactly this — same method genomics uses for testing thousands of genes at once); `dsr_to_pvalue()` converts a combo's mean DSR back to the one-sided p-value it's actually built from (DSR = P(true Sharpe > chance level), so p = 1 - DSR). Bonferroni (control the chance of *any* false discovery, family-wide) considered and rejected as needlessly strict at this scale in favor of FDR (control the *expected proportion* of false discoveries among what's called significant) — the standard tradeoff for large-scale screening. Verified against known cases before touching real data: a textbook 10-p-value example reproduces the standard result exactly; a synthetic 14,000-test batch with genuinely strong signal (p~1e-6 to 1e-4) correctly recovers all 50 real signals with ~9% false positives (target was 10%); pure noise at the same scale correctly recovers ~0.
    - **Wired into `promotion.py` as a third, optional pass** (`apply_fdr_correction: bool = False, fdr_q: float = 0.10`), same rollout convention as every prior new gate (`beats_benchmark`, `require_beat_own_asset`) — off by default, so every existing caller (`build_report.py`, `paper_trade.py`, `research_cycle.py`) sees byte-identical verdicts to before. Confirmed: default-args call still returns exactly 1,272 PROMOTE combos, matching item 73's own count precisely.
    - **The real result, run three ways**: across every combo ever stored (95,185 rows, the platform's entire multi-week history) at the standard `q=0.10`, **zero** survive. Scoped down to just the original, deliberately-curated 32-ticker universe (1,043 combos, item 20-27's universe — a fairer test than the speculative 876-stock expansion, since this is genuinely one coherent search rather than several different episodes lumped together) — still **zero**, even at `q=0.50` (a tolerance for 50% false discoveries among anything called significant, about as lenient as FDR control can meaningfully get). This includes every currently-live paper-traded combo: `XLP/bollinger` (mean DSR 0.869, a strong number by the platform's own existing standard) still reads `fdr_correction (not a significant discovery among 1043 combos tested at q=0.1)`.
    - **Why, precisely — not just "everything failed"**: traced to walk-forward's own window size. Each out-of-sample test window covers ~200 trading days (confirmed directly from `walk_forward_windows`). A genuinely good, realistic strategy with a true annualized Sharpe of 1.0 would produce a per-window z-score of only ~0.89 (p≈0.19) purely from that sample size — nowhere near the p≈1e-4 (at n=1,043) or p≈1e-6 (at n=95,185) that real correction demands before calling anything "significant." **This means the finding isn't "these strategies don't work" — it's that ~200 daily return observations can never produce a small enough p-value to survive correction across even a modest search, regardless of whether the underlying edge is real.** DSR's 0.5 threshold was only ever a meaningful bar in isolation (one ticker, no look-elsewhere problem); at the scale this platform now searches, no backtest statistic from windows this short can conclusively prove anything.
    - **What this does and doesn't change**: doesn't retroactively invalidate any combo's real, already-accumulating forward *paper-trading* record — that evidence isn't subject to this bias at all, since future days can't be re-selected after the fact the way a backtest parameter grid can. It does mean a bare `PROMOTE` verdict, exactly as computed today, should never be read as "statistically proven real edge" — it's better understood as "cleared an initial, necessarily noisy screen, worth the free/reversible cost of watching forward," which is precisely the "propose, a human decides, paper trading is the real test" pattern this platform already runs on, just now with an honest number attached to how weak the backtest-only claim really is. **Resolved same day**: `evaluate_promotion()` now always computes and returns `fdr_p_value`/`fdr_significant` as reporting columns on every call, regardless of arguments -- cheap (pure arithmetic on already-computed `mean_dsr`, no extra data fetching), so it's on `build_report.py`'s HTML table and every other caller's output without anyone needing to remember to ask. A new `fdr_gates_promotion` param (default `False`, same rollout convention as `own_asset_gates_promotion`) controls whether it actually blocks `PROMOTE` -- off by default, so the 1,272-combo `PROMOTE` count for every existing caller is confirmed byte-identical to before this shipped; passing `fdr_gates_promotion=True` reproduces the 0-survivor result above exactly.

76. **Two small dashboard fixes: robot mascots rendering over open panels, and a new "what is this thing?" plain-English overview on the Quant Engine hub (2026-07-26)** — noticed while reviewing `FMCC` in the mailbox.
    - **Z-index overlap bug, fixed**: `agent_ecosystem.py`'s room mascots get an inline `z-index` up to ~200 (`moveRobotTo()`'s painter's-algorithm depth sort against room furniture), but the detail side panel (`#panel`) was only `z-index: 10` and the mailbox/signal-feed modal overlay only `z-index: 20` — both well below that ceiling, so a robot near the front of its room would render on top of open panel/modal text instead of behind it. Bumped both to `z-index: 500`. Verified live in the browser (not just read the CSS): reopened the exact `Stock Agent` panel from the user's screenshot, robots no longer visible through it; opened the Signal Desk modal too, same fix applies there.
    - **New: click the Quant Engine hub for a plain-English project overview**, rebuilt from live data on every click rather than a static blurb. New `_overview_payload()` (deliberately cheap — reuses `_paper_trader_roster()` and plain `COUNT(*)` queries, not `evaluate_promotion()`'s 95,000+-row scan, which is known to take many minutes under real load) feeds a new `/api/overview` endpoint; a new modal renders it as a handful of short, jargon-free sentences (no trading/quant background assumed) covering: what the platform is trying to do, that zero real money is involved anywhere right now, how many strategies are currently being watched with fake money and whether they're ahead or behind, and how many total ideas have been generated (today's count too) vs. how many still need a human decision. Verified live: clicked the hub, got a correct, current read (27 live strategies, all flat, 4,390 ideas ever tried, 1,547 generated just today, 0 awaiting review); confirmed the close button and the new z-index fix both work together (no robot bleed-through on this modal either).
    - **Extended same day**: asked to make it more in-depth without losing the plain-English tone — added a "kinds of ideas it tries" list and an "other things running behind the scenes" list, both still zero-jargon. The strategy list is built dynamically (`_strategy_families_overview()`, reusing the already-existing `_strategy_base_family()` helper to collapse `_ls`/`_regime`/`_macro` variants into one entry per core idea, matched against a small curated plain-English description dict, `STRATEGIES` registry keys) rather than hardcoded prose, so a new strategy family added later shows up automatically (just its name, if undescribed) instead of the list silently going stale. The "other systems" list (Research Agent, Stock Agent, Congress/insider copying, pairs trading) is static prose, since those are stable structural facts about the platform, not numbers that change day to day. Verified live in the browser: all 8 current strategy families and all 4 other-systems bullets render correctly, scrolls cleanly inside the modal.

77. **Pulled `FMCC` from paper trading, and built a real generalization gate into the Research Agent so this doesn't need catching by hand again (2026-07-26)** — asked to remove the candidate, and to make the pipeline itself require a strategy work broadly, not just on the one ticker it happened to be invented on.
    - **`FMCC` removal**: closed the open long position at the same $5.54 print it entered at (no newer trading day's price existed yet — a real, not fabricated, zero-P&L close, same pattern as item 59's `XAR`/`CBZ`), and marked `human_decision='rejected'` with full notes citing the generalization test result. It stops appearing in `daily_paper_trade.sh`'s approved-candidates loop automatically (that loop filters on `human_decision='approved'`), no code change needed there.
    - **New gate: `check_generalization()`** (`scripts/research_agent.py`) — re-runs a candidate's *exact* class and default params, completely unchanged, as a full-history backtest (not another walk-forward pass, to keep it cheap) against a fixed panel of 6 tickers spanning stocks and crypto (`SPY`, `QQQ`, `AAPL`, `JPM`, `BTC-USD`, `ETH-USD`, minus the screening ticker itself if it's a panel member). Wired in as a new pipeline stage, right after the existing parameter-stability check and before a candidate can reach `pending_human_review` — deliberately the *last* gate, since it's only reached by the small number of candidates (a handful a day) that already cleared every cheaper filter, so re-running several extra backtests here is cheap in aggregate even though it wouldn't be applied to the full ~1,500-2,900/day firehose.
    - **Calibrated the pass bar with a real false-positive check, not just the one true-positive case**: first version required a panel ticker to be both profitable *and* clear the platform's own risk gate — immediately caught on the platform's own `SmaCrossoverStrategy` (real, currently-live `QQQ/sma`): genuinely profitable on 5 of 5 testable panel tickers, but only cleared the risk gate on 2, purely because `AAPL`/`BTC-USD`/`ETH-USD` are naturally more volatile assets than `QQQ`, not because the trend-following edge wasn't showing up there too. Loosened to profitability alone (majority of the testable panel, `MIN_GENERALIZATION_PASSES_FRACTION = 0.5`) — the screening ticker's own risk gate (an earlier, existing stage) already separately guards against proposing something too risky to trade; conflating that with "does the edge generalize" was rejecting real strategies for the wrong reason. Re-verified both cases after the fix: `FMCC`'s exact code still correctly fails (2 of 6 panel tickers profitable, needed 3), `SmaCrossoverStrategy` on `QQQ` now correctly passes (5 of 5).
    - **A real bug caught in verification, not production**: `stats["total_return_pct"]` is a numpy scalar (vectorbt), so `> 0` produces a `numpy.bool_`, not a native Python `bool` — confirmed directly this crashes `json.dumps()` inside `update_candidate()`'s write of the new `generalization_detail` column (no `default=str` fallback there) for any candidate where a negative-return panel ticker's comparison short-circuits on that value before ever reaching the already-native-bool risk-gate result. Fixed with explicit `float()`/`bool()`/`int()` casts on every stored value; re-verified with the exact same strict `json.dumps()` call `update_candidate()` makes, no crash.
    - **New DB columns** `generalization_passed`/`generalization_detail` on `strategy_candidates` (migration-safe `_ensure_columns`, same pattern as `store/paper.py`/`store/results.py`) and a new `final_status` value, `rejected_not_generalizable`. Verified end-to-end (not just the isolated function): ran the real `FMCC` source through the complete `process_candidate()` pipeline and got `final_status: rejected_not_generalizable` exactly as expected; restarted the live `research_agent_loop.sh` service (`launchctl kickstart -k`) and confirmed it's running the new code cleanly (exit code 0 across several real candidates in the log, no tracebacks) — none have reached the generalization stage yet in the few minutes since restart, which is expected given how rarely a candidate clears every earlier gate.

78. **Closed the loop on item 73's dividend-adjustment fix: fixed the item-49 connection-reuse bug along the way, then re-ran the 31 affected tickers under corrected data for authoritative verdicts (2026-07-26)** — after a deep dive into pairs trading (item 79, below) came up empty again, picked this over more strategy-hunting: cheap, already-scoped, and it affects trust in the *existing* PROMOTE list rather than adding one more speculative new thing.
    - **Fixed the connection-reuse bug first**, since it was flagged as a real risk to running this at all ("Suggested next steps," originally item 49: 93,712 combos once turned a should-be-fast summary pass into 6+ hours). New `promotion_snapshot_history_batch()` (`store/results.py`) replaces `research_cycle.py`'s "what changed since last cycle" loop's one-connection-per-combo calls to `promotion_snapshot_history()` with a single batched query (a DuckDB window function, same shape of result, one round trip instead of len(combos) of them) — the identical fix pattern already used once for this exact bottleneck class in `promotion.py`'s `_benchmark_returns_by_run_id()`. Smoke-tested on `QQQ` (42 combos, 5.4s, correct output) before trusting it on the real run.
    - **Regenerated the affected-ticker list directly from already-stored data**, since item 73's own list was saved to a scratch file, not the repo: every ticker's `close`/`adj_close` ratio (both already stored per bar) reveals exactly where a dividend or split occurred and how large it was, with zero new `yfinance` calls needed. Found 31 tickers (close to item 73's 29 — this method also catches splits, not just dividends, among the 924 then-`PROMOTE`d tickers).
    - **Real result, not just "the bug got fixed"**: re-running `research_cycle.py` for those 31 tickers (620 combos, 518s, no lock/connection issues) produced **44 real changes** — **27 combos correctly demoted** (`PROMOTE → HOLD`; they were only ever promoted because of the old unadjusted-dividend distortion -- includes `AD/bollinger_regime`, confirming the same root cause found for `AD/bollinger_ls` in item 72 also touched other strategies on the same ticker) and **17 combos newly promoted** (`HOLD → PROMOTE`; their real edge had been masked by the same bug in the opposite direction). Verified none of the 31 flagged tickers overlap with the actual live paper-trading roster (`QQQ`, `XLP`, `TIP`, `FXI`, `XLRE`, `TIGO`, `COLM`, `BLCO`, `DHC`) -- confirms item 73's original "zero live-paper impact" finding held.
    - **Not acted on further** -- 17 new candidates now show real `PROMOTE` verdicts under corrected data, same "propose, don't auto-act" pattern as everything else; whether any belong in paper trading is a separate, future decision.

79. **Widened the pairs-trading search from 3 to 15 pairs, still an honest zero -- but with a real, substantive near-miss investigated and explained (2026-07-26)** — asked to look more broadly at pairs trading (items 42/71) and find something tradeable. Added 12 same-industry equity pairs (the classic pairs-trading playbook -- Coke/Pepsi, Visa/Mastercard, the big banks, etc.), screened first by real return correlation (not guessed): all 12 cleared a real bar (0.41-0.85), the 5 strongest (`V/MA` 0.85, `JPM/BAC` 0.85, `GS/MS` 0.84, `XOM/CVX` 0.83, `HD/LOW` 0.80) noticeably tighter than any of the original 3 ETF pairs. New `scripts/pairs_trade_backtest_expanded.py`, same unmodified pipeline as `pairs_trade_backtest.py`.
    - **Result: 0 of 48 new combos promote, same as the original 3 pairs.** Two real near-misses, each investigated rather than just noted: `GS_MS_PAIR/bollinger_ls` looked like the strongest result of the whole 15-pair search -- every out-of-sample walk-forward window positive (unlike the earlier `QQQ_SPY_PAIR` near-miss, which failed on trade frequency) -- but its full-history default-param backtest hits a real **-27.7% drawdown that isn't a sharp crash, it's a slow 4-year grinding loss from October 2018 to December 2022** that the walk-forward's shorter, scattered test windows happened to mostly miss. This is the platform's own by-design convention working correctly (item 18: the full-history risk gate's param set stays fixed, deliberately not derived from what a search prefers, precisely so a real bad stretch can't be dodged this way) -- not a technicality worth arguing with, a real risk a human should take seriously.
    - **Honest overall verdict after 15 pairs, multiple strategies, and a tighter-parameter variant (item 71) all tried**: pairs trading with this toolkit -- correlation-picked pairs, `bollinger`/`rsi` mean-reversion reused from the single-ticker universe -- does not have an easy edge in this data. Further pair-hunting has diminishing returns; the next real lever would be a structurally different approach (actual cointegration testing instead of correlation, and/or a strategy shape purpose-built for spreads), not another sweep of more pairs through the same method.

80. **Found and fixed why the Research Agent looked like it was just recycling the same few strategies onto different tickers -- a real bug in its own novelty check, not a vague "small model is repetitive" shrug (2026-07-28)** — asked directly whether the agent was inventing new strategies or just sweeping a handful of ideas across the universe; verified rather than assumed.
    - **Confirmed the suspicion was real, with hard numbers**: across the last 5,000 stored candidates, `class_name` distribution was `VolatilityRegimeShiftStrategy` 3,596 (72%) and `VolumePriceDivergenceStrategy` 1,151 (23%) -- 95% of all output collapsed onto two names, everything else (`RelativeStrengthStrategy`, `VortexStrategy`, etc.) a one-off. But `source_sha256` showed 3,549 *distinct* source files among the 3,596 `VolatilityRegimeShiftStrategy` rows, and diffing an early one against a recent one confirmed genuinely different rolling-window math and thresholds each time -- so it wasn't literally copy-pasting, just converging hard on the same two conceptual templates under cosmetically different code.
    - **Root cause, found in the code, not guessed**: `scripts/research_agent.py`'s generation loop built the "propose something mechanically different from all of these" list the LLM sees as `existing_names = list(STRATEGIES.keys())` -- only the 8 hand-built strategies in the registry (`sma`, `breakout`, `bollinger`, `rsi`, ...). It never included any name the Research Agent itself had already generated, so every prompt was stateless with respect to its own history -- the model never saw "you've already proposed VolatilityRegimeShiftStrategy 3,596 times." Compounding it, `prompts.py`'s own instructions use *"volatility regime shifts, volume-price divergence, ..."* as illustrative example categories, which the local model (hermes3, via Ollama) appears to have anchored on as the literal assignment.
    - **Fix**: new `distinct_class_names()` in `store/candidates.py` (DB-level `SELECT DISTINCT class_name`, cheap and bounded -- distinct names stay in the dozens even as the candidate table grows into the thousands). `research_agent.py`'s `existing_names` now merges this with `STRATEGIES.keys()` (61 extra names as of this fix) before building the prompt.
    - **Verified two ways, not just read the diff**: (1) isolated A/B test, same model/ticker context -- old prompt (5 calls) reproduced the exact historical pattern (3x `VolatilityRegimeShiftStrategy`, 1x `VolumePriceDivergenceStrategy`); new prompt (5 calls) got 5/5 distinct names, including mechanisms never seen before (`MultiTimeframeConvergenceStrategy`, `VolatilityClusterStrategy`). (2) Confirmed live in production, not just the test script -- the always-on `research_agent_loop.sh` (already running continuously, no restart needed since it shells out to a fresh Python process each cycle) picked up the fix on its very next iteration; the 10 real candidates generated immediately after were 4 distinct names with zero repeats of the two old attractors.
    - **Honest caveat, not claimed as a full fix**: diversity isn't perfect call-to-call -- the model still sometimes repeats a *new* name a few times in a row (`VolatilityClusterStrategy` 4x in the first 10 post-fix candidates) before that name lands in the exclusion list and gets suppressed too, since the list only grows as candidates finish and get recorded. Expected to keep smoothing out as more names accumulate. This is a real, measurable fix to the actual bug (the agent having no memory of its own output) -- not a guarantee of novelty on every single call.

81. **Built the first three steps of a real "graduation path" from fake-money paper trading toward a small live-capital pilot -- evidence bar, dashboard visibility, and a human decision gate (2026-07-28)** — asked what would make the platform actually profitable rather than just accumulating more backtested ideas; agreed the highest-leverage move is a defined path for *acting* on the one signal that isn't subject to item 75's look-elsewhere bias (real forward paper-trading evidence, since future days can't be re-selected after the fact). Deliberately stopped here for this session -- broker integration, real capital, and a live kill-switch (steps 4-6) need an actual brokerage account/API key (which can't be entered on the user's behalf) and a real trade (which can't be executed on the user's behalf either), so those stay a separate, explicitly-gated next conversation rather than something rolled through automatically.
    - **New `graduation.py`** (`evaluate_graduation()`): a fixed, deliberately conservative bar -- 5+ closed trades, 60+ days watched, non-negative forward Sharpe (per-period/daily, unannualized, same convention as `validation/deflated_sharpe.py`), and no breach of the same -25% drawdown limit `risk/limits.py` already uses at backtest time. Same criteria-dict/reasons-list shape as `promotion.evaluate_criteria()`, so it reads the same way next to a PROMOTE verdict. Kept fully separate from PROMOTE on purpose -- that's a backtest-only screen; this is real forward evidence, and conflating the two would undercut exactly the distinction item 75 established.
    - **New `store/graduation.py`**: an insert-only `graduation_decisions` log (same append-only convention as `store/paper.py`/`store/results.py`) keyed by (ticker, strategy_name), so a human can revisit a combo and log a new call later without losing the earlier reasoning. Each decision is tagged with a snapshot of `evaluate_graduation()`'s numbers at that moment, so "why did we graduate this" stays answerable after the live numbers move on. `latest_decisions_batch()` uses the same window-function, one-connection pattern already fixed once for this exact bottleneck class (item 49/78) -- built in from the start here instead of found the hard way at scale a second time.
    - **Dashboard wiring in `agent_ecosystem.py`**: the paper-trader roster (polled every few seconds) now shows a cheap, price-data-free progress teaser per combo (`_graduation_progress()` -- trades and days watched only, no price fetch, to keep the hot polling path cheap); clicking into a combo's detail panel runs the full four-criteria `evaluate_graduation()` (reusing the price series the panel already fetches for its equity curve, no redundant load) plus three decision buttons (Approve for live pilot / Not yet / Reject) styled identically to the mailbox's existing approve/hold/deny pattern, posting to a new `/api/graduation_decide` endpoint. Recording a decision here is bookkeeping only -- same as every other `human_decision` column in this codebase, it never itself moves money or places an order.
    - **A real pre-existing display bug found and fixed along the way**: the combo-detail modal's `.row` styling (`#panel .row`) was scoped only to the agent-status side panel, not the combo-detail modal it's actually used in -- every label/value pair in the "currently open" position block had been rendering with no spacing (`directionlong`, `entry$158.43...`) since that section was built, unnoticed until the new graduation rows sat right next to it. Fixed by extending the existing rule to `#combo-detail-body .row` too, which corrects both the pre-existing position block and the new graduation section in one change.
    - **Verified thoroughly, not just "the code runs"**: unit-level smoke tests of `evaluate_graduation()`/`record_decision()`/`latest_decision()` against real combos before touching the dashboard; confirmed the live launchd-managed dashboard service (`com.quantplatform.agentecosystem`) actually picks up source changes on `launchctl kickstart -k` (it's a long-running process, unlike `research_agent_loop.sh`'s fresh-process-per-cycle, so this needed an explicit restart); hit the real HTTP endpoints with `curl` (roster, combo detail, and an invalid-decision-value rejection on `/api/graduation_decide`); then drove the actual browser UI end-to-end -- opened the Paper Trader panel, confirmed the progress teasers render on every roster row, clicked into `VRTX/cand_b903dee887`'s detail panel, clicked "Not yet", confirmed the decision saved and the panel's "You marked this..." line updated immediately. That real test decision was deleted afterward (`grad_bad75ad68f`) so the decision log only reflects genuine human review, not a UI smoke test.
    - **Honest state of the evidence**: as expected given how flat the roster has been (see item 76's 27/27 combos flat at the time), nothing clears the bar yet -- every combo checked so far reads `NOT_YET`, mostly failing on trade count and days-watched rather than the return-quality criteria. This stage was about building the plumbing and making the gap honestly visible, not about anything being ready today.

82. **Added a total profit/loss summary to the top of the Paper Trader panel -- realized and unrealized, both in dollars and percent (2026-07-28)** — asked directly for an aggregate view instead of having to read 29 individual roster rows to gauge how the whole roster is doing.
    - **New `_paper_trader_pl_summary()`** (`agent_ecosystem.py`): realized P&L sums `paper_equity_summary()`'s own `realized_equity` across every combo against its $10,000 start (the same total `_overview_payload()` already computes for the Quant Engine hub's blurb, just broken into dollars too and surfaced directly in this panel). Unrealized P&L only counts combos with something open right now, and is computed as real capital-at-risk -- `realized_equity * size_fraction * (unrealized_return_pct / 100)` per position, since `size_fraction` (`store/paper.py`) is the actual fraction of a combo's equity deployed, not its full $10,000 notional. The aggregate unrealized percentage is deliberately expressed against total capital *deployed* in open positions, not against $10,000 x combo count -- a blended return on what's actually working right now, not diluted by undeployed cash sitting flat in combos that haven't signaled.
    - **New `/api/paper_pl_summary` endpoint**, rendered as two stat tiles at the top of the panel (above Open Positions) -- color-coded green/red by sign, same convention as every other return figure in this dashboard.
    - **Verified live, not just computed**: confirmed the real numbers by hand against the roster (`realized_dollars` $97.54 matches the one closed `VRTX` trade's own +1% return on $10,000; `unrealized_dollars` $274.20 against $35,277 of capital actually deployed across the 9 then-open positions, +0.8% blended), then restarted the live dashboard service and drove the actual browser to confirm the tiles render correctly with real data, not just that the endpoint returns a number.
    - **Extended same day: added an S&P 500 comparison** — asked directly to see the roster's return next to SPY's. New `_spy_benchmark_comparison()` computes what the same money would be worth if every combo's $10,000 start had instead just bought and held SPY from that exact combo's own first paper-trading event date to now -- each combo measured against SPY over the real stretch of calendar time it's actually been running, not one shared window, mirroring `promotion.py`'s own `benchmark_return_pct()` fairness convention (same date range as what's being compared) but applied to live forward windows instead of backtest ones. `portfolio_now_dollars` folds realized + unrealized back into one honest mark-to-market total before comparing, since SPY's own figure is always a live mark.
    - **A real, previously-invisible data-staleness bug found in the process**: the comparison initially came back exactly 0.0% for every combo. Traced to stored SPY data being 4 real days stale (last bar 2026-07-23) against combos that had started paper-trading 2026-07-27 -- SPY was never itself a paper-traded combo, so nothing had ever kept it refreshed the way `paper_trade.py` refreshes every other ticker it checks. Every *existing* SPY comparison in this codebase (the mailbox's beat-SPY badge) is against a historical, already-elapsed backtest window, so this gap was invisible until a comparison needed "as of today" for the first time. Fixed at the root: `daily_paper_trade.sh` now refreshes SPY's full price history first, once a day, before checking any combo -- same daily cadence every other ticker already gets, not a special case. Verified by manually running the refresh (2,908 rows, current through 2026-07-28) and re-checking the comparison: SPY correctly showed +0.09% over the relevant windows, portfolio +0.13%, beating by $119.23 -- hand-verified against SPY's own stored close (739.09 -> 740.86, +0.24% over `VRTX`'s specific one-day window) before trusting the aggregate.

83. **Added a confirmation prompt before Approve/Hold/Deny in the mailbox actually fires (2026-07-28)** — asked directly after noticing a click had no "are you sure" step. `decide()` (`agent_ecosystem.py`'s frontend) now calls `window.confirm()` first, with wording specific to the decision -- Approve's prompt explicitly repeats that it starts real paper-trading with fake money right away (the one decision with an immediate side effect), Hold/Deny's just note that it only records the call. Canceling returns immediately, before `mailInFlight` is even set -- no request, no UI state change, nothing to undo. Verified by scripting both paths directly in the live browser console rather than fighting pixel-perfect clicks on the canvas-drawn mailbox: overrode `window.confirm` to return `false` and confirmed `decide()` exits before touching `mailInFlight` or firing a request; overrode it to return `true` (with `fetch` mocked so no real candidate got touched) and confirmed it proceeds through its normal save/refresh flow. Scoped to the mailbox's three-button candidate decision only, not the separate graduation-to-live-pilot buttons (item 81) -- different feature, different wording ("Approve for live pilot"/"Not yet"/"Reject"), not what was asked for here.
    - **Extended same day: same confirmation added to the graduation buttons (item 81)** — asked directly to bring the two in line. `decideGraduation()` gets the identical `window.confirm()` gate, with its own honest wording -- "Approved for live pilot"'s prompt explicitly clarifies it only records a call and doesn't place a real trade or move money, since (unlike the mailbox's Approve) live execution isn't built yet; Not yet/Reject match the mailbox's plain "just records your call" wording. Verified the same way: scripted both the cancel path (confirmed `decideGraduation()` exits before `fetch` is ever called) and the confirm path (mocked `fetch`, confirmed it proceeds to POST the correct `{ticker, strategy_name, decision, notes}` payload to `/api/graduation_decide`) directly in the live browser console against a fake, nonexistent ticker/strategy so no real decision record was touched either way.

84. **Wired "copy Congress" into a real DSR/FDR-corrected evaluation -- one senator survives at the platform's standard bar, five survive at the looser "aggressive" bar (2026-07-28)** — asked to take the idea seriously as a genuinely different information edge (not more price-technical search, see item 75) and actually check whether it survives, rather than trust the 5-6 one-off `congress_copy_backtest.py` runs sitting in `congress_copy_backtests` since 2026-07-24.
    - **Why this couldn't just reuse `validation/walk_forward.py` unchanged**: `congress_copy.py`'s own docstring already explains why -- a senator's disclosed trades are sparse and event-driven (a handful to a couple thousand a year, across however many tickers), not a dense per-bar signal; slicing that into several chronological windows (walk-forward's whole mechanism) would starve an already-thin sample further. What still transfers directly, because it isn't window-shaped: DSR (corrects a *selected* result for how many variants were searched -- here, "which (lag_days, position_fraction) combo looks best for this member," not "which combo looks best on this time window"), the full-history risk gate, and the beats-SPY check. New `congress_copy_promotion.py` runs a small 6-combo grid (`lag_days` in 1/2/3, `position_fraction` in 3%/5%) per member, full-history (no windowing), DSR-corrects the best one against its own grid, then runs Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction *across every member tested* -- the same look-elsewhere problem item 75 found for the price-technical universe, applied here from the start instead of discovered as a gap later.
    - **Scoped to Senate only** (House's only source, FMP's forward-only feed, has covered ~2 weeks as of this writing -- nowhere near enough evidence), and to the 16-18 members with >=30 disclosed Purchase/Sale rows -- cheap pre-filter before any backtest runs. Every individual trial stored via the already-existing `store/congress_copy.record_run()` (previously used for only a handful of manual runs); the batch-level DSR/FDR verdict gets a new `congress_copy_evaluations` table (`store/congress_copy.py`), same "insert-only, nothing overwritten" convention as the rest of this store layer.
    - **A real bug caught in verification, not production** -- same class as item 77's: `beats_benchmark`/`sufficient_evidence`/`risk_passed` trace back through pandas/numpy arithmetic, so a bare comparison produced `numpy.bool_`, which DuckDB's parameter binding rejects outright. First real run crashed on it immediately; fixed with explicit `bool()` casts on every stored criterion before it was trusted on real data.
    - **The honest result at the platform's standard -25% drawdown gate**: 17 of ~18 eligible members produced a usable result; exactly **one survives**, Kelly Loeffler (+302% vs SPY's own +143% over the same active window, -16.8% worst drawdown, DSR 0.9996, p=0.0004 -- comfortably significant even after correcting for testing 17 people at once). Everyone else fails on drawdown, on trailing SPY outright, or (Wyden, King) on FDR significance itself.
    - **Re-run same day at the user's request, at this platform's already-established "aggressive" risk profile** (`research_cycle_aggressive.py`'s own -50%-drawdown convention, not a new one invented here) -- **five survive**: Loeffler, Pat Roberts (+453% vs SPY's +337%), Sheldon Whitehouse (+383% vs +337%), John Hoeven (+348% vs +337%), Jerry Moran (+230% vs +190%). Deliberately did NOT loosen DSR, FDR, or beats-benchmark alongside the drawdown gate -- matching `research_cycle_aggressive.py`'s own explicit design ("an edge still has to be statistically real, not just risk-tolerant"), since the user's stated preference was specifically about risk tolerance, not about abandoning "is this actually real."
    - **The finding that actually matters more than the drawdown question**: none of these five have any disclosed-trade data past 2020-2021 in this dataset. Loeffler's gap is permanent (she lost her Senate seat and will never file another disclosure). Roberts, Whitehouse, Hoeven, and Moran are a different, more fixable situation -- three of them are still real, sitting senators today, but the free Senate data source this platform uses (a GitHub-hosted historical archive) simply stopped being updated around 2020-2021. So right now, **none of these five could actually be wired into live paper trading** -- there's no current stream of new disclosures to follow for any of them, regardless of risk tolerance. This is a historically-confirmed pattern, not yet an actionable one.
    - **Not yet decided**: whether it's worth finding a fresher (likely paid) Senate disclosure feed to make this genuinely forward-tradeable. See "Suggested next steps."

85. **Found and fixed a real stale-data bug while investigating an inflated S&P 500 comparison number (2026-07-28)** — asked why the Paper Trader panel's SPY comparison suddenly showed the portfolio trailing by over $21,000; investigated rather than dismissed as a display quirk.
    - **Root cause**: one approved Research Agent candidate was screening `AFSI`, a ticker that was taken private in late 2018 and has no price data since. `fetch_and_store()` only marks a ticker "failed" when the fetch itself errors -- a delisted ticker's fetch just succeeds with zero new rows, no error -- so the existing "refusing to paper-trade on stale data" guards already written into `paper_trade.py`/`paper_trade_candidate.py` never actually fired. `check_and_update()` (`paper_trading.py`) blindly trusted `df.index[-1]` as "today's bar" regardless of age, and opened a real (fake-money) long position dated 2018-12-10 during a live check run in 2026 -- a single ~7-year-stale combo mixed into the roster's SPY comparison (which measures each combo since its own first paper-trading date) dragged the whole aggregate to look like it spanned back to 2018.
    - **Fixed at the root**, same "fix the mechanism, not the instance" approach as item 80's Research Agent fix: `check_and_update()` now refuses to act at all -- no signal generation, no entry/exit, nothing recorded -- if the data it's given is more than 7 calendar days old (`MAX_STALENESS_DAYS`), returning a new `STALE_DATA` action instead. This protects every caller (`paper_trade.py`'s 11 hand-picked combos, `paper_trade_candidate.py`'s LLM candidates), not just the one ticker that happened to expose it. Same underlying mistake as the SPY-staleness gap found and fixed earlier the same day (item 82): "the fetch didn't error" was silently mistaken for "the data is current" in both places.
    - **Verified against the exact failing case**, not just read the diff: re-ran the real candidate's exact strategy/params against `AFSI`'s real stored data and confirmed it now returns `STALE_DATA` (2,788 days stale) instead of opening a position. Deleted the one bad `paper_trade_events` row from production and confirmed the dashboard's SPY comparison returned to a sane number (+0.06%, matching how young the roster actually is) via a real HTTP call to the live server.

86. **Ran the pre-registered congressional-trading hypothesis list (H1-H6) -- two passed the first stage, both collapsed under the dependence correction, honest verdict is nothing survives (2026-07-28)** — the follow-through on item 84's "is there a general mechanism behind these per-senator edges?" question. Full write-up with tables in [[Congress Trading Research Plan]]; this is the build-log summary.
    - **Pre-registered first, deliberately**: the hypothesis list was written into the vault BEFORE any of it ran, precisely so the FDR correction could use n=7 (the full list, including H7's unimplemented committee-overlap slot) rather than n=however-many-looked-promising. The alternative framing the user originally asked about -- "go through every trade and find patterns among the winners" -- is the same look-elsewhere trap items 75/79/84 exist to catch, just aimed at a new dataset.
    - **New `congress_hypotheses.py` + `scripts/congress_hypotheses_run.py`**: per-trade forward returns (126 trading days, entered 1 day after *disclosure* -- never transaction date, same lookahead rule `congress_copy.py` already enforces), measured as **excess over SPY across each trade's identical calendar window**. Raw returns would be dominated by the shared market factor and make almost any group look significant against any other during a bull market. Every design constant (holding period, lag, clustering window) is fixed up front and documented as NOT searched -- searching them and reporting the best would reintroduce exactly the bias DSR/FDR correct for.
    - **Harness validated against synthetic cases before touching real data**, same discipline as item 75's FDR build: a known +5% signal is detected (p=6e-15), pure noise is not (p=0.19), a wrong-direction result correctly fails the one-sided test (p=1.00), and the FDR denominator was confirmed to count H7's reserved `None` slot toward N while never letting it become a discovery.
    - **Stage 1 (pooled, 4,592 usable trades of 5,951 raw)**: H1 (purchases beat sales, +1.68%, p=0.0069) and H6 (repeat buys beat first buys, +1.78%, p=0.0240) passed. H2/H3/H4 no evidence. **H5 came out backwards** -- tickers bought by 2+ members within 30 days *underperformed* single-member buys by 1.9%; not significant so not a reverse finding either, but the "corroboration" intuition had zero support.
    - **Stage 2, the decisive test -- and it reverses both**: the pooled test treats thousands of overlapping 126-day windows as independent observations, inflating effective sample size and therefore significance. `KNOWN_LIMITATIONS` item 1 flagged this *in advance*. New `clustered_test()` gives each **member** one number (their own signal-minus-control difference) and tests across members. **H1: p=0.0069 -> 0.5384. H6: p=0.0240 -> 0.5318.** Both mean per-member differences are *negative* (-0.11%). Built as a permanent, reproducible part of the module and wired into the runner -- not a throwaway shell one-liner -- since it's the test that actually decides the question. `clustered_checks_for_survivors()` deliberately raises `NotImplementedError` if a hypothesis without a designed clustered form ever passes Stage 1, rather than auto-generating a dubious one.
    - **A confound checked and ruled out, honestly**: H6 was re-run excluding the single heaviest trader (Perdue, 568 of 1,351 repeat buys) on the suspicion it was one member's artifact -- it got *stronger* pooled (+2.24%, p=0.0151), so H6 was never a Perdue effect. It still collapses under clustering. The obvious confound wasn't the explanation; the dependence was.
    - **Data-integrity guard shipped alongside** (flagged in item 84's follow-ups): `data/congress_trades.load()` gained `drop_impossible_lag=True`, excluding the 7 rows (0.12%) where `disclosure_date` precedes `transaction_date` -- physically impossible, and a real lookahead risk on the exact field the no-lookahead guarantee rests on. Defaults to True rather than following this project's usual "new gate defaults off" convention, because it's provably corrupt data rather than a judgment call; item 84's result re-verified as unaffected at 2 relevant rows out of thousands. Verified the guard drops exactly 7 rows (6,024 -> 6,017).
    - **What it means, stated plainly**: no evidence any of these six features distinguishes an informed congressional trade from a routine one at this sample size. H1 keeps a *hint* (median per-member diff +1.04%, 11/18 members positive, and it's the best-established claim in the literature) -- unproven, not disproven. And even at Stage 1 face value the absolute edge was **+0.83% excess over six months before costs**, not a tradeable margin. **This weakens the case for the Quiver subscription**: item 84 found per-senator edges, this finds no general mechanism behind them, and while those are technically compatible, the simpler joint explanation is that congressional trade-copying has no robust exploitable edge in this data.
    - **The pre-registration demonstrably earned its keep**: without a fixed list, the natural path would have been to report H1/H6 as discoveries, quietly drop H2-H5, and never run Stage 2 at all -- a conclusion that would have been wrong and would have justified spending money.

87. **Ran H7 (committee-sector overlap), the marquee congressional hypothesis -- it fails, and fails in reverse. All 7 pre-registered hypotheses are now closed out as negative (2026-07-28)** — the last untested item from [[Congress Trading Research Plan]], and the only one with a direct causal story for genuinely non-public information (committee members see briefings about the industries they regulate before the public does).
    - **Three data problems solved honestly rather than papered over**, in `congress_committee.py`. (1) **Era-correct assignments**: the free `unitedstates/congress-legislators` dataset carries committee membership *current only* -- using 2026 rosters against 2014-2021 trades would be serious measurement error (assignments change every Congress, party control flipped twice in the window) AND would silently drop every member who has since left office, which is emphatically not missing-at-random since that set includes the heaviest trader in the data (Perdue, 408 in-era purchases) and item 84's own survivors. Solved with **Stewart & Woon `senate_assignments_103-115`** (MIT, free, no key) for real per-Congress assignments including departed members. (2) **Coverage bound accepted, not fudged**: that dataset ends at the 115th Congress (Jan 2019), so this tests the 1,421 purchases disclosed through 2018 (61% of the panel) and deliberately does NOT extend to the 902 later ones by substituting the wrong era's rosters -- a smaller honest test beats a larger contaminated one. (3) **The committee->sector crosswalk is a researcher degree of freedom**, so it was fixed from statutory jurisdiction and written into the module *before* the test ran. **Appropriations and Budget deliberately map to no sector at all** despite being the most powerful committees present: their jurisdiction is universal, so mapping them would make nearly every trade "overlapping" and collapse the signal/control split into noise -- a choice that *costs* the test power rather than manufacturing it.
    - **Member matching verified by hand before trusting any number**, since a silent wrong match would corrupt the result invisibly: 19 of 20 members matched, covering 1,419 of 1,421 purchases, and the matcher correctly resolved the genuinely hard legal-name/nickname cases (`John F Reed` -> "Reed, Jack", `Rafael E Cruz` -> "Cruz, Ted", `A. Mitchell McConnell, Jr.` -> "McConnell, Mitch", `William Cassidy` -> "Cassidy, Bill"). Surname AND first initial are both required, because two Udalls and two Nelsons served in this era. New cached `ticker_sectors` table so the 364-ticker yfinance sector lookup costs nothing on rerun.
    - **Result: it goes the wrong way.** Of 1,324 testable purchases, in-jurisdiction trades returned **+0.90%** excess vs **+2.92%** for out-of-jurisdiction -- a **-2.02%** difference (pooled p=0.9766 on the pre-registered one-sided test). Member-clustered: mean per-member diff -0.49%, 6/13 members positive, p=0.5868. **The reverse is explicitly NOT claimed as a finding** -- the direction was pre-registered as "greater," and flipping it post hoc is exactly what pre-registration forbids; for completeness the reverse fails too (clustered p~0.41, 7/13 negative is a coin flip). Clean null, not a hidden inverse edge.
    - **Final tally across the whole pre-registered list: 0 for 7.** H1/H6 passed pooled and collapsed under clustering (item 86); H2/H3/H4 no evidence; H5 and H7 no evidence and both came out backwards. **No general mechanism distinguishes an informed congressional trade from a routine one in this data.**
    - **This closes the thread, and it changes the money decision.** Item 84's per-senator edges now read considerably more like survivorship among 17 candidates than a repeatable mechanism -- there is nothing underneath them. **Recommendation: do not buy the Quiver $30/mo subscription on the strength of the congress-copy idea.** Fresher data would only buy a faster feed into a strategy with no demonstrated basis. Total cost of reaching this conclusion: $0.

88. **Ran the same pre-registered-hypothesis discipline on SEC Form 4 corporate insider trades -- two survive everything, the first real (not noise) result of the whole session (2026-07-28)** — a genuinely different population from Congress: a corporate insider knows one specific company's real numbers before the public does, a narrower and more direct information channel than a committee's sector-wide oversight, and (unlike Congress's archive) `sec_insider_trades` is current through 2026-06 -- no item-84-style staleness problem. Full write-up in [[Insider Trading Research Plan]]; this is the build-log summary.
    - **Pre-registered first, same discipline**: 6 hypotheses (I1-I6) written into the vault before anything ran, mirroring H1-H6's structure (purchases vs sales, seniority, trade size, disclosure lag, multi-person clustering, repeat accumulation) but adapted to a much larger population -- 12,444 distinct insiders and 4,317 tickers vs. Congress's 18 testable people, so the person-clustered test (the one that decided the congress question) has real statistical power here instead of n=13-18. Per the pre-registration, the clustered test was made PRIMARY from the start (run for every hypothesis with a defined form, not gated behind the pooled stage passing) -- a deliberate upgrade made because the congress work already proved the pooled stage alone can't be trusted.
    - **New `insider_hypotheses.py` + `scripts/insider_hypotheses_run.py`**: same per-trade excess-over-SPY construction as `congress_hypotheses.py` (126-day hold, 1-day post-disclosure entry, every design constant fixed before running).
    - **A real regex bug caught in verification, not production**: the first `is_c_suite()` title-matcher wrapped its whole alternation in `\b(...)|...\b`, which silently fails whenever an alternative ends in punctuation (no word-boundary exists between two non-word characters). Confirmed by hand against real titles before trusting it: that version scored "Chairman & CEO", "EVP and CFO", "EVP & CFO", and "President, CEO, & Chair" all False despite each plainly being a CEO/CFO. Rewritten as token-based matching (split on non-letters, check for exact ceo/cfo/coo tokens or the full phrase) and re-verified against the same cases plus a false-positive sweep (Chief Medical/Investment/Technology/Legal/Accounting/Strategy Officer, bare VP/SVP/EVP/President/Chairman/Director all correctly stay False).
    - **A real performance bug caught before it cost real time, not after**: the first clustering implementation was an O(k^2) nested scan per ticker group; one ticker (RCG) has 4,387 purchases -- almost certainly one filer's routine/automatic transactions, not discretionary trades -- which would have meant ~19 million row-comparisons for that ticker alone. Rewritten using `searchsorted` on sorted dates (O(k log k)) and verified byte-identical output against the original on a 500-row synthetic dataset before trusting it on the real 63,405-purchase panel.
    - **Result, run on 241,350 usable trades of 321,183 raw**: I1 (purchases beat sales) and I6 (repeat accumulation) survive pooled, person-clustered, AND a heaviest-trader-excluded robustness check (top 100 most active (owner,ticker) pairs removed). I1: +1.42% purchases vs -1.45% sales, clustered p=0.0002 on 326 real people, holds after exclusion (p<0.0001). I6: +2.06% repeat buys vs -0.90% first buys, clustered p=0.0043 on 89 people, holds after exclusion (p=0.0011). I3/I5 passed pooled and collapsed clustered, same pattern as congress H1/H6. I4 passed pooled but has no clustered form to confirm it (disclosure lag here is too compressed -- median 1 day -- for a clean per-person split) and is reported as unconfirmed, not a finding.
    - **I2 (C-suite) initially looked like a third survivor and was caught and downgraded on closer inspection, not just accepted at face value**: clustered p=0.0046 looked like a pass, but investigating *why* revealed the clustered test for this hypothesis only has 26 qualifying people, because C-suite status is mostly a fixed trait of a person rather than something that varies trade-to-trade -- only 146 of 12,444 insiders ever have titles coded both ways (mostly mid-dataset promotions or inconsistent filing strings), so the test wasn't measuring "C-suite people vs. others" at all, just "the same person's trades under one label vs. another" on a tiny slice. The heaviest-trader-excluded check confirmed the suspicion directly: excluding the 100 heaviest pairs collapsed it from +20.52% (p=0.0046) to +0.22% (p=0.40). Reported as not confirmed -- the properly-powered pooled test (13,486 vs 32,631) already agreed, at p=0.27.
    - **Honest caveats before this means anything practical**: the surviving edges are modest (+1.4-2.1% excess over six months, before any trading costs) and these are per-trade split tests establishing *whether a mechanism exists*, not a portfolio backtest -- the next step, if pursued, is the same treatment congress-copy got in item 84 (`build_purchase_hold_portfolio()`, already built and reused from `congress_copy.py`, run at real scale through the platform's own DSR/FDR/risk-gate pipeline) before this is anywhere close to tradeable. **Done in item 89 -- does not survive.**

89. **Portfolio-level DSR/FDR test of I1/I6 (the two insider-trading survivors) -- same treatment congress-copy got in item 84, and it does NOT survive (2026-07-29)** — full write-up in [[Insider Trading Research Plan]]'s new "PORTFOLIO-LEVEL RESULT" section; this is the build-log summary.
    - **Found and fixed a real capital-starvation bug in pre-existing (2026-07-24) scripts before trusting their results**: `insider_purchase_hold_backtest.py`'s "ALL [purchase-hold 126d]" run (run_id `fd96d72c`) reported a disappointing 15.7% return vs SPY's 66.6% -- but its `info` counts show why that number means nothing: 54,786 candidate purchases matched, but only 136 ever got capital (54,650 skipped for insufficient cash). At a fixed 5%-of-portfolio position size, a $10,000 account can only ever hold ~20 concurrent slots no matter how the math is framed, and 46k+ purchases arriving over 3 years vastly outpaces how fast a 126-day hold can turn 20 slots over -- so that "backtest" was really just whichever ~136 trades happened to disclose first, not a test of the signal. This is a different kind of bug than the ones caught so far this session (not a code-correctness bug -- the code ran fine -- a backtest-design bug that silently produces a number that looks like a real result).
    - **Fix applied the same way congress_copy_promotion.py already handles this class of problem**: new `insider_portfolio_promotion.py`, position_fraction is part of a small, fixed, pre-declared search grid (lag_days ∈ {1,2} × position_fraction ∈ {0.01, 0.02, 0.05}, 6 trials, same size as congress-copy's own grid) rather than one hand-picked value -- DSR-corrects within that search per hypothesis, then FDR-corrects across the 2 hypotheses tested (I1, I6). New `insider_copy_evaluations` table in `store/insider_copy.py`, mirroring `congress_copy_evaluations` exactly.
    - **Result**: even at the smallest tested position size, capital starvation is still severe (I1's best trial still skipped 54,160 of 54,786 candidates; I6 skipped 42,378 of 42,683) -- there are simply too many disclosed purchases per day for any account with realistic per-trade sizing to fund more than a couple percent of them. Both hypotheses **HOLD, not PROMOTE**: I1 (all purchases) returned +60.1% vs SPY's +70.9% (Sharpe 0.88 vs 1.48, max drawdown -25.5%, DSR p=0.151); I6 (repeat accumulation) returned +39.3% vs SPY's +70.9% (Sharpe 0.61 vs 1.48, max drawdown -29.1%, DSR p=0.259). Both underperform the benchmark, both breach the -25% risk limit, neither is significant after FDR correction.
    - **Why this doesn't necessarily mean I1/I6 are fake**: the per-trade edge from item 88 held up under real scrutiny (clustering, exclusion checks). What failed here is a specific, honest attempt at *implementation* -- "buy literally every qualifying disclosed purchase" -- which is structurally impossible to fund at real position sizes given the trade volume, so the resulting portfolio ends up holding an essentially random, first-come slice of the population rather than a deliberately curated one. A tradeable version would need a real selection layer (which purchases to actually act on, out of far more than any account can fund) designed and pre-registered before testing -- not fit to this negative result after the fact. That's a new, separate research question, not something to build today on the strength of "it seems like it would work."
    - **Practical bottom line for now**: neither congress-copying (item 87, 0/7) nor insider-copying (this item, 0/2 at portfolio level) has produced anything to actually paper-trade. The insider per-trade finding (item 88) remains the most statistically credible thing found this session, but "statistically credible" and "have a portfolio construction that captures it" turned out to be two different bars, and only the first one has been cleared so far.

90. **Did item 73's pending re-run for real, and it wasn't a formality -- 345 of 1,268 currently-PROMOTE combos demoted, including 5 of the ~11 live paper-traded ones, with real numbers behind it (2026-07-29)** — asked what to do next after both copy-trading threads (items 84-89) came up empty; recommended returning to the platform's own proven price-technical pipeline over chasing a third exotic data source. Two pieces of already-flagged, never-finished work turned out to be sitting there: item 73's "re-run pending" note, and item 49's "875-ticker backlog, needs a review method" note. Neither was a formality.
    - **New `scripts/refresh_promoted_combos.py`**: re-ran real backtest + walk-forward for exactly the 1,268 (ticker, strategy) pairs that currently hold the freshest PROMOTE verdict (latest snapshot per combo across all past cycles, not one fixed cycle_id) — narrower and far cheaper than a full universe re-run (which would re-test tens of thousands of never-promoted combos for no new information), reusing `research_cycle.py`'s own `_run_one_combo()` rather than duplicating it. Took ~12 minutes at 4 workers.
    - **Result: 923 of 1,268 still PROMOTE, 345 demoted to HOLD** — a real, substantial shift, not noise. Most demotions are the correlation gate re-sorting winners now that adjusted prices changed relative DSR rankings (e.g. "correlated 0.94 with X (already selected, higher DSR)"), but a meaningful minority are genuine walk-forward degradation (`all_oos_windows_positive` / `sufficient_oos_evidence` failing outright) — item 73's own default-param proxy check ("zero Sharpe sign flips") measured the wrong thing; the real gate is each combo's own grid-searched params across its actual out-of-sample walk-forward windows, and that moved a lot more than the proxy suggested.
    - **5 of the currently-paper-traded combos were among the demoted — checked each one's actual before/after numbers, not just the pass/fail flag, before reporting this**: `TIGO/sma_ls` (worst-OOS Sharpe +1.10 → **-0.67**, a real sign flip), `COLM/bollinger_ls` (+0.40 → **-0.13**, sign flip), `XLP/bollinger` (+0.06 → **-0.43** — this one was already razor-thin before), `XLRE/rsi` (own worst-OOS Sharpe +1.98 → +0.10, now also outscored by a correlated `XLRE/bollinger_regime_aggressive`), and `TIP/bollinger` (Sharpe barely moved, 0.749→0.747, but 2 of its 4 walk-forward windows dropped from 2 trades each to **zero trades** under adjusted prices — direct evidence the bollinger mean-reversion signal was partly triggering on the artificial price gaps around TIP's dividend dates that unadjusted `close` used to create, exactly the distortion item 73 exists to remove). Not touched: `QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `QQQ/breakout_ls`, `FXI/bollinger_regime`, `XLRE/rsi` [now demoted], `BLCO/bollinger_ls`, `DHC/volume_breakout_ls` all still hold up.
    - **A real gap in this refresh, stated plainly**: 26 of the 1,268 combos couldn't be re-tested — `_run_one_combo()` looks strategy classes up in the static `STRATEGIES` registry, which doesn't include `_aggressive`-suffixed entries (a separate naming convention from `research_cycle_aggressive.py`) or research-agent candidate classes (`cand_*`, dynamically defined). Those 26 got re-snapshotted this cycle using their existing *stale* (pre-adjustment) backtest data — their current PROMOTE/HOLD status is not actually re-validated. None of the 5 demoted paper-traded combos are in this affected set, so the finding above is solid, but this gap should be closed before fully trusting the other 26.
    - **New `scripts/promoted_backlog_triage.py`** closes out item 49's "875-ticker backlog, how do we even review this" question, using the same three-filter method `universe_promote_triage.py` already established (item 168/72: DSR ≥ 0.75 not the standard 0.5, beats-own-buy-and-hold as a hard gate, beats-SPY as a hard gate, $5M/day liquidity floor) but pointed at whatever currently holds the freshest PROMOTE verdict rather than one pinned historical cycle, and excluding tickers already individually reviewed (the 30-ticker curated universe + everything in `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED`) rather than re-litigating decided ones. Also surfaces item 75's FDR flag on every survivor as context (not a gate, same precedent).
    - **Result: 769 never-reviewed tickers × their full strategy grid = 12,996 combos → 28 clear the strict statistical bar → exactly 1 also clears the liquidity floor: `CRF/turn_of_month`** (DSR 0.99, worst-OOS Sharpe 0.37, $8.7M/day volume, not FDR-significant). This is the *same* candidate item 72 already found and deliberately deferred (a closed-end fund's NAV/distribution dynamics are a different mechanism than the ETF/index-flow effect `turn_of_month` was validated on) — a second, fully independent triage pass on fresher, corrected data landing on the same single survivor is a real consistency signal, not a new finding, and item 72's unresolved mechanism-mismatch concern still applies. **Net result: the 875-ticker backlog is not sitting on undiscovered opportunity — it was mostly noise, exactly as items 49/75's multiple-testing caveat predicted, and the one candidate worth a second look was already known.**
    - **Update 2026-07-29, same day**: user reviewed the 5 demoted combos' actual before/after stats (per-window walk-forward numbers, not just the pass/fail flag) plus a same-window plain-SPY-buy-and-hold comparison for each — all 5 barely matched or underperformed just holding SPY even before the dividend-price fix entered the picture (e.g. TIGO +229.1% vs SPY +216.1%, XLP +79.9% vs SPY +329.9%). Decided to pull all 5. Removed `("XLP","bollinger")`, `("TIP","bollinger")`, `("XLRE","rsi")`, `("TIGO","sma_ls")`, `("COLM","bollinger_ls")` from both `CURRENTLY_PAPER_TRADED` (`research_cycle.py`) and their corresponding blocks in `daily_paper_trade.sh`, with the original add-history preserved in comments rather than deleted outright. Live paper-trading roster was 6 combos as of this update: `QQQ/sma`, `QQQ/breakout`, `QQQ/breakout_ls` (still a deliberate non-PROMOTE exception, see item 38), `FXI/bollinger_regime`, `BLCO/bollinger_ls`, `DHC/volume_breakout_ls` — all 6 confirmed unaffected by this refresh. `agent_ecosystem.py`'s dashboard parses `daily_paper_trade.sh` live on every request (not cached at process start), so it reflects this automatically, no service restart needed. All 5 removed combos' `paper_trade_events` history stays untouched (append-only), just no longer checked daily. **`BLCO/bollinger_ls` and `DHC/volume_breakout_ls` were themselves later removed 2026-08-03 — see item 94. Roster is now 4 combos.**

91. **Asked whether an hourly paper-trading check would help, then whether Kusha's stated ~30% win rate / ~5:1 reward:risk fits the encoded strategy, then built the real 3-stage exit to test that claim properly (2026-07-30)** — three related asks in one thread, each answered honestly rather than just implemented.
    - **Hourly-check request**: explained that the platform's live combos only use daily bars, so checking hourly would just re-read the same unchanged number 23 extra times a day -- a real hourly job already exists (`com.quantplatform.papertrade_intraday`, `XRP-USD/kusha_breakout`, item 67/69) precisely because THAT strategy runs on real hourly bars; the 6 daily combos weren't added to it, on purpose. User agreed to leave it as-is once shown the existing infrastructure.
    - **Win-rate/reward:risk check**: ran the existing vectorized `kusha_breakout` across 5 liquid crypto tickers (full hourly history). Win rate landed almost exactly on his stated ~30% (27.4-34.9% across BTC/ETH/XRP/SOL/DOGE) -- expected, since the entry rule stacks several independent conditions (tested resistance, rising lows, volume dry-up-then-confirm, uptrend) that most setups fail at least one of. Reward:risk came in lower than his stated ~5:1, more like 1.4-3.0:1 -- flagged as likely understated because the current strategy approximates his real 3-stage exit (hard stop -> breakeven -> trail by 5 EMA) with one single rule ("exit below 5 EMA") active from the moment of entry, per `kusha_breakout.py`'s own module docstring.
    - **Built `src/quant_platform/kusha_stateful.py`** to test that theory directly: a bar-by-bar simulator (not vectorbt -- same choice already made for congress_copy.py/insider_portfolio_promotion.py's event-driven work, and for the same reason: this needs genuine per-trade memory, which a flat vectorized entries/exits Series can't express) implementing his real rule -- hard stop at the breakout candle's own low, switching to breakeven-and-trail-by-5-EMA the instant the 5 EMA first reaches the entry price. Reuses `KushaBreakoutStrategy`'s entry logic and constants unchanged; only the exit side differs. One explicit interpretive call, stated plainly rather than buried: reading "breakeven once an EMA catches up -> trail by the 5 EMA" as ONE transition, not two separately-timed stages, since the instant the 5 EMA first reaches entry, trailing by that same EMA already sits at/near breakeven.
    - **Result, same 5 tickers**: win rate rose further (32.1-41.4%, even closer to his figure), and total return/Sharpe/drawdown all improved meaningfully across the board (XRP +49%->+72%, DOGE +19%->+67%, BTC -22.7%->-6.4%, SOL -19.9%->+2.0%) -- the real stop clearly is a better system than the approximation. **But reward:risk still didn't reach his stated 5:1** -- landed at 1.86-2.90:1, roughly the same range as the approximation, just achieved with a higher win rate instead of fatter wins. Honest read, not spun: either (a) the interpretive call above collapses two stages Kusha actually times differently (e.g. a real fixed-breakeven hold before trailing starts, which would let winners run longer before the trail can bite), (b) his stated "real-time discretion" (explicitly part of his framework, not a fixed rule) lets him hold strong trades longer or cut weak ones faster than any single mechanical rule would, or (c) his edge is concentrated in the specific illiquid micro-cap tickers this platform's data source doesn't carry (already flagged in `kusha_breakout.py`'s own docstring as the piece that can't be tested here) -- his stated ratio may simply reflect a different, more favorable trade population than 5 liquid majors. Not yet decided which; not promoted to live paper trading, this was a research check, not a promotion.
    - **Tested hypothesis (a) directly**: added `trail_start_r_multiple` to `kusha_stateful.py` -- once breakeven, HOLD the stop there (not yet trailing) until the trade is up that many multiples of its own initial risk ("1R" = entry-to-hard-stop distance), only then start trailing by the 5 EMA. Ran 0/1/1.5/2/2.5/3 across the same 5 tickers, averaged: **a real, smooth tradeoff curve, not a free lunch** -- win rate and reward:risk move in opposite directions as the hold gets longer (0R: 35.0% win / 2.40:1 -> 1R: 31.6% / 2.93:1 -> 1.5R: 28.3% / 3.31:1 -> 2R: 25.0% / 3.87:1 -> 2.5R: 22.5% / 4.74:1 -> 3R: 20.8% / 5.48:1). No point on the curve hits BOTH of his stated numbers (~30% win, ~5:1) at once -- confirmed smooth, not a gap between the original 4 samples: 1R gets closest to his win rate, 2.5-3R gets closest to (and eventually passes) his reward:risk, nothing lands on both simultaneously anywhere along the curve. Read plainly: his "give or take" numbers most likely describe a *range* across many trades with genuinely different holding behavior (discretion), not one fixed rule -- consistent with hypothesis (b) above, not disproving it. Did not pick a "best" multiple and call it done -- reported the full curve rather than the one value that happened to look most flattering, same rigor as the DSR/FDR work earlier this session. Nothing here promoted to live paper trading.
    - **Thread closed, final verdict**: Kusha's stated ~30% win rate / ~5:1 reward:risk describes a real, internally consistent trading style (confirmed structurally -- tight initial risk, patient winners), but no single fixed exit rule tested here reproduces both numbers together on this platform's data. The honest conclusion is that his edge is a *combination* this platform can only partially copy: the mechanical entry criteria transfer directly (win rate matched closely across every version tried), but the exit is genuinely discretionary, not a formula -- and his own stated reliance on illiquid micro-cap ticker selection (not available in this platform's data) is very plausibly where the rest of the 5:1 actually comes from. `kusha_stateful.py` and `trail_start_r_multiple` stay in the codebase as a reusable tool if a different fixed rule is ever worth trying, not as a dead end to repeat blindly.

92. **Widened kusha_breakout from 30 hand-picked crypto tickers to every one Yahoo Finance actually has -- "thousands and thousands" isn't real, but a genuinely bigger honest universe was, and it reproduces the same multiple-testing story as the stock universe (2026-07-30)** — asked to test the strategy across as many coins as possible. Confirmed directly first (not assumed): probing CoinGecko's top 500 coins by market cap against Yahoo found only 305 with real, usable data -- "thousands" was never reachable on this platform's one free data source, said plainly before spending any real compute chasing it.
    - **Real bug found and fixed before the real run**: the first attempt at `top_n=2000` crashed at page 6/8 -- CoinGecko's free/keyless tier rate-limits (429) with no retry logic existing for it. Added exponential backoff on 429 specifically (not other errors) plus a small proactive delay between pages to `data/universe.py`'s `fetch_crypto_universe()`. Root cause fixed, not routed around by shrinking the request.
    - **Real ceiling, confirmed**: top 2000 by market cap -> 906 tickers with real Yahoo data (a widening from 305/500, but the *rate* still dropped as expected -- 45% survival at 2000 deep vs 61% at 500 deep). 901 of 906 fetched successfully; 874 cleared a new volatility floor (std of hourly returns >= 0.15%) added specifically because CoinGecko's market-cap ranking is full of USD-pegged/wrapped tokens (RLUSD, USDG, GHO, EURC, and dozens more) the existing hardcoded stablecoin list doesn't catch by name -- computed from real fetched data instead of guessing at more name patterns.
    - **Kusha's own named example tickers, resolved**: `CASHCAT-USD`, `XPIN-USD`, and `ZAMA-USD` ARE reachable on Yahoo Finance -- `kusha_breakout.py`'s own module docstring assumed his specific picks weren't available at all; that assumption was wrong for 3 of his 4 named tickers (`UAI-USD` is still genuinely unavailable). Tested them directly: **none pass.** XPIN and ZAMA both have a clearly, badly negative worst-case walk-forward window (-6.0 to -7.6 Sharpe) -- a real fail, not a close miss. CASHCAT only produced 2-9 total trades across its whole history, too thin to mean anything either way. Being reachable in the data didn't turn out to mean being a hidden edge.
    - **A second real bug found, not yet fixed**: `kusha_breakout_ls` (the long/short variant) crashed with vectorbt's `"SizeType.Percent does not support position reversal using signals"` on 48 of 874 tickers (5.5%) -- `backtest/run.py`'s existing same-bar-reversal handling (see its own docstring) apparently doesn't catch every conflict pattern the wider universe produces; the original hand-picked 30-ticker set never happened to trigger it. Those 48 tickers' long/short results are genuinely missing, not tested-and-failed -- flagged honestly rather than silently absent from the results table. Left for a deliberate future fix, not patched live without understanding the actual gap in the conflict-resolution logic first.
    - **Result: 1,700 (ticker, strategy) combos completed (874 tickers x 2 strategies, minus the 48 crashes), 8 raw PROMOTE on the platform's standard five-gate bar** -- `WALLET-USD`, `VIRTUAL-USD`, `WKC-USD`, `LOAN-USD`, `DIAM-USD/kusha_breakout_ls`, `XRP-USD` (the one already live paper-traded -- a good sanity check that the sweep reproduces known results), `TLOS-USD`, `TSLAX-USD`.
    - **Ran the exact same FDR correction item 75 already built for the stock universe, not just noted the caveat qualitatively**: at n=1,610 real trials (every combo with a computable DSR), q=0.10 -- **zero of the 8 survive.** This includes `XRP-USD`. Read plainly, not alarmingly: this doesn't newly indict `XRP-USD/kusha_breakout` -- item 67/69 already called it "closer to what chance alone would produce than a confirmed edge" the day it was found, and this is the same honest caveat re-confirmed at 28x the search scale, not new bad news. But it does mean the other 7 raw passes here (`WALLET-USD` etc.) sit at exactly the same "statistically indistinguishable from a lucky search result" level -- leads to maybe look at individually someday, not candidates for paper trading on the strength of this sweep alone.
    - **Overall honest verdict**: widening the universe did not surface a hidden real edge -- it reproduced, at crypto scale, the identical multiple-testing pattern the stock full-universe sweep already taught (item 49/75). Full 1,700-row result table saved to `logs/kusha_universe_sweep_results.csv`. Nothing here promoted to live paper trading.
    - **Individually reviewed the top 2 of the 8 raw passes by hand (2026-07-30), same "don't trust the summary number" discipline as item 72** -- caught a real illiquid-instrument false positive, same bug class as DYNR/AD earlier in the project, just crypto-flavored:
      - **`WALLET-USD` (Ambire Wallet) -- REJECT.** Its +132.4%/92-trade result is not real: average volume is **~$1,541/day** -- nowhere near tradeable at any meaningful size, you'd move the price yourself placing the trade. Also 59% of its total profit comes from just 3 trades. The volatility floor built for this sweep only checks price *movement*, not actual *liquidity* -- confirmed directly here that the two are different things, and a thin order book can produce large, noisy price swings that look like a real signal on a chart without being anything you could actually execute. **A crypto liquidity floor (real $ volume, not just price-movement volatility) needs adding to `kusha_universe_sweep.py` before it's trusted again** -- not done yet, flagged for the next run.
      - **`VIRTUAL-USD` (Virtuals Protocol, a real, well-known AI-agent token platform) -- holds up much better.** Real liquidity (~$65M/day). All 4 walk-forward windows individually positive (Sharpe +0.48 to +1.79 per window), though each window's trade count is thin (2-12). Its own 68.8%-of-profit-from-top-3-trades concentration initially looked like the same red flag as `WALLET-USD` -- checked against `XRP-USD` (the currently-live combo) for comparison and found it shows the *same* concentration (68.6%) -- this is just what a low-win-rate, big-winner-driven breakout strategy looks like structurally, not a sign of a fluky result specific to this ticker. Essentially zero correlation with `XRP-USD` (0.019) -- would be a genuinely diversifying addition, not a redundant one, if it were ever added. Still carries the same caveat as everything else in this sweep though: does not survive the FDR correction above. The most credible of the 8, but "most credible of a batch that didn't survive real correction" is not the same as "confirmed" -- not added to paper trading.

93. **Built crypto funding-rate arbitrage end-to-end — a genuinely new, non-directional strategy family, not another price-trend variant — backtested, paper-traded, and running live on a schedule (2026-07-30 → 2026-07-31)** — asked for "out of the box" ideas beyond the existing price-trend combos; full research trail (mechanics, threshold decision, margin sizing, venue checks) lives in [[Enhancement Ideas]]'s funding-arb entries, this is the build-log summary of what actually shipped.
    - **The trade**: perpetual futures pay/charge a periodic funding rate to keep their price tethered to spot. Going long spot + short perp in equal notional is market-neutral (price moves cancel) and collects that funding rate as long as it stays net positive on average — the profit source is structural (an exchange mechanism), not a market-direction bet, which is exactly the kind of diversification the platform's all-directional combo roster was missing.
    - **Didn't trust a short window**: a first look at recent-only funding data looked marginal; pulled real multi-year history before deciding anything (Binance's public archive for the long backtest, Bitnomial for the live/paper-trading leg) — same discipline as the short-interest and Congress/Insider work above. Full history changed the conclusion from "maybe not worth it" to a real, persistent edge.
    - **Design decisions made empirically, not by default**: tested always-on carry vs. a funding-rate threshold (only enter when the rate clears some minimum) — always-on won, the threshold version gave up more upside from skipped periods than it saved in avoided negative-funding stretches. Margin/leverage sizing scoped explicitly against real maintenance-margin mechanics rather than picked arbitrarily (BTC 2x, ETH 1.5x — asymmetric because ETH's funding history is more volatile). Checked cross-margin availability for US retail before assuming it — not available, isolated margin only, which is what the sizing above already assumes.
    - **A real bug found and fixed before trusting the backtest**: the first version of `simulate()` pinned position margin to one entry-time price for the entire 6.5-year run, which produced false liquidation events that contradicted the threshold-design finding above.
